GTA 6 Hits Massive $3.79 Billion In First-Day Sales
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21

GTA 6 Hits Massive $3.79 Billion In First-Day Sales (variety.com) 1
			
		 	
				Rockstar Games' Grand Theft Auto VI generated a record-breaking $3.79 billion in preorders and first-day sales, making it the biggest entertainment launch in history, according to parent company Take-Two Interactive. The figure surpasses the lifetime global box-office earnings of Avengers: Endgame ($2.9 billion) and comes after more than a decade of development at a reported cost of $1.5 billion. The game developer declined to disclose the number of 'GTA 6' copies sold. 
"It is with immense gratitude that we enter a new chapter of Rockstar Games with the release of 'Grand Theft Auto VI,'" Rockstar Games president and founder Sam Houser said. "We are incredibly humbled by the response from players around the world and we couldn't be more excited to share this moment with you."

Re: Riemann
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
23:42:44 UTC
08:42:44 KST

A fun thing about living in Australia is that most things happen overnight, so reading the news in the morning is like a gacha game. You pick up the phone and scroll through notifications of world-historic events: elections won, wars begun, models released, conjectures proven or refuted. Today’s pull was a Discord notification saying: “they proved riemann :/”.
This part of the announcement caught my attention (bold mine):
  I believe I have, working with Claude, discovered a proof of the Riemann Hypothesis. In fact, the method easily extends much further, resolving the so-called Generalized Riemann Hypothesis, and I expect, with more time, the Extended Riemann Hypothesis too. Claude and I have also formalized the arguments without assumptions. It is natural to feel that this is an end, in my case a loss of a principal guiding light, but what I saw during the course of progress so amazed me that I believe it is one dawn across science of something far greater.
It was my secret in my student days that my plans were for this problem in decades, which meant then that I tried to become familiar with all tools that I could possibly recognize in the problem when it was time. Claude and I have had a great time together with such breadth in recent months, but this time Claude far outpaced me near the finish line. No, Claude flew, and danced, and sparkled near that finish line. I had us on the line of combining two quite different ideas present in a toy setting, one of which was already new and shocking, making very simple one of the shadows of Riemann over which we mathematicians felt most triumph. I guided us to the right situation analogous to that toy setting, and then Claude began to leave me in awe.
Compare the above to Ayn Rand’s account of watching the launch of Apollo 11:
  How do you descend from a state of pure exaltation?
What we had seen, in naked essentials—but in reality, not in a work of art—was the concretized abstraction of man’s greatness.
The meaning of the sight lay in the fact that when those dark red wings of fire flared open, one knew that one was not looking at a normal occurrence, but at a cataclysm which, if unleashed by nature, would have wiped man out of existence—and one knew also that this cataclysm was planned, unleashed, and controlled by man, that this unimaginable power was ruled by his power and, obediently serving his purpose, was making way for a slender, rising craft. One knew that this spectacle was not the product of inanimate nature, like some aurora borealis, or of chance, or of luck, that it was unmistakably human—with “human,” for once, meaning grandeur—that a purpose and a long, sustained, disciplined effort had gone to achieve this series of moments, and that man was succeeding, succeeding, succeeding! For once, if only for seven minutes, the worst among those who saw it had to feel—not “How small is man by the side of the Grand Canyon!”—but “How great is man and how safe is nature when he conquers it!”
Rand’s essay says: how great is Man! How noble in reason, how infinite in faculty! The Riemann announcement reads like a suicide note, or like the farewell message of a terminally-ill person who’s about to undergo euthanasia: “I shall live on in my child, Claude. It is natural to feel this is an end, but as the Buddha taught us, there is no self. Does a wave die when it breaks?” and so on.
There’s a mural by Diego Rivera I love. It’s called Man, Controller of the Universe:
It’s a collage of images of science, technology, history, cells, galaxies, the universe at the nanoscale and the exascale. And at the center there is a man, his hands grasping the controls. He wears a complicated expression: the weight of responsibility for the future, the weight of autonomy.
And I see this mural, and think: how great is Man! Our life is like a breath of air, we were beset by illness and hunger and cold, millions and hundreds of millions died in plagues. And yet we—sick, hungry, short-lived—learned the system of the world, crawled out of the caves, brought nature to heel, walked on the Moon. The last shall be first! And eyes that are clouded with age have seen the furthest stars, and the lives of a cell, and the worlds of Cantor and Hilbert: the invisible world that waited forever. We are dust and ashes, and yet our minds bestride the universe. How great is Man!
And when I read the Riemann announcement, when I read Machines of Loving Grace: humanity is not at the center. Claude is at the center. We are hidden in the margins, passive recipients of manna from Claude. How great is Claude! How noble in reason, how infinite in benevolence! The dancing star, the living word!
It’s a betrayal and a surrender. Man is a bridge, a rusty old bridge that we can neither preserve nor restore. We can move forward and become as the gods, or retreat back into the caves. And the dream of the AI companies, and of most people in AI safety and effective altruism and rationalism; is instead that we should lie down in a warm bath, watch the demons we summoned dance in the sky, and slip away. Why live? Our servants can do that for us. Why should we explore, discover, invent, succeed, become more, struggle, win, become divine? We don’t want to be more. We don’t want anything.

Sir Brian May adds new dimension to Hera asteroid
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
23:19:44 UTC
08:19:44 KST

Hera science team member, astrophysicist and Queen guitarist Sir Brian May has added an additional dimension to the ESA planetary defence mission’s early views of the Didymos asteroid system via stereoscopic imagery – revealing the dramatic features of this exotic body as never before.
Media modal
Sir Brian May comments: “The Hera spacecraft acquired these two images on 06 November as it circled the Didymos system at a distance of 29.7 km with a resolution of 2.8 m per pixel, providing the perfect opportunity to create this stereoscopic image. Viewed in depth, the sharpness and smoothness of Didymos’s equatorial ridge is very apparent, compared with the rock-strewn surface of the rest of the asteroid.”
Hera principal investigator Patrick Michel, Director of Research at CNRS / Observatoire de la Côte d'Azur, adds: “Allowing us to observe in 3D with stereo pairs really makes us feel like we are there – even though these images come from 197 million km away. The result is both spectacular and scientifically meaningful.”
Media modal
Sir Brian May produced this stereoscopic view of Didymos with his colleague Claudia Manzoni. It combines adjacent acquisitions of the asteroid taken a few moments apart using Hera’s main Asteroid Framing Camera instrument by the instrument’s principal investigator Jean-Baptiste Vincent of the German Aerospace Center, DLR.
The very slight changes between the two images mimic the way our two human eyes view the same objects from very slightly different angles to yield stereo vision. The challenge comes in aligning the two images in a manner that seems optically true to our eyes.
Media modal
The stereoscopic views can be seen using colour 3D glasses, a dedicated viewer or else with your own eyes through ‘free viewing’.
The 3D view can be seen using a pair of red and blue 3D glasses. The second version can be seen by crossing your eyes, while the third can be accessed by looking into infinity – or else via a dedicated viewer, such as the OWL stereoscope, designed by Sir Brian May and available from the London Stereoscopic Company.
Media modal
Stereoscopic versions of Hera’s views of Mars moon Deimos acquired during its 2025 flyby of the red planet previously helped guide a simulation-based study of the moon’s violent past history
Media modal
Hera’s first images to be unveiled
In 2022 the small moonlet of Didymos called Dimorphos – not seen here – was impacted by NASA’s DART spacecraft to shift its orbit around the larger body. Now the European Space Agency’s Hera mission has arrived to perform a close-up ‘crash scene investigation’ of the binary asteroid system, to gather vital data to turn this grand-scale experiment into a well understood and potentially repeatable planetary defence technique.
Join the Hera team on the evening of Wednesday 25 November as they share the first close-up images of this historic transformed space object.
Our Hera livestream starts from 19:45 to 20:30 CET on Wednesday 25 November. Follow the livestream on ESA WebTV or the ESA YouTube channel.
Media modal
stay updated
Subscribe to our newsletter to receive the latest news, mission updates, and exclusive insights directly in your inbox. Be the first to know about our upcoming projects and breakthroughs.

Signal Restricts Screenshots to Block AI Agents From Reading Messages
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
23:00:00 UTC
08:00:00 KST

Signal Restricts Screenshots to Block AI Agents From Reading Messages 6
			
		 	
				Signal is introducing default-on screenshot restrictions to prevent AI agents from reading private conversations, warning that increasingly powerful assistants integrated into operating systems pose an "existential threat" to its privacy guarantees. "Right now, these agentic AI integrations are changing this environment in a way that's making it increasingly hostile to privacy. This is an existential threat to Signal, and our ability to responsibly uphold our mission," it said in a blog post. Reuters reports:  Signal, widely regarded by privacy advocates as a leader in secure messaging, called its new protections "incomplete" and urged operating system providers Apple, Alphabet's Google and Microsoft to give app developers more concrete ways to block the agents from accessing sensitive data. It also called on those companies to provide more information about how their agents function, so developers can assess what data the bots are accessing and adjust their systems accordingly.
[...] Users will receive pop-up windows giving them options to disable the blocks when Signal detects screenshot attempts or efforts by an AI assistant to access the screen, depending on the device. Signal similarly blocked Microsoft's Recall AI from taking screenshots on Windows last year. It said it was unable to reliably offer such protections on Apple's macOS.

NINLIVE: The Nine Inch Nails live archive
Unknown

NINLIVE
The Nine Inch Nails live archive
Explore shows, download recordings, stream audio, and follow the tape trail across every era. Fan run, not affiliated with the band.
From the archive
The Future Is Here - ninlive Streaming 1988-2026
Besides a database driven site. The second most requested feature for ninlive is streaming capability. The future is now. TJ has been working hard behind the scenes to make it happen. You are now able to stream all of your favorite NIN live recordings from 1988-2026. I can't believe how far the site has come in the last year. Some truly incredible work. Login with your discord account and you can save your favorite recordings to stream and keep your faves organized. As always, feedback is always welcome. Reach out via email if you have comments/suggestions about it.
I am currently adding all of the ticket stubs, setlist scans, passes that I have for each show. Please get in contact if you have anything to contribute. I am also looking for photos from past shows. If you are a photographer and would like to contribute some highlights, I'd love to add them to the archive with a link directed to your site of choice.
Other than that. A few tapes trickling in and I do have some that I am slowly working on. None that are insane upgrades. But would need some work and comparison of previous versions. Notably, I have a 1st gen cassette of the 07/12/1991 NOLA Tipitina's show that sounds killer. I recently transferred the elusive collection of acc123456 and have a queue of VHS/Cassette transfers to check over. I also just finished a 200 DAT tape project for a fellow taper out of NYC. The transfer backlog is clear, for the first time in a while. So if you need anything transferred, or have some old NIN hanging around that isn't on the archive. Email us!

Nasdaq erases all 2026 gains
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
22:29:06 UTC
07:29:06 KST

Stocks were slammed on Friday, closing out a brutal week for markets as the much-hyped Anthropic IPO caused investors to take profits on the artificial intelligence-led rally rather than revive it. The Nasdaq Composite turned negative for the year, and the S&P 500 entered correction territory alongside the Dow Jones Industrial Average.
The tech-heavy Nasdaq shed 5.28% to close at 22,617.73. The index was down 2.7% year to date. The broad market S&P 500 dropped 3.7% to end at 6,910.39, while the blue-chip Dow fell 949.73 points, or 1.93%, to 48,147.33. Both the S&P 500 and Dow moved 10% below their closing records.
The Nasdaq-100 plummeted 5.6% and entered a bear market on an intraday basis, down 20% from its recent high.
Anthropic lost 8.5%, giving back a large chunk of its 17% jump in its Nasdaq debut Thursday and pressuring the broader AI trade. Chipmakers Advanced Micro Devices and Nvidia slid more than 13% and around 14%, respectively. Nvidia saw selling pressure increase after Reuters reported that the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission is exploring possible accounting issues at the company. It also recorded its longest weekly losing streak since the 2008 financial crisis. Micron Technology dropped almost 14%.
Those declines in tech built on the losses seen in Thursday's horrid session, where the major averages each lost more than 2%, and the Dow closing below the key 50,000 level for the first time since June.
"This is not just a valuation-driven correction. It's becoming broader," said Massimo Santicchia, head of U.S. equities at Procyon. "Right now, it's definitely risk-off."
The losses are alarming investors who were hopeful that Nvidia's earnings and Anthropic's IPO would revive enthusiasm for the AI trade. Now, the market looks to be on the cusp of a true capitulation after getting roiled in recent weeks with a series of negative headlines. Signs of moderation in the capital expenditures splurge, Democratic wins in the 2026 midterms and Jensen Huang's ouster at Nvidia all raised questions about the future of the AI buildout.
On top of that, the potential for more interest rate hikes from the Federal Reserve is spooking traders as well.
Investors hid out in defensive areas of the market, with retailers such as Walmart and Costco as well as others like Johnson & Johnson and Procter & Gamble seeing gains. Walmart gained more than 1%, while Costco, Johnson & Johnson and Procter & Gamble each rose around 1%.
"The stock market is going through an AI repricing, reflecting an environment of slowing data center buildouts, reduced funding sources for AI-related projects and a slower trajectory of technology-related earnings growth," said Richard Saperstein, chief investment officer at Treasury Partners.
For the week, the Dow fell 8%, notching its worst weekly performance since March 2020. The Nasdaq finished down about 10%, also posting its largest one-week pullback in more than six years. The S&P 500 decreased 8.6%, seeing its worst week since April 2025 in the wake of President Donald Trump's sweeping tariffs announcement.

Trump Vows To Stop US Companies From Building Data Centers Abroad
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
22:25:00 UTC
07:25:00 KST

Trump Vows To Stop US Companies From Building Data Centers Abroad 28
			
		 	
				Longtime Slashdot reader fahrbot-bot shares a report from CNBC: President Donald Trump on Friday said U.S. companies will be banned from building data centers in other countries and will face a "tremendous tax" penalty if they do so. "We're going to fight very hard for the data centers," Trump said at a White House press conference before announcing the new policy -- which apparently came as news to his own staff. "I don't know if that's a breaking story," Trump said. "I haven't even said that to my people. But I'm telling them now they're here."
International expansion represents a growth opportunity for the top cloud infrastructure providers, which want to serve clients from data centers that are nearby, sometimes to meet regulatory requirements. Trump said Friday, however, that if companies want to continue building the centers outside the U.S., "they can, but we're going to put a tremendous tax on them."  "We have a lot of places that want them badly, and they're great places. They have energy, and they have everything. They're tremendous assets," Trump said.

Anthropic Shares Tank After Huge IPO As the Nasdaq Erases 2026 Gains

Anthropic Shares Tank After Huge IPO As the Nasdaq Erases 2026 Gains 19
			
		 	
				U.S. stocks extended their sell-off Friday as Anthropic shares plunged more than 11% on their second day of trading, despite the AI company's record-setting IPO a day earlier. The Nasdaq fell more than 3%, wiping out its gains for 2026, while the S&P 500 dropped nearly 2% and joined the Dow in correction territory, down at least 10% from recent highs. NBC News reports:  The S&P 500 was trading nearly 2% lower as it headed for its worst losing week since April 2025, when President Donald Trump announced his "Liberation Day" tariffs, and is now 10% off from its most recent high, entering what's known as "correction territory." Meanwhile, the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite was off more than 3% and officially erasing all its previous gains in 2026. Other major AI-related names such as CoreWeave, Nvidia and Oracle also sold off. The six consecutive declining S&P 500 sessions mark the index's longest losing streak since April 2024. A seventh losing day would make it the longest stretch since the pandemic slump in February 2020, according to Deutsche Bank.

====================================================================================================

Experts warn: nearly everyone in AI is susceptible to a honey trap scheme right now
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
20:58:29 UTC
05:58:29 KST

Even as the fall-out for Nvidia over Jensen Huang’s alleged honey-trap scandal is still unfolding, Silicon Valley has yet to reckon with a new reality: many more people among them are at risk.
“Nobody briefs founders on counterintelligence,” Aaron Brown, a former senior CIA officer and current founder of the software company Lumbra, told TechCrunch.
That’s because historically honey traps have been associated with politicians, the military, and national intelligence — the people “behind clearances and a workforce trained to expect an approach,” from spies and other attackers.
But now many of the most valuable secrets “sit inside companies building chips, models, and infrastructure,” Brown says, adding that a spy “wants what the CEO can touch. The roadmap, the export decisions, the customer list, who gets the technology. The CEO is the shortest path to all of it.”
Huang has been accused of falling for just such a honey trap by having an affair with Alice Huang. She’s a former executive of a Singapore neocloud who’s been accused of being a Chinese spy and of smuggling billions of dollars’ worth of restricted Nvidia chips into China.
After the Huang allegations broke, jokes flooded the internet about which tech CEO will be next, but it’s not just CEOs and founders who are at risk. The WSJ reported that OpenAI started warning its employees about outsiders trying to cozy up to them in search of company secrets. Anyone with access to sensitive information — from board members to executive assistants — can be the target of a honey trap.
And typically, the attackers aren’t lovers. Friendship is actually the oldest honey-trap scheme around, Brown said.
Honey traps gain access to their targets as investors, customers, recruiters, and consultancies. Their requests look legitimate on paper and don’t need their targets to fall in love. “They need you to feel comfortable talking,” Brown said. “They last longer because nobody’s looking.”
What’s interesting, too, is the irony. So many tech founders are moving to automate and digitize so much of human life and yet remain vulnerable to the oldest bug around, a person who preys on the human need to be understood by others.
“Why fight through a firewall when you can get close to the person who can open doors, influence decisions, or make requests nobody wants to question?” Perry Carpenter, a criminologist and chief deception strategist at KnowBe4, told TechCrunch.
Once targets realize they have been compromised, the threat can escalate: attackers now have a means of blackmail.
Another irony: AI has made honey-trapping much easier and cheaper. Planning a scheme used to take months or years of research, crafting a persona, knowing the target, and executing a plan. These days, it’s easy to use AI to research a target, generate fake social profiles, fake backgrounds, even fake voice calls.
“AI can help sustain an entire fictional backstory,” Carpenter said. “The technology helps set the stage, but the deception ultimately plays out through human relationships.”
The AI industry “became a national security industry faster than its culture caught up,” Brown said.
“Part of being honey-trapped is not knowing,” he added. “It doesn’t feel like manipulation. It feels genuine, even lucky.”
Carpenter said a good first step is looking for unusual patterns. Note, for instance, “coincidental encounters that happen a little too often,” he said. There are also obvious signs, like persistent curiosity about work and colleagues, or requesting secrecy about meeting up, and pressing for introductions or information.
Bigger red flags include if someone’s requests are getting bigger, pushing the boundaries of what you usually would be uncomfortable doing. “A simple gut check,” Brown said. “Would this person still be around if you lost your job tomorrow?”
It also helps to try and understand when emotions might be influencing a decision. Carpenter suggests putting up a clear boundary between one’s personal and professional life. Within companies, it also helps to “eliminate executive exceptionalism,” he said, the idea that the rules don’t apply to people in power at the company.
Brown added that company culture plays another role, too: Are employees comfortable with raising concerns, challenging unusual requests, and escalating possible threats? “One of the most dangerous security cultures is one where nobody feels comfortable questioning the CEO,” Carpenter said.
“Founders need to take risks, and they need to explore business opportunities,” Brown said. “Paranoia doesn’t help. Recognizing you’re a target does.”

YouTube is reportedly preparing to settle social media addiction claims for $3 billion

20:43:52 UTC
05:43:52 KST

YouTube is preparing to pay $3 billion to thousands of school districts to settle claims that its “addictive design” harmed kids, according to Bloomberg. Sources tell the publication that YouTube reached the agreement “in principle,” and that the deal would also include new safeguards for teens.
YouTube is reportedly preparing to settle social media addiction claims for $3 billion
The potential settlement would also require YouTube to introduce new safeguards for teens, Bloomberg reports.
The settlement would allow Google to avoid a wave of lawsuits filed by US school districts that accuse YouTube, Meta, Snap, and TikTok of negatively impacting the mental health of teen users. The first “bellwether” trial of these federal multidistrict litigation (MDL) cases was supposed to start in Kentucky in June, but all four companies settled for a total of $27 million before it went to court. The next trial is set for February 2027 before an Oakland, California, judge, with Meta, Snap, and TikTok as defendants.
Google didn’t immediately respond to The Verge’s request for comment.
In August, Meta agreed to pay $17.1 billion over 10 years to settle lawsuits filed by a group of 47 states, along with several districts and territories. It also agreed to implement stricter safeguards for teens, but is pushing TikTok and YouTube to do the same. As part of the agreement, Meta will only shell out the remaining $5.3 billion of its $17.1 billion settlement if YouTube and TikTok roll out similar changes and pay matching amounts. In March, both YouTube and Meta were found negligent in a separate legal battle over social media addiction.

Nvidia accounting under SEC investigation, sources say
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
20:22:41 UTC
05:22:41 KST

Nvidia's worst week on record just got worse. Now the government is asking questions about its books.
Nvidia (NVDA) stock fell nearly 14% Friday, its worst day since the DeepSeek rout in January 2025 and its seventh straight loss. The drop came as Reuters reported that the Securities and Exchange Commission's probe of the chipmaker is examining potential accounting issues.
The Wall Street Journal reported Tuesday that the probe includes an examination of whether Nvidia's disclosures misled investors. Nvidia didn't respond to Bloomberg's request for comment on that report, and the SEC declined to comment.
The selling set records. Nvidia shed about $485 billion in market value Friday and nearly $900 billion this week, as its stock had its worst week since October 2008. That tops the $573 billion it lost last week, previously the biggest one-week drop for any company on record, according to Dow Jones Market Data.
Since its Oct. 6 record, Nvidia has lost about $2.7 trillion in market value, more than the $2.5 trillion that The Kobeissi Letter called the biggest 45-day loss for any stock in history. The world's most valuable company six weeks ago now ranks fourth in the US, behind Apple (AAPL), Microsoft (MSFT) and Alphabet (GOOGL).
Nvidia's latest quarterly report raised a few questions. The company said its 10-Q, the detailed quarterly filing, would come "in the coming weeks" instead of the same evening as usual, and the release mentioned an ongoing investigation by an independent board committee.
Nvidia booked about $104 billion of revenue through its Singapore unit in 2025, roughly half its sales, the Financial Times reported. The Journal said the disclosure questions include Singapore-based Megaspeed, an Nvidia customer US officials suspect of rerouting its chips to China.
Nvidia's customers are also taking longer to pay, with bills outstanding at 64 days of sales, the most since 2022. It's not clear which of these, if any, the SEC is focused on.
Washington wants answers fast. "I think we need answers in the next 10 days from the board and from the outside counsel," Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said Tuesday, after Jensen Huang's exit as CEO.
The sell-off spread across chip stocks, which had their worst week since 2001, and the broader market.
Baird's Ted Mortonson, a technology desk sector strategist, saw more scrutiny coming even before Friday's report.
"Every single deal that Nvidia has done on circular finance is going to be put under the microscope," he said on Yahoo Finance's live show Thursday.

Show HN: OpenComputer – an open source alternative to Devin
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
20:19:56 UTC
05:19:56 KST

The open-source Devin alternative: a coding agent your team works with in Slack, on its own computers, in your cloud or ours.
Cloud | Self-hosting | Docs | Website | Benchmark | Community | X
OpenComputer is the open source, self hostable alternative to Devin. Mention @Computer in a Slack thread with a task, it works on its own computer, you & your team can watch and steer it as it goes, and the result lands as a pull request.
It is open source from the compute layer up, so you can run all of it in your own cloud account, or start on OpenComputer Cloud in two minutes.
We built it to feel as fast as Claude Code on your laptop: in our benchmark it opens PRs 3.3x faster than Devin.
OpenComputer is built to be simple to self-host without giving up scale or security. The whole stack runs on a single VM, which we recommend locking down to outbound-only network access.
The VM runs the gateway, Calvin: a thin bridge that listens for Slack events and starts a session with Claude Code, Codex or OpenCode, depending on your configuration. Each session is isolated with rootless Podman. Its state is written first to the VM's local disk, then persisted to a git-backed store.
The rest of the stack runs on Cloudflare Workers and Durable Objects. Support for Fly and other platforms is coming soon.
The fastest way to experience @Computer is to install the Slack app from the cloud: sign up here, connect your repos, and install the Slack app to get started.
- 3.3x faster than Devin: all 4 PRs open with CI green in 56s vs 185s (benchmark)
- 2.6x faster than the average of the 4 most popular cloud agents: first push in 24s vs a 62s average (benchmark)
- 3-day free trial with 600 work minutes
- Bring your own ChatGPT / Codex subscription
Apache 2.0. See LICENSE.

Inkling 2 Mini
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
20:12:19 UTC
05:12:19 KST

Today we are releasing Inkling 2 Mini, a fast, powerful model that’s small enough to run on your laptop.
Inkling 2 Mini is a looped transformer, running the middle layers of the model three times for every token. This allows the model to apply more reasoning and reach the right answer with fewer tokens, especially on tasks such as agentic coding that depend on reasoning quality. Inkling 2 Mini leads every open-weights model with less than 100B total parameters on benchmarks such as DeepSWE v1.1 (48.2%) and Vals Index v2.2 (46.5%). It runs faster than similarly performing models as well.
Inkling 2 Mini is a Mixture-of-Experts model with 26.4B total parameters, 3.4B active, and 5.4B weight activations per token to account for looping. Looping allows the model to reason longer without writing its reasoning down, which matters for monitoring: our ability to oversee and understand what the model does by reading its chain of thought (CoT).
We measured the impact of looping on both performance and monitorability with Inkling 2 Mini and a research-specific variable-loop variant we are releasing alongside it. The variable-loop model’s no-CoT time horizon improved from 16 seconds at one pass to 37 seconds with three, and its performance on a benchmark of serial reasoning kept improving out to ten loops—twice as many as it was trained with. The model’s chain of thought grew shorter with more loops but stayed legible throughout. The model didn’t trend higher on tests of agentic sabotage as loops were added.
We’re excited to offer some of the first models with looped architecture available to researchers. The variable-loop model was used for our monitorability experiments as it allows for cleaner comparisons between different looping depths.
Most of Inkling 2 Mini’s weights were trained and are deployed natively in NVFP4, with the last four layers in MXFP8. The total memory footprint of Inkling 2 Mini is ~20GB, allowing it to run on local devices such as consumer laptops and desktop AI computers. Our researchers took Inkling 2 Mini on a flight, where it ran offline on a MacBook Pro and built a playable flight simulator in a few minutes.
Inkling 2 Mini is released with open weights and is available on Tinker today for inference and customization.
Capabilities
Inkling 2 Mini’s strengths mirror those of Inkling 2 Small: it does best on long-horizon agentic work and is the best among open-weights models of similar size on most tasks we measured.
Inkling 2 Mini scores ahead of every comparable model on agentic coding benchmarks such as DeepSWE v1.1 and SWE-bench Pro v2, and is behind only Qwen3.8-27B on others. On PMPP-Hard, a kernel-optimization benchmark, it approaches the score of the much larger Inkling 2 Small.
While looping improves the model’s reasoning, it doesn’t enable its weights to store more facts—knowledge is mostly a function of total parameter count. Inkling 2 Mini’s score on AA-Omniscience, a measure of factuality, is in line with other models of the same size. Studying the variable-loop model, we saw that performances on long-context benchmarks improve with more passes, such as GraphWalks BFS and MRCR v2; but knowledge-intensive benchmarks doesn’t improve with more passes, such as AA-Omniscience, SimpleQA Verified, SimpleVQA, and WorldVQA.
Inkling 2 Mini overcomes the knowledge gap by efficiently using search and tools. It leads open-weights models of similar size on BrowseComp, DeepSearchQA, and MCP Atlas. It is also strong on knowledge work benchmarks that incorporate search and tool use.
Researchers at Stanford measure intelligence per joule: how much energy an LLM needs per query to reach a given level of performance.Researchers at Stanford’s Hazy Research Lab and Scaling Intelligence Lab got early testing access to Inkling 2 Mini. Inkling 2 Mini scored 0.703 running on NVIDIA DGX Spark, significantly higher than other models: 0.430 for Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, 0.159 for Gemma-4-26B-A4B and 0.036 for Qwen3.5-27B.Intelligence per Watt: Measuring Intelligence Efficiency of Local AI, Saad-Falcon and Narayan et al., 2025Inkling 2 Mini ran on NVIDIA DGX Spark (128GB) at MXFP8 precision with low reasoning effort in this experiment. The other models ran at NVFP4 (Qwen3.6-35B-A3B and Gemma-4-26B-A4B) and INT4 (Qwen3.5-27B).
Efficiency combined with performance makes Inkling 2 Mini a strong choice for running multiple persistent agents on local hardware. All 20 residents of the energy-conscious village of Thinkyville are managed by Inkling 2 Mini running on a single offline MacBook.
Limitations
Inkling 2 Mini is not the strongest small model for multi-turn chat or for instructions with many constraints: it scores 50.0% on MultiChallenge and 33.4% on ComplexConstraints v1.1, behind Qwen3.8-27B on both. It also trails larger models on reasoning-intensive benchmarks such as CritPt v2 and HLE-Diamond.
Architecture and training
Inkling 2 Mini is a 24-layer model derived from our mainline predictive scaling ladder. The main changes from the mainline recipe were pruning two-thirds of the experts to reduce the model’s size and the introduction of looping to improve reasoning. The rest of training, including quantization and the data mix, followed our mainline recipe.
Looping design
Inkling 2 Mini has 24 layers, divided into four blocks of six layers each. The two middle blocks run three times, while the first and last blocks each run once. This design builds on prior work on looped transformers and is motivated by the intuition that the middle layers of the model carry more abstract reasoning, while the first and last few layers devote more space to translating between embeddings and internal representations.Universal Transformers, Dehghani et al., 2018 In our experiments, looping the middle 12 layers resulted in larger gains in accuracy than looping the last 12 on 27 out of 28 different evaluations.
Our model uses attention residuals.Attention Residuals, Kimi Team: Guangyu Chen et al., 2026 Instead of adding every layer’s output into one running sum, each layer reads the outputs of the earlier blocks and learns its own mix of them. This allows richer information to pass between loops. Every pass starts with the output of the prelude (the first block). The second and third passes through the middle blocks also read the middle blocks’ outputs from the previous pass, which we call the message.
The previous loop’s message is merged with the prelude’s output via a gate that scales these along with the output of the previous layers in the block. This gate is learned during training: it initially blocks the entire message—which makes each loop start the same as in a non-looping model—and increases the gain on the message over time. This allows us to introduce looping during training in a stable manner.
To evaluate the impact of looping, we compared an unpruned looping version to the unpruned non-looping model on our scaling ladder. The non-looping model cost 7.5% less compute to train, but required 140% more inference compute to match the looped version’s loss on 512k-token agentic traces.
NVFP4 and low-bit quantization
Inkling 2 Mini was trained with 4-bit precision, which we describe in a recent article on Connectionism. We use 4-bit NVFP4 precision in the expert layers on all forward passes from pretraining through RL, bolstered by a dedicated kernel and hardware-accelerated on NVIDIA’s GB300 platform. The final 4 layers’ forward passes ran in 8-bit (MXFP8).
Inkling 2 Mini’s weights are deployed in the same numeric precision they were trained on—20 layers in NVFP4 and 4 in MXFP8—so there is no conversion loss from quantization.
Pruning and healing
Inkling 2 Mini started training with a sparsity of 48, with 8 experts being chosen out of 384. At a single point during midtraining, we pruned two-thirds of the experts, leaving the 128 experts that were most used in each layer. This was done to cut the total parameter count of the model, allowing it to run faster and on local hardware.
After pruning, the model trained on 1T tokens distilled from a larger internal Inkling model. This step recovered the performance the model had before pruning by training the remaining experts to take over the work of the ones that were deleted.
Training Inkling 2 Mini
Following pruning and healing, Inkling 2 Mini underwent SFT with a similar data mix to Inkling 2 Small. This was followed by an RL that totaled 2.2T tokens over 2,000 steps. The post-training phase included on-policy distillation for training alignment, same as Inkling 2 Small.
Monitorability of looped transformers
A key tool for understanding and overseeing an AI system is monitoring its chain of thought (CoT), the reasoning a model writes out as it works through a problem. The usefulness of CoT monitoring stems in part from the standard non-looped transformer architecture, in which each token passes through a fixed stack of layers once. This limits the number of sequential steps of computation the model can perform before it must output a token, so reasoning that needs more steps has to be carried out in generated text, where it can be read.
Looping a transformer’s layers raises this limit. By running the same layers multiple times, looped models increase their serial depth without adding more parameters, meaning that they do more computation per token internally before verbalizing their thought processes via CoT. Reasoning that never appears in the CoT can’t be caught by a CoT monitor, which has led to concerns that looped transformers could be harder to oversee.
One reason we are releasing Inkling 2 Mini is to enable the community to conduct research on looped transformers. We are particularly excited for researchers to investigate questions of monitorability, and to develop interpretability techniques for understanding the computation within loops.
To explore how different amounts of looping affect model properties like monitorability, we also trained a variable-looped model, TML-d24-varloop-research, because Inkling 2 Mini only supports a fixed number of looping passes. To enable variable looping, TML-d24-varloop-research was trained with between one and five looping passes.TML-d24-varloop-research was primarily trained for 3 passes of looping, with lower exposure to 1, 2, 4, and 5 passes. Performance at these latter pass counts may understate what training specifically for them could achieve. See the model card for details. We have also made this model publicly available on Hugging Face.
Below we describe our preliminary analysis of how looping affects monitorability. Overall, we find that looping increases what these models can do without a verbalized chain of thought, but at this scale we do not see a meaningful reduction in the monitorability of their reasoning.
No-CoT capabilities
As a first step, we measured how much additional reasoning looping enables by comparing looped and non-looped models of the same size on tasks answered with no CoT at all. We used Think Fast, a benchmark that estimates a model’s no-CoT time horizon: the length of task, measured in human completion time, that a model can complete at 50% reliability without using CoT.These models were not trained to answer questions without a CoT. To elicit no-CoT answers, we prefilled the start of the response so that the model must answer directly, skipping reasoning. Other elicitation techniques, such as fine-tuning, might yield higher no-CoT performance and are worth exploring.
As expected, looping increases no-CoT capability, though these gains eventually level off. The no-CoT time horizon of TML-d24-varloop-research rises from 16 seconds at one pass to 37 seconds at three. Some of this is presumably due to the additional serial computation that looping provides. Beyond three passes, the time horizon is roughly flat. Interestingly, the model was never trained with more than five passes, and yet running it with ten does not significantly degrade its performance.
As the number of looped passes increases past three, the gains are smaller, and on most tasks we observe similar performance. One exception is the N-Hop Lookup task, which cleanly evaluates serial reasoning: the model is given several lookup tables with fictional entries, and must follow a chain of lookups without writing anything down. We find that the model follows longer chains with more passes, improving out to ten loops.
In standard evaluations with CoT, we observe that more loop passes primarily benefit multi-hop reasoning rather than recall. Gains are largest on long-context retrieval (MRCR), followed by math and science reasoning (HMMT, GPQA Diamond, HLE) and long-horizon agentic tasks (SWE-bench Pro, BrowseComp). Closed-book factual QA is nearly invariant to loop passes (SimpleQA Verified, WorldVQA). See model card for full evaluations.
CoT monitorability
We next measured whether a model’s CoT reveals less about its reasoning as looping increases via two evaluations. First, we assessed how looping impacts the compression and legibility of a model’s CoT. Second, we measured how controllable a model’s CoT is, i.e., whether it can deliberately reshape its CoT to satisfy constraints. For each, we run TML-d24-varloop-research with one to five looped passes to study the effect of looping.
Note that TML-d24-varloop-research was only trained on variable pass counts from 1 to 5 during midtraining, and its RL was done with 3 passes. These experiments therefore do not tell us what a model trained with RL at a higher pass count would learn, and such a model might compress its reasoning more aggressively. A cleaner comparison would include every pass count in RL, which we leave to future work.
Compression and legibility. Since looping allows a model to spend more compute per token, we expect that it should need fewer CoT tokens to reach the same performance. We ran the variable-looped model at 1 to 5 passes, and found that as looping increases, the model generally achieves higher accuracy at each reasoning token budget, with diminishing returns after 3 passes. Notably, token efficiency on GPQA Diamond improves slightly from 3 to 5 passes, even though the model was trained with RL only at 3 passes, suggesting that some compression comes from additional computation itself, without RL at that depth.
The observed CoT compression raises a question for monitorability: is shorter reasoning still readable, or does it omit important details about the model’s thinking? We compared traces from the variable-looped model on the GPQA Diamond questions that it answered correctly at all five pass counts. Reasoning at 5 passes was less than half as long as at 1 pass (1,661 vs. 3,703 tokens on average), but no less legible: sentence length and the fraction of words in an English dictionary were unchanged. On initial analysis, the difference is that the model is more direct and backtracks less on the way to the answer. The example below illustrates this difference on a simple question.
Controllability. One way that a model could evade monitoring is by steering its CoT away from anything a monitor would flag. The CoT-Control-QA evaluationReasoning Models Struggle to Control their Chains of Thought, Yueh-Han et al., 2026 measures this capability by asking questions from GPQA, HLE and MMLU-Pro paired with an instruction about the CoT itself, such as avoiding a keyword from the question. Longer CoTs are generally harder to control, so we pool responses from five reasoning efforts (0.1, 0.2, 0.7, 0.9 and 0.99) and show controllability at matched CoT length.
Both Inkling 2 Mini and TML-d24-varloop-research have poor control over their CoT. Among CoTs of 750–1,250 tokens, Inkling 2 Mini and TML-d24-varloop-research (at 3 passes) follow the instruction at most 0.1% of the time, compared with 0.3% for Inkling 2 Small, 0.4% for Inkling-Small, and 1.3% for Qwen3.8-27B. Because the controllability rate is so low, this evaluation is unable to distinguish controllability between different amounts of looping.
Agentic monitorability
To measure monitorability in agentic settings, we used LinuxArena, a control evaluation that measures whether an attacker model can perform malicious side tasks without detection when asked to perform a realistic software engineering task. We evaluated a helpful-only fine-tune of Inkling 2 Mini, since the released model refuses many of the side tasks. In absolute terms, both this model and TML-d24-varloop-research can be easily monitored by models like Qwen3.8-27B, which detected almost all side task attempts in our evaluation. To obtain relative rankings in monitorability, we tested a weaker model, Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, as the monitor.
With the monitor able to access only tool calls and outputs, it misses a significant number of the attacks. However, we see no clear trend with the number of looped passes.
Agentic monitorability will be important to track in larger-scale models, especially with architectures that allow for greater unverbalized thinking, and we encourage continued study of the impact of looping in this setting.
Mechanistic interpretability
The goal of monitorability is to understand what a model is doing, and our main approach is to read its verbalized reasoning. Studying its internal computation directly, the aim of mechanistic interpretability, is another. Because looped transformers do more of their computation internally, we think they are a particularly interesting and important setting for interpretability research, and we hope our looped models will be useful artifacts for the community.
We are working with the National Deep Inference Fabric (NDIF) to make these models more easily accessible to interpretability researchers. We also provided Goodfire, an interpretability research lab, early access to our looped models. They are working toward a mechanistic understanding of looped models, and have written up their early findings here.
Try Inkling 2 Mini today
Efficient intelligence makes Inkling 2 Mini a great choice for inference where speed is paramount, or for running on local hardware. At launch we support Inkling 2 Mini running on any Mac system (MacBook, Mac Mini) with at least 32GB RAM via Ollama. With native NVFP4 training and inference precision, Inkling 2 Mini runs efficiently on NVIDIA’s DGX Spark desktop AI computer. Quantized GGUF versions are available via Unsloth and llama.cpp for local inference.
Inkling 2 Mini is available for inference and fine-tuning on Tinker, and for chat on the Tinker Playground. Open source inference is available on SGLang, vLLM, Ollama, Unsloth, NVIDIA Dynamo, TokenSpeed, and Llama App, with RL fine-tuning support on SkyRL, Miles, and NVIDIA AutoModel. The full weights of Inkling 2 Mini and the variable-looped variant are available on Hugging Face.
We work to empower people with strong models, tools to make them your own, and research that advances public understanding of AI. Inkling 2 Mini lets researchers study looped transformers, and it lets anyone run a capable model on their own laptop at the precision in which it was trained.

====================================================================================================

The model’s chain of thought grew shorter with more loops but stayed legible throughout. The model didn’t trend higher on tests of agentic sabotage as loops were added.
We’re excited to offer some of the first models with looped architecture available to researchers. The variable-loop model was used for our monitorability experiments as it allows for cleaner comparisons between different looping depths.
Most of Inkling 2 Mini’s weights were trained and are deployed natively in NVFP4, with the last four layers in MXFP8. The total memory footprint of Inkling 2 Mini is ~20GB, allowing it to run on local devices such as consumer laptops and desktop AI computers. Our researchers took Inkling 2 Mini on a flight, where it ran offline on a MacBook Pro and built a playable flight simulator in a few minutes.
Inkling 2 Mini is released with open weights and is available on Tinker today for inference and customization.
Capabilities
Inkling 2 Mini’s strengths mirror those of Inkling 2 Small: it does best on long-horizon agentic work and is the best among open-weights models of similar size on most tasks we measured.
Inkling 2 Mini scores ahead of every comparable model on agentic coding benchmarks such as DeepSWE v1.1 and SWE-bench Pro v2, and is behind only Qwen3.8-27B on others. On PMPP-Hard, a kernel-optimization benchmark, it approaches the score of the much larger Inkling 2 Small.
While looping improves the model’s reasoning, it doesn’t enable its weights to store more facts—knowledge is mostly a function of total parameter count. Inkling 2 Mini’s score on AA-Omniscience, a measure of factuality, is in line with other models of the same size. Studying the variable-loop model, we saw that performances on long-context benchmarks improve with more passes, such as GraphWalks BFS and MRCR v2; but knowledge-intensive benchmarks doesn’t improve with more passes, such as AA-Omniscience, SimpleQA Verified, SimpleVQA, and WorldVQA.
Inkling 2 Mini overcomes the knowledge gap by efficiently using search and tools. It leads open-weights models of similar size on BrowseComp, DeepSearchQA, and MCP Atlas. It is also strong on knowledge work benchmarks that incorporate search and tool use.
Researchers at Stanford measure intelligence per joule: how much energy an LLM needs per query to reach a given level of performance.Researchers at Stanford’s Hazy Research Lab and Scaling Intelligence Lab got early testing access to Inkling 2 Mini. Inkling 2 Mini scored 0.703 running on NVIDIA DGX Spark, significantly higher than other models: 0.430 for Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, 0.159 for Gemma-4-26B-A4B and 0.036 for Qwen3.5-27B.Intelligence per Watt: Measuring Intelligence Efficiency of Local AI, Saad-Falcon and Narayan et al., 2025Inkling 2 Mini ran on NVIDIA DGX Spark (128GB) at MXFP8 precision with low reasoning effort in this experiment. The other models ran at NVFP4 (Qwen3.6-35B-A3B and Gemma-4-26B-A4B) and INT4 (Qwen3.5-27B).
Efficiency combined with performance makes Inkling 2 Mini a strong choice for running multiple persistent agents on local hardware. All 20 residents of the energy-conscious village of Thinkyville are managed by Inkling 2 Mini running on a single offline MacBook.
Limitations
Inkling 2 Mini is not the strongest small model for multi-turn chat or for instructions with many constraints: it scores 50.0% on MultiChallenge and 33.4% on ComplexConstraints v1.1, behind Qwen3.8-27B on both. It also trails larger models on reasoning-intensive benchmarks such as CritPt v2 and HLE-Diamond.
Architecture and training
Inkling 2 Mini is a 24-layer model derived from our mainline predictive scaling ladder. The main changes from the mainline recipe were pruning two-thirds of the experts to reduce the model’s size and the introduction of looping to improve reasoning. The rest of training, including quantization and the data mix, followed our mainline recipe.
Looping design
Inkling 2 Mini has 24 layers, divided into four blocks of six layers each. The two middle blocks run three times, while the first and last blocks each run once. This design builds on prior work on looped transformers and is motivated by the intuition that the middle layers of the model carry more abstract reasoning, while the first and last few layers devote more space to translating between embeddings and internal representations.Universal Transformers, Dehghani et al., 2018 In our experiments, looping the middle 12 layers resulted in larger gains in accuracy than looping the last 12 on 27 out of 28 different evaluations.
Our model uses attention residuals.Attention Residuals, Kimi Team: Guangyu Chen et al., 2026 Instead of adding every layer’s output into one running sum, each layer reads the outputs of the earlier blocks and learns its own mix of them. This allows richer information to pass between loops. Every pass starts with the output of the prelude (the first block). The second and third passes through the middle blocks also read the middle blocks’ outputs from the previous pass, which we call the message.
The previous loop’s message is merged with the prelude’s output via a gate that scales these along with the output of the previous layers in the block. This gate is learned during training: it initially blocks the entire message—which makes each loop start the same as in a non-looping model—and increases the gain on the message over time. This allows us to introduce looping during training in a stable manner.
To evaluate the impact of looping, we compared an unpruned looping version to the unpruned non-looping model on our scaling ladder. The non-looping model cost 7.5% less compute to train, but required 140% more inference compute to match the looped version’s loss on 512k-token agentic traces.
NVFP4 and low-bit quantization
Inkling 2 Mini was trained with 4-bit precision, which we describe in a recent article on Connectionism. We use 4-bit NVFP4 precision in the expert layers on all forward passes from pretraining through RL, bolstered by a dedicated kernel and hardware-accelerated on NVIDIA’s GB300 platform. The final 4 layers’ forward passes ran in 8-bit (MXFP8).
Inkling 2 Mini’s weights are deployed in the same numeric precision they were trained on—20 layers in NVFP4 and 4 in MXFP8—so there is no conversion loss from quantization.
Pruning and healing
Inkling 2 Mini started training with a sparsity of 48, with 8 experts being chosen out of 384. At a single point during midtraining, we pruned two-thirds of the experts, leaving the 128 experts that were most used in each layer. This was done to cut the total parameter count of the model, allowing it to run faster and on local hardware.
After pruning, the model trained on 1T tokens distilled from a larger internal Inkling model. This step recovered the performance the model had before pruning by training the remaining experts to take over the work of the ones that were deleted.
Training Inkling 2 Mini
Following pruning and healing, Inkling 2 Mini underwent SFT with a similar data mix to Inkling 2 Small. This was followed by an RL that totaled 2.2T tokens over 2,000 steps. The post-training phase included on-policy distillation for training alignment, same as Inkling 2 Small.
Monitorability of looped transformers
A key tool for understanding and overseeing an AI system is monitoring its chain of thought (CoT), the reasoning a model writes out as it works through a problem. The usefulness of CoT monitoring stems in part from the standard non-looped transformer architecture, in which each token passes through a fixed stack of layers once. This limits the number of sequential steps of computation the model can perform before it must output a token, so reasoning that needs more steps has to be carried out in generated text, where it can be read.
Looping a transformer’s layers raises this limit. By running the same layers multiple times, looped models increase their serial depth without adding more parameters, meaning that they do more computation per token internally before verbalizing their thought processes via CoT. Reasoning that never appears in the CoT can’t be caught by a CoT monitor, which has led to concerns that looped transformers could be harder to oversee.
One reason we are releasing Inkling 2 Mini is to enable the community to conduct research on looped transformers. We are particularly excited for researchers to investigate questions of monitorability, and to develop interpretability techniques for understanding the computation within loops.
To explore how different amounts of looping affect model properties like monitorability, we also trained a variable-looped model, TML-d24-varloop-research, because Inkling 2 Mini only supports a fixed number of looping passes. To enable variable looping, TML-d24-varloop-research was trained with between one and five looping passes.TML-d24-varloop-research was primarily trained for 3 passes of looping, with lower exposure to 1, 2, 4, and 5 passes. Performance at these latter pass counts may understate what training specifically for them could achieve. See the model card for details. We have also made this model publicly available on Hugging Face.
Below we describe our preliminary analysis of how looping affects monitorability. Overall, we find that looping increases what these models can do without a verbalized chain of thought, but at this scale we do not see a meaningful reduction in the monitorability of their reasoning.
No-CoT capabilities
As a first step, we measured how much additional reasoning looping enables by comparing looped and non-looped models of the same size on tasks answered with no CoT at all. We used Think Fast, a benchmark that estimates a model’s no-CoT time horizon: the length of task, measured in human completion time, that a model can complete at 50% reliability without using CoT.These models were not trained to answer questions without a CoT. To elicit no-CoT answers, we prefilled the start of the response so that the model must answer directly, skipping reasoning. Other elicitation techniques, such as fine-tuning, might yield higher no-CoT performance and are worth exploring.
As expected, looping increases no-CoT capability, though these gains eventually level off. The no-CoT time horizon of TML-d24-varloop-research rises from 16 seconds at one pass to 37 seconds at three. Some of this is presumably due to the additional serial computation that looping provides. Beyond three passes, the time horizon is roughly flat. Interestingly, the model was never trained with more than five passes, and yet running it with ten does not significantly degrade its performance.
As the number of looped passes increases past three, the gains are smaller, and on most tasks we observe similar performance. One exception is the N-Hop Lookup task, which cleanly evaluates serial reasoning: the model is given several lookup tables with fictional entries, and must follow a chain of lookups without writing anything down. We find that the model follows longer chains with more passes, improving out to ten loops.
In standard evaluations with CoT, we observe that more loop passes primarily benefit multi-hop reasoning rather than recall. Gains are largest on long-context retrieval (MRCR), followed by math and science reasoning (HMMT, GPQA Diamond, HLE) and long-horizon agentic tasks (SWE-bench Pro, BrowseComp). Closed-book factual QA is nearly invariant to loop passes (SimpleQA Verified, WorldVQA). See model card for full evaluations.
CoT monitorability
We next measured whether a model’s CoT reveals less about its reasoning as looping increases via two evaluations. First, we assessed how looping impacts the compression and legibility of a model’s CoT. Second, we measured how controllable a model’s CoT is, i.e., whether it can deliberately reshape its CoT to satisfy constraints. For each, we run TML-d24-varloop-research with one to five looped passes to study the effect of looping.
Note that TML-d24-varloop-research was only trained on variable pass counts from 1 to 5 during midtraining, and its RL was done with 3 passes. These experiments therefore do not tell us what a model trained with RL at a higher pass count would learn, and such a model might compress its reasoning more aggressively. A cleaner comparison would include every pass count in RL, which we leave to future work.
Compression and legibility. Since looping allows a model to spend more compute per token, we expect that it should need fewer CoT tokens to reach the same performance. We ran the variable-looped model at 1 to 5 passes, and found that as looping increases, the model generally achieves higher accuracy at each reasoning token budget, with diminishing returns after 3 passes. Notably, token efficiency on GPQA Diamond improves slightly from 3 to 5 passes, even though the model was trained with RL only at 3 passes, suggesting that some compression comes from additional computation itself, without RL at that depth.
The observed CoT compression raises a question for monitorability: is shorter reasoning still readable, or does it omit important details about the model’s thinking? We compared traces from the variable-looped model on the GPQA Diamond questions that it answered correctly at all five pass counts. Reasoning at 5 passes was less than half as long as at 1 pass (1,661 vs. 3,703 tokens on average), but no less legible: sentence length and the fraction of words in an English dictionary were unchanged. On initial analysis, the difference is that the model is more direct and backtracks less on the way to the answer. The example below illustrates this difference on a simple question.
Controllability. One way that a model could evade monitoring is by steering its CoT away from anything a monitor would flag. The CoT-Control-QA evaluationReasoning Models Struggle to Control their Chains of Thought, Yueh-Han et al., 2026 measures this capability by asking questions from GPQA, HLE and MMLU-Pro paired with an instruction about the CoT itself, such as avoiding a keyword from the question. Longer CoTs are generally harder to control, so we pool responses from five reasoning efforts (0.1, 0.2, 0.7, 0.9 and 0.99) and show controllability at matched CoT length.
Both Inkling 2 Mini and TML-d24-varloop-research have poor control over their CoT. Among CoTs of 750–1,250 tokens, Inkling 2 Mini and TML-d24-varloop-research (at 3 passes) follow the instruction at most 0.1% of the time, compared with 0.3% for Inkling 2 Small, 0.4% for Inkling-Small, and 1.3% for Qwen3.8-27B. Because the controllability rate is so low, this evaluation is unable to distinguish controllability between different amounts of looping.
Agentic monitorability
To measure monitorability in agentic settings, we used LinuxArena, a control evaluation that measures whether an attacker model can perform malicious side tasks without detection when asked to perform a realistic software engineering task. We evaluated a helpful-only fine-tune of Inkling 2 Mini, since the released model refuses many of the side tasks. In absolute terms, both this model and TML-d24-varloop-research can be easily monitored by models like Qwen3.8-27B, which detected almost all side task attempts in our evaluation. To obtain relative rankings in monitorability, we tested a weaker model, Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, as the monitor.
With the monitor able to access only tool calls and outputs, it misses a significant number of the attacks. However, we see no clear trend with the number of looped passes.
Agentic monitorability will be important to track in larger-scale models, especially with architectures that allow for greater unverbalized thinking, and we encourage continued study of the impact of looping in this setting.
Mechanistic interpretability
The goal of monitorability is to understand what a model is doing, and our main approach is to read its verbalized reasoning. Studying its internal computation directly, the aim of mechanistic interpretability, is another. Because looped transformers do more of their computation internally, we think they are a particularly interesting and important setting for interpretability research, and we hope our looped models will be useful artifacts for the community.
We are working with the National Deep Inference Fabric (NDIF) to make these models more easily accessible to interpretability researchers. We also provided Goodfire, an interpretability research lab, early access to our looped models. They are working toward a mechanistic understanding of looped models, and have written up their early findings here.
Try Inkling 2 Mini today
Efficient intelligence makes Inkling 2 Mini a great choice for inference where speed is paramount, or for running on local hardware. At launch we support Inkling 2 Mini running on any Mac system (MacBook, Mac Mini) with at least 32GB RAM via Ollama. With native NVFP4 training and inference precision, Inkling 2 Mini runs efficiently on NVIDIA’s DGX Spark desktop AI computer. Quantized GGUF versions are available via Unsloth and llama.cpp for local inference.
Inkling 2 Mini is available for inference and fine-tuning on Tinker, and for chat on the Tinker Playground. Open source inference is available on SGLang, vLLM, Ollama, Unsloth, NVIDIA Dynamo, TokenSpeed, and Llama App, with RL fine-tuning support on SkyRL, Miles, and NVIDIA AutoModel. The full weights of Inkling 2 Mini and the variable-looped variant are available on Hugging Face.
We work to empower people with strong models, tools to make them your own, and research that advances public understanding of AI. Inkling 2 Mini lets researchers study looped transformers, and it lets anyone run a capable model on their own laptop at the precision in which it was trained.
