AI claimed a solution to the 167 year-old Riemann Hypothesis — and a human did, too
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
19:32:08 UTC
04:32:08 KST

It’s a big week for Levent Alpöge: The mathematician saw his employer, Anthropic, become the first publicly traded frontier AI lab. He also appears to have solved one of the most important problems in mathematics.
“The precise timing is horrible,” he wrote in an essay announcing his findings and describing how he messaged Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei last Sunday night to ask how to announce that he had found a Lean-compilable solution to the Riemann hypothesis, which dates back to 1859 and is among the Millennium Prize math problems.
The hypothesis conjectures that there is a hidden structure underlying the appearance of prime numbers. If the claimed result holds true, we’re entering sublime-mysteries-of-the-universe territory.
Maybe the best way to understand how exciting this is to the mathematics community is to watch the experts puzzling through the result in a chat on Zulip. Mathematicians like Chris Birkbeck, a number theorist at the University of East Anglia, are posting thoughts like this …
The cohomology bound is proved by what looks like an automorphy-lifting argument. It genuinely uses the existence of Galois representations attached to regular algebraic, polarized cuspidal automorphic representations of GL_n over CM fields, along with base change, Jacquet–Shalika, Clozel’s purity, Bernstein–Zelevinsky, the main theorem of CM, Deligne–Ribet and more.
For my part, I tapped out of mathematics at college calculus, so I can’t help decode exactly what that means.
But that comment was followed by 20 readers reacting 🤯.
It will be a while before the mathematics community feels like it has a handle on the proof, but most appear confident that the formalized Lean result will hold. Lean is an an open-source coding language used to express mathematical ideas as code, allowing proofs to be formally verified. Several participants in the Zulip chat, including MIT mathematician Andrew Sutherland, say they have successfully confirmed that Alpöge’s solution can be compiled successfully.
Alpöge appeared in his essay to feel a bit guilty about releasing the proof without a truly human-intelligible explanation, but his obsession with the problem since childhood made him feel he “could not live” if he waited to release the finding.
Alpöge described a “stream-of-consciousness jaunt through the experience of finding all the pieces of the argument through incredible confusion with Claude” with more than a hint of AI euphoria, describing how the model “flew, and danced, and sparkled near that finish line.”
However, for all that action to mean anything, we’ll have to wait for humans to be able to understand the result and explain it to each other, something Alpöge has committed to pursue.
Alpöge and a collaborator, NYU mathematician Tristan Buckmaster, also released another important proof related to the Navier-Stokes equations, problems that helped kick off the race between frontier labs and mathematicians to solve outstanding problems this summer.
Meanwhile, Ravi Vakil, a Stanford professor and the president of the American Mathematics Association, posted a proof of the General Riemann hypothesis written by Stanford postdoc Alessandro Masullo, noting that “it is all human, with no AI. The hope was to bring Alessandro to Stanford for a year to polish the writing and then announce it after running it by enough people. Now it seems best to put out the current draft.”
Without being expressed in Lean, it is too early to say whether the proof checks out, much less if anyone understands it.
In a brief introduction to his proof, which he said is the result of 12 years of work, Masullo wrote that “if any message at all this work is meant to send to the mathematician who feels discouraged by the pace and tone of the current climate, it is this: we are still here. We are very much alive. We are not going anywhere.”
This story has been updated to include that Alpöge’s Lean proof has been checked by independent sources.

Winedroid – run Android apps without Android (WASM demo)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
19:30:16 UTC
04:30:16 KST

Yes, it's running APK files that I downloaded from F-Droid.
Is this running Android?
No, this is running Winedroid, it's written completely in Rust and it essentially replicates Android so it works everywhere.
What is the size of the runtime loaded in the browser?
12MB right now.
Why doesn't XYZ app work?
Because the project is still improving day by day. If you want your app's support added, add a new issue on GitHub.
Why is it kinda slow?
Again, it's still early, but also our JIT doesn't work on web, so the experience will be smoother if you run Winedroid natively instead of from your browser.

GrapheneOS claims Google is now waging war against it, could lock Pixel bootload
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
19:21:49 UTC
04:21:49 KST

Affiliate links on Android Authority may earn us a commission. Learn more.
GrapheneOS claims Google is now waging war against it, could lock Pixel bootloader
Nov 20, 2026 — 2:25 AM ET
- The GrapheneOS team has claimed that Google was trying to harm the project and wipe it out.
- Google was allegedly contacting Android OEMs to ask whether they were working with GrapheneOS, and was purportedly threatening these brands.
- The team also suggested that Google could start locking the bootloader on Pixels and taking other actions to make Android forks unviable.
GrapheneOS is the leading Android fork if you want a secure and private smartphone experience. Pixels were the only officially supported platform, but the team is also working with Motorola to launch a Motorola Signature 27 phone with GrapheneOS. However, the developers of this software have now made some interesting claims against Google.
The GrapheneOS team claimed on X that Google was contacting Android OEMs to ask whether them whether they were working with the GrapheneOS project. Furthermore, the team asserts that Google was threatening these brands by citing Google Mobile Services licensing terms.
The group also suggested that Google might eventually institute a variety of measures to thwart GrapheneOS and other Android forks:
We expect Google will begin bootloader locking Pixels, continue heavily pushing the illegal Play Integrity API and trying to make forks of AOSP non-viable. Many of their actions are clear violation of US criminal law. US antitrust law even includes prison time for individuals.
GrapheneOS leadership even said they wanted to talk to US attorney-general Todd Blanche and others in a bid to stop “Google’s escalating anti-competitive behavior.” In saying so, the team also praised Google’s engineers for directly working with the project.
“There are a lot of engineers at Google who support what we’re building and have directly worked with us,” it explained. “People there have repeatedly helped us and even explicitly added support for GrapheneOS to Google software including supporting Vanadium for Play services FIDO2 and passkeys.”
Unfortunately, the team says Google’s business division was the problem rather than its engineers:
Google’s business side is actively trying to wipe out alternatives. Google Mobile Services licensing has many illegal terms aimed at preventing competition. That isn’t enough for them. They’re very directly trying to harm GrapheneOS in multiple ways and outright wipe it out.
Either way, you won’t need to buy a Pixel phone if you’d like to use GrapheneOS. The project formed a partnership with Motorola last year, and the Signature 27 will be available as a GrapheneOS variant. Nevertheless, it would be very concerning if Google were pressuring OEMs to stop working with GrapheneOS. This apparent pressure would also come years after Google lost court battles over its agreements with OEMs. These previous agreements included terms restricting manufacturers when it came to shipping devices with Android forks.
We’ve contacted Google and Motorola for comment regarding these GrapheneOS claims. We’ll update our article as soon as the companies issue a response to us.

California Issues Subpoena to Nvidia Over Chip Sales to China
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
19:00:00 UTC
04:00:00 KST

California Issues Subpoena to Nvidia Over Chip Sales to China 17
			
		 	
				California Attorney General Rob Bonta has issued subpoenas to Nvidia and former CEO Jensen Huang regarding reports that they misled investors about advanced AI chips potentially reaching China. "The conduct of Nvidia and its former CEO raises bright red flags, including about the ways in which the company may have misled investors," Bonta said in a press release. "My office is investigating whether Nvidia or Jensen Huang's actions resulted in China obtaining advanced chips or confidential information -- and if Nvidia misled investors and the public about this risk, in violation of the law." The probe follows Huang's resignation amid allegations involving a suspected Chinese spy and comes as the SEC reportedly conducts a similar investigation. The Hill reports:  Bonta's office said the information obtained through the subpoenas will help it to determine if Nvidia, which is based in Santa Clara, Calif., violated state law through its alleged actions. "Nvidia is in a messy, transnational situation but the underlying principle of our corporate securities law is clear: Companies cannot mislead their investors," Bonta continued.
[...] Following the Huang scandal, the House Foreign Affairs Committee released documents Thursday showing the U.S. authorized the sale of nearly 900,000 Nvidia and AMD AI chips to Defense Department-designated Chinese military companies. An Nvidia spokesperson previously told The Hill, "The review and licensing process spanned more than half a year, with approvals subject to strict conditions and limited volumes." "Despite receiving some licenses, we have since stopped shipping H200s to China," the spokesperson added.

Trump threatens ‘tremendous tax’ for moving AI data centers outside the US
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
18:50:19 UTC
03:50:19 KST

President Donald Trump promised to put a “tremendous tax” on companies that build data centers outside of the US, even as states and cities around the country are placing restrictions on new applications to build them.
Trump threatens ‘tremendous tax’ for moving AI data centers outside the US
While policymakers across the country are putting new restrictions on the data center buildout, Trump is making threats.
“We’re not going to allow our companies to build data centers outside of our country,” Trump said at a press conference Friday following his summit with China President Xi Jinping and Russia President Vladimir Putin. “They allowed that to happen with chips and everything else, and it was very destructive to our country. It’s where we started going behind.” Should the US allow data centers to be built outside of the country, Trump warned, “China will take them all.”
Trump didn’t share many details behind the plan, and apparently was floating the proposal publicly for the first time. “I don’t know if that’s a breaking story. But I haven’t even said that to my people,” Trump said, flanked by Vice President JD Vance and Secretary of State Marco Rubio. “But I’m telling them now. They’re here. Many of them are here.”
Trump’s promise could be difficult to fulfill with a new Democratic majority Congress being sworn in within a couple of months. Even members of his own party recognize that the data center buildout has become an unpopular issue across the board. With states installing moratoriums and new restrictions on data center proposals, companies may be forced to look outside of the country, but Trump’s threat is an attempt to make that a less attractive alternative.
Here’s a transcript of the key part of Trump’s remarks:
Whoever wins SI wins. And you ought to take advantage of the data centers. Make them pay you. Make them pay your community. But take advantage. Don’t shut them off. And there’s always somebody else in this country. But if it’s not this country, there’s somebody else outside.
We’re not going to allow our companies to build data centers outside of our country. We’re not going to allow that to happen. You know, they allowed that to happen with chips and everything else, and it was very destructive to our country. It’s where we started going behind.
So I just, I don’t know if that’s a breaking story. But I haven’t even said that to my people, but I’m telling them now. They’re here. Many of them are here. And we’re not going to allow companies to build data centers in other countries. If they do that, they can, but we’re going to put a tremendous tax on them.

Super-hot rock geothermal could end data center energy woes
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
18:34:00 UTC
03:34:00 KST

Geothermal startups are in a race to see who can tap the deepest, hottest rocks. The winners could power the next phase of the grid, data centers and all.
The broader markets got their first taste of enhanced geothermal earlier this year when Fervo Energy went public. But this week was all about super-hot rock geothermal.
Geothermal has become a darling among investors, tech companies, and even the Trump administration. The basic technology has been around for decades, but advances in drilling techniques borrowed from the oil and gas industry has supercharged it. The U.S. has plenty of potential, too, with up to 57 terawatts of generating capacity, more than enough to power the next wave of AI data centers.
An emerging class of geothermal energy, known as super-hot rock, could help the industry unlock much of that potential.
The secret to super-hot rock geothermal is what happens to water at extreme depths. As it gets closer to the superheated core of the Earth, both temperature and pressure rise significantly. When they get high enough, ordinary water turns into a supercritical fluid — a sort of gaseous-liquid state that can carry tremendous amounts of energy. A single super-hot rock well could generate 15 megawatts of electricity or more.
Super-hot rock geothermal is inching closer to commercial reality. Quaise Energy said on Tuesday that it had landed Google as a customer. The hyperscaler said it would pay more than $500 million to secure 50 megawatts of power from a Quaise project in central Oregon, which will tap into searing heat three miles deep.
Google is paying a premium for the amount of power Quaise will deliver, but that’s in line with the company’s strategy of getting in early with potential suppliers. For instance, it was an investor in and an early customer of Fervo.
Meanwhile, on Monday, Khosla-backed Mazama Energy announced it had figured out how to keep its drill heads cool as they tap into super-hot rocks. And last week, XGS Energy was given a grant by the Department of Energy to develop and refine materials, well designs, and models to improve reliability at depths where super-hot rock exists.
XGS is dipping its toes in the super-hot waters, while Mazama and Quaise have made it their core focus, though they’re taking very different approaches.
Quaise’s technology sounds like science fiction: Starting at about 2.5 miles deep, the company will swap out its conventional drill bit for one powered by a gyrotron, bombarding rock with millimeter-wave radiation and turning it into ash. The hope is that the technology can make drilling under super-hot conditions cost-effective, something that’s challenging with traditional metal drill bits, which wear out faster at those temperatures.
Mazama, on the other hand, thinks that traditional drill bits will work just fine if they can be kept cool.
The startup’s newly announced technology, Carbon Chill, uses carbon dioxide in place of water-based drilling “mud” to keep the bit cool. At those depths and temperatures, the carbon dioxide turns supercritical, allowing it to hold significant amounts of energy. Just above the drill bit, the CO2 flows through a valve that looks similar to a rocket nozzle. As the gas flows through the valve and expands on the other end, the expansion rapidly cools it, allowing it to absorb significant amounts of heat. The CO2 also removes drill cuttings on its trip to the surface, where it’s filtered and reused.
In both cases, Quaise and Mazama are betting that their technology could leapfrog today’s state-of-the-art commercial geothermal power plants. By going deeper and hotter, they’re hoping to get substantially more power out of each well. Given that drilling accounts for about a quarter to a third of a geothermal power plant’s total costs, drilling fewer wells could make a project more competitive.
Investors have been betting that the technologies will soon prove themselves. Quaise has raised more than $270 million from investors, including Prelude Ventures, Collaborative Fund, and Engine Ventures, with Vinod Khosla stepping in as an angel investor, according to PitchBook. Mazama has raised $171 million from investors, including Khosla Ventures, Doerr Capital, and Bill Gates.
As with any new technology, there’s some risk that the new techniques won’t pay off. But early results suggest they will — Mazama drilled its second well 80% faster than its first, and its CO2-cooling tech cut drilling time another 20%, co-founder and CEO Sriram Vasantharajan told TechCrunch.
That, plus Google’s vote of confidence in Quaise, suggests the race to super-hot rock geothermal is, well, heating up.

====================================================================================================

We promoted developers into architects without informing them
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
18:17:51 UTC
03:17:51 KST

Yesterday, I was debugging a performance problem with our Google Forms addon. It turned out my colleague had vibe coded the logging part, so that he could monitor the new installs every week. Monitoring new customers is the most important part of running a business, so he is absolutely right in doing that. Except, he was logging the user’s email and Google has severely restricted how much detail you can get about the user since last December. So, AI had written a series of try/catch statements:
- First, try to get the email if the user gave consent to share it during installation.
- If it fails, it would try to fetch his user ID.
- If that fails, it would log an error saying the user has installed, but we don’t know his email or user ID.
All of these are one line of code, but internally each of these makes multiple API calls to Google servers. Since logging was the first line to execute after installation, the welcome screen took 15 seconds to load.
What is the right code for this?
If you are a senior developer or architect who understands the number of API calls behind each method, you would have:
- Logged just the timestamp of the install instead of trying to fetch the email or user ID, and told the marketing team they won’t be able to send the follow-up email based on installation alone.
- Or, if marketing wants the user’s email on install, you would have shown the welcome screen first and logged the email or user ID asynchronously from the browser, while the user is looking at the welcome screen. But calling your server from the addon’s welcome screen might require your server to bypass security and log an unauthenticated payload.
In other words, your job as an architect is to push back on requirements that can slow down the app. Or, find a way to work around the performance and security issues by making it async or accepting a reasonable hack.
Why AI won’t push back
AI is great at writing code. But it doesn’t know if you are launching the app or have millions of users, which it needs to make the right architecture call to log a timestamp vs email. It is also sycophantic and tries to execute your requirements exactly without pushing back, which means you end up with a slow app that takes 15 seconds to load a static welcome screen.
Essentially, if you generate code by specifying your requirements instead of specifying your architecture, AI will decide the architecture on your behalf. It is so paranoid about security that it will never write code to accept an unauthenticated payload. Even if you write it yourself, it will rewrite it into an authenticated payload and screw up performance or force users to sign in again on your app.
Your new job is to architect
AI coding tools are tuned to implement your functional requirements and two non-functional requirements, i.e. security and backward compatibility to avoid breaking your app. This invariably leads to:
- Architecture that trades off performance for security and slows down the app.
- Multiple versions of datamodel design with redundant tables, columns and code.
That means you define the architecture, design the datamodel first and ask AI to code within that constraint. Every time you give a requirement that changes your architecture or design, coding tools have to ask you before changing it, instead of changing it on their own. You have to tell it when it needs to be backward compatible, so it’s ok to keep multiple versions of logic or database tables, and when to delete the old logic and tables and keep a single version for maintainability.
If you are an architect who is used to designing the datamodel, delegating the implementation to developers and then reviewing their code to see if they have implemented it correctly, this should be natural to you. But the problem is that today we have taken developers who were great at coding and moved them into an architect’s job. They see that the job they love, i.e. coding, is now completely done by AI. But nobody has told them about the new job of pushing back on requirements, designing the datamodel, constraining AI to their architecture and reviewing that it falls in line. This is the reason for all the confusion and hate among developers about vibe coding.
Your future as developer
In our case, the right fix is simple. Log the timestamp, show the welcome screen first and tell marketing what they can’t get on install. But AI will never make that call for you, because it doesn’t know your users and it won’t push back on your requirements. That is the architect’s job. If you are a developer, the coding job is going away. But it doesn’t mean your job is going away. Non-developers who don’t understand architecture and design will end up with bad performance and unmaintainable database tables. So, stop missing the coding and become an architect. Learn to push back on requirements, design the datamodel, constrain AI to it and review the code the way an architect reviews a developer’s code. You got promoted. Nobody told you, so I am telling you.

====================================================================================================

- Or, if marketing wants the user’s email on install, you would have shown the welcome screen first and logged the email or user ID asynchronously from the browser, while the user is looking at the welcome screen. But calling your server from the addon’s welcome screen might require your server to bypass security and log an unauthenticated payload.
In other words, your job as an architect is to push back on requirements that can slow down the app. Or, find a way to work around the performance and security issues by making it async or accepting a reasonable hack.
Why AI won’t push back
AI is great at writing code. But it doesn’t know if you are launching the app or have millions of users, which it needs to make the right architecture call to log a timestamp vs email. It is also sycophantic and tries to execute your requirements exactly without pushing back, which means you end up with a slow app that takes 15 seconds to load a static welcome screen.
Essentially, if you generate code by specifying your requirements instead of specifying your architecture, AI will decide the architecture on your behalf. It is so paranoid about security that it will never write code to accept an unauthenticated payload. Even if you write it yourself, it will rewrite it into an authenticated payload and screw up performance or force users to sign in again on your app.
Your new job is to architect
AI coding tools are tuned to implement your functional requirements and two non-functional requirements, i.e. security and backward compatibility to avoid breaking your app. This invariably leads to:
- Architecture that trades off performance for security and slows down the app.
- Multiple versions of datamodel design with redundant tables, columns and code.
That means you define the architecture, design the datamodel first and ask AI to code within that constraint. Every time you give a requirement that changes your architecture or design, coding tools have to ask you before changing it, instead of changing it on their own. You have to tell it when it needs to be backward compatible, so it’s ok to keep multiple versions of logic or database tables, and when to delete the old logic and tables and keep a single version for maintainability.
If you are an architect who is used to designing the datamodel, delegating the implementation to developers and then reviewing their code to see if they have implemented it correctly, this should be natural to you. But the problem is that today we have taken developers who were great at coding and moved them into an architect’s job. They see that the job they love, i.e. coding, is now completely done by AI. But nobody has told them about the new job of pushing back on requirements, designing the datamodel, constraining AI to their architecture and reviewing that it falls in line. This is the reason for all the confusion and hate among developers about vibe coding.
Your future as developer
In our case, the right fix is simple. Log the timestamp, show the welcome screen first and tell marketing what they can’t get on install. But AI will never make that call for you, because it doesn’t know your users and it won’t push back on your requirements. That is the architect’s job. If you are a developer, the coding job is going away. But it doesn’t mean your job is going away. Non-developers who don’t understand architecture and design will end up with bad performance and unmaintainable database tables. So, stop missing the coding and become an architect. Learn to push back on requirements, design the datamodel, constrain AI to it and review the code the way an architect reviews a developer’s code. You got promoted. Nobody told you, so I am telling you.

====================================================================================================

Vibecoding an Inference Chip
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
18:15:16 UTC
03:15:16 KST

One of the more entertaining things about programming recently is that coding agents let me work in entirely new domains by giving me access to new programming languages and paradigms. Lately, that has meant hacking on hardware, now that writing low-level software is much easier. So, in this blog post, I’m going to try to recount the experience of vibe coding an FPGA, going through a series of “oh shit” moments several times, and giving myself a new appreciation for what’s possible if you have a large token budget (thanks YC) and interesting problems to point the models at.
I overheard some founders recently talk about how FPGAs work. I’ll admit I was relatively naive about them. I’d never really experimented with them in my startup or in college, so I knew little about them before. The basic idea I was familiar with: you can program a circuit into a reprogrammable chip. So rather than having a general purpose computing architecture (like a CPU) you can have a reprogrammable one, i.e. the configuration loaded onto the chip determines how its logic and routing are connected, and that (in theory) enables performance advantages. I also know that it took a mountain of C++ to write and involved manually analyzing waveforms for simulation and verification. I had never done this before though and it seemed cumbersome.
That’s about what I knew going into this.
Naturally, my ML brain thought “could we use these for AI inference”. I’m not the first to think this — there are a few repos attempting to port ML models to FPGAs on Github. But I hadn’t actually seen a writeup on whether coding agents are good at writing the low level C++ and Verilog code to program them and verifying the outputs so that they actually run accurately. (Spoiler alert, they are incredible at programming them.)
So I decided I needed to get my hands on one. AWS has F2 instances with FPGA hardware, and that seemed easier than going and buying physical hardware, so I asked Codex to spin up a reservation and got cooking. It had a relatively limited memory of 16 GB of FPGA HBM. Around the same time, I was working with the DeepGrove founders who were discussing their new ternary weight architecture that is well suited for memory limited cases. It’s SOTA on Mac hardware with actual agentic tool calling, so I figured why not, let’s see if I can get this working on an FPGA and fast enough to be useful.
What followed was a cloud agent running in Conductor for several days with me mostly just encouraging it periodically while it both (1) got a model fully running and (2) increased its speed by 33× from the first full-model bit-exact baseline to get it to a real-time relevant speed. It was one of the most mind blowing autoresearch loops I’ve run, ultimately writing roughly 3M lines across the experiment history and parsing 30M lines of output files, burning only $10,000 in Claude and OpenAI tokens to run hundreds of experiments. Most of the code is C++ and some Verilog. It’s been probably a decade since the last time I wrote any serious amount of C++ and I’ve never written Verilog. I honestly don’t know the first thing about Verilog. Most of my prompts were not particularly clever: they consisted mostly of me saying things like “you’re a superintelligent model, time to be more creative” and “let’s go for 2x faster, you got this”. Sometimes I brought some ML knowhow like “hey it’s time to consider speculative decoding” or “why is that running on CPU, you should port it”. But for the most part, it was just vibes and I’m pretty sure that with enough time the model would have figured out those things. Also, it seemed that both Astra and Opus (new Fable wasn’t out yet) were pretty capable at this and I could use them interchangeably. I’m sure someone has a good benchmark for which is smarter but both passed my smart-enough threshold.
From “it works” to 213 tokens per second
The chart follows the measured optimization sequence: 6 → 213 tokens/s, or 33× the baseline.
How much did the human advice help?
At one point, around when it got to 10 TPS, I messaged the DeepGrove founders about this, and they replied surprised that it was working so easily, saying they had tried this a few months back and the models were not quite good enough to be particularly effective. They sent me a prompt that summarizes some of their lessons and I copy-pasted it into my session to see if it’d matter. It was essentially the most significant piece of actual guidance I added to this session. The prompt is reproduced below.
Read the full FPGA design prompt
here is what someone else did for a dense bs=1 decode optimized fpga implementation for a dense qwen style model with ternary weights. you should take this with a grain of salt and pick things that you believe are acutally useful to add to your plan. Please also dont do things if you dont think is useful that take a lot of times like runnning really long test of a cycle accurate simulation etc or like physical design since those can take like hours. here are some ideas please pick and choose use this as context and not somehting that you shoudl be strictly guided by somethings that could help. prioritiize speed of like getting this up and running though..
MATMUL (TERNARY ENGINE)
- No multipliers. A 2-bit weight drives a 3:1 mux that picks x, ~x, or 0. The unused 4th code raises an error flag, so corrupt weights get caught.
- Negation is ~x + 1. Lanes flag negatives, and the row reducer adds the +1s once per row instead of once per lane.
- Activations are grouped integers: INT12, or INT16 where we needed more range (MLP down projection). Reduce as integers, apply FP32 group scales, accumulate in FP32.
- The pre-projection RMSNorm is deferred: quantize the activation first, apply the normalization scale after accumulation. It’s cheaper and numerically equivalent.
- 8 cores in two clusters. Projections that are independent (gate/up) run at the same time on separate core groups.
- Result: about half the DSPs of an INT8/INT16 design at the same rate, traded for more LUTs.
MEMORY
- Weights are packed at 2 bits, 8x more per transfer than 16-bit. At batch 1, weight fetch costs far more energy and time than the math.
- Weights stream from HBM straight into the compute lanes and skip the on-chip buffers (they’re used once per token). Activations are cached next to each core and reused across rows.
- HBM routing is one chain per memory channel instead of a big crossbar. Lay out data so all 32 channels stay busy.
- On-chip buffers are typed slots with an explicit lifecycle (open -> write -> commit -> read -> release). Each slot has a side memory for scales/exponents. The buffer checks format, range and producer, which catches scheduling bugs in hardware.
- The KV cache is MXINT8: 8-bit values with one shared exponent per 32-channel block. That’s small enough to stream, with accuracy held.
- For MoE: stripe each expert across every channel, and map expert ID to address at runtime instead of using a fixed program.
ATTENTION
- Attention streams over the cache in 64-token tiles with an online softmax (FP32 running max/sum). The full score matrix is never stored.
- Probabilities are UINT16 and the context accumulates in FP32.
- Each attention unit interleaves two independent jobs on one datapath (C-slow), which hides pipeline latency without duplicating hardware.
- Q/K head norm and RoPE got their own small vector unit, so they don’t block the general vector path. The V projection overlaps with that work.
- New K/V is appended to HBM as soon as it’s produced. We used a sliding window to bound cache reads.
SCHEDULING
- One controller issues work in program order, with tags and barriers. Engines overlap only when the buffer dependencies are explicit, so we got overlap without races.
- For MoE: the router is the new critical path, because nothing can fetch until it finishes. Keep it in FP32 (lower precision changed which experts were picked), and overlap whatever you can around it.
CORRECTNESS (maybe not neecessary to folow super close)
- We fixed a numerics contract first (formats, rounding points, operation order) and built hardware to match it. Vector ops must match the reference model’s order and precision, not just be “close”.
- Verification ladder:
      
  - Per-block cocotb unit tests.
  - One full layer co-simulated against the real checkpoint.
  - Multi-token, full 24-layer decode compared token-for-token with a reference oracle.
  - Byte-level accounting of KV cache writes.
- We kept simulation results, timing signoff and board results separate, and never claimed one from another.
- Every published number links to the report it came from.
PHYSICAL DESIGN (maybe not necessary for u)
- The chip has 3 regions (SLRs). Keep wide datapaths inside one region and register every crossing. Long links use credits, not combinational ready.
- We placed engines next to their data: memory logic near HBM, attention in the DSP-dense region.
- Screen logic depth (10 levels max at 250 MHz) right after synthesis, and fix it with pipelining, sharding, or C-slow. Full place-and-route is saved for integration.
- Dev loop: small change -> lint -> unit test -> quick synthesis -> depth/timing check -> restructure.
The coding agent diligently considered these asks, but it didn’t seem to make all that much of a difference qualitatively. Indeed, when I asked it later to read all of the logs and determine which of these instructions it actually used, it said:
Read the agent’s retrospective: what it adopted and rejected
Its biggest design ideas were already in my plan before you pasted it. You pasted it on Oct 29 at 21:50 UTC: 4,741 characters describing what someone else did for a dense, batch-1, ternary FPGA decoder. Within 70 seconds I had sorted it into ten ideas to adopt and five to reject, and written that into every design agent’s brief. The shorter remark from your friend Edward, about using lookup tables as logic gates, came 24 minutes earlier and left nothing on the speed line.
Design ideas I adopted: mostly confirmation.
- Already planned: streaming weights straight into the compute lanes, running rows in parallel, and reading the attention cache in bursts were all in the design brief I’d launched 35–40 minutes earlier.
- Match what was later built:
      
  - interleaved lanes in the router (step 3);
  - a separate unit for head norms and RoPE (step 4);
  - a program-order sequencer (step 5);
  - a registered boundary on every crossing between dies (steps 8 and 12).
- I can’t show these wouldn’t have happened without the notes.
Ideas I rejected: all five numerics shortcuts. Lower-precision activations, a deferred norm, an 8-bit KV cache, online softmax and 16-bit probabilities each change the output bits. That is where their design got its efficiency, and your bit-exact rule ruled it out.
Process advice: this changed things right away.
- What you asked: not to do things that take hours.
- What I stopped: a simulation that had run for about 24 hours, and two 250 MHz builds.
- What I switched to: a short loop of C tests, synthesis and a logic-depth check, with one place-and-route per integration.
- Likely gain: it got hardware results sooner.
- Possible cost, which I can’t prove: two hangs that only RTL simulation could show reached the board the next day, and the big integration lost 38 hours to them.
One real find it triggered. While checking their memory ideas, I found AWS’s own reference memory design, which reaches 426 GB/s across all 32 memory channels. The memory subsystem was built from it.
In other words, for most of the actual design ideas it is unclear but there is good reason to believe the model would have figured them out, especially because it came up with them many cycles later after several compaction cycles. But the process advice was useful immediately for getting to hardware results sooner and increasing iteration speed. In other words, the humans are still useful at defining how to iterate fast and what correctness looks like, and the agents are great to manage the exploration.
The optimization log
Here are the changes the autoresearch loop found. Interestingly, it’s mostly a series of modest improvements that compound 1.5x ish every time, rather than one or two giant ones.
0Baseline6.43 tok/s
Baseline: the first full-model bit-exact FPGA image (R8), served with host-side prefix caching.
1.0× from baseline
1Host overhead8.07 tok/s
Host software only: per-command overhead fell from ~10 ms to 0.9 ms (persistent register mapping, streaming reads, no per-command SDK calls or fsyncs).
1.25× this step · 1.25× from baseline
2Burst attention14.02 tok/s
A 128-lane attention engine that reads the KV cache in bursts instead of a word at a time, keeping the reference's exact summation order.
1.74× this step · 2.18× from baseline
3Parallel router20.53 tok/s
A parallel MoE router with an exact GCC-sort tie-breaker plus a streaming output head with built-in argmax; moderate-effort routing let it run at 250 MHz.
1.46× this step · 3.19× from baseline
4Glue engine30.24 tok/s
A glue engine that parallelizes norms, RoPE, quantization and copies between engines in certified exact orders.
1.47× this step · 4.70× from baseline
5New pipeline49.21 tok/s
The full new pipeline (streaming ternary projection lanes + a new sequencer replacing R8's control path), after fixing two hangs that only appeared in hardware.
1.63× this step · 7.65× from baseline
6Prompt speculation51.25 tok/s
Exact speculative decoding: the host drafts tokens by prompt lookup and the FPGA verifies several in one pass.
1.04× this step · 7.97× from baseline
7Router relay58.43 tok/s
Fixed a relay that moved router weights between dies at 1/6 speed: router stall per layer 43,862 → 12,292 cycles.
1.14× this step · 9.08× from baseline
8Head shadow94.44 tok/s
Head shadow v2 (start the next token on a provisional guess; catch misses early) plus cross-die timing fixes, enabling 237.5 MHz kernel / 450 MHz memory.
1.62× this step · 14.68× from baseline
9Draft model105.63 tok/s
Exact speculative decoding with a real draft model: a cut-down Maple on the F2's host CPUs drafts ahead, and the FPGA verifies every token.
1.12× this step · 16.42× from baseline
10Memory ports108.81 tok/s
Doubled the memory ports from 4 to 8 (two projection lanes, output head striped across banks): 1.39× per clock, but congestion cut the clock to 181/375 MHz.
1.03× this step · 16.91× from baseline
11Attention overlap134.99 tok/s
Hid the attention engine's KV fetches behind its compute, and replaced the router's 2,048-step serial sum with an exact order-independent one (12,292 → 2,338 cycles per layer).
1.24× this step · 20.98× from baseline
12Weight packing171.3 tok/s
Lossless 5-trit weight packing (−20% weight bytes), shorter serial steps, and moving the output head to the far die, which relieved congestion and allowed 225/400 MHz.
1.27× this step · 26.63× from baseline
13Re-speculation184.19 tok/s
Re-speculation after head-shadow misses, speculative MoE expert prefill during the router, and a die rebalance (without the unused DDR controller) that ran 231.25/450 MHz on both boots.
1.08× this step · 28.63× from baseline
14Parallel head loader213.13 tok/s
A head loader that reads both of its memory banks at once (+17.3%, measured on and off on one image), a runtime expert-prefetch policy, and the router-job timing fix; run one clock step lower (225/425 MHz) after a soak test found random faults at 231.25.
1.16× this step · 33.13× from baseline
How it compares
It’s difficult to do a 1:1 comparison across these configurations. I didn’t run the same autoresearch loop on every alternative, and I assume there is more performance to extract if I did. The point is to show how slow the FPGA was in its first implementation, and how far the optimization loop took it.
The measurements below are from November 15 on AWS, replaying the same 8-chat × 3-turn benchmark. Prices are the on-demand rates recorded with that experiment. “Exact turns” counts outputs that matched the pinned CPU reference. The GPU implementations are included for context but did not satisfy the same bit-exact criterion but are pretty close in practice.
What happens as the context grows?
This is one generation starting with a 16-token prompt and continuing until the 8,192-token context is full: 8,176 generated tokens. The five middle columns show decode speed in tokens/s at the indicated context length for several generations of iteration. The last column is wall-clock time for the whole run/
* For r8 and r9, the first two columns come from separate, shorter runs generating 2,032 tokens. The other columns come from the full-context run.
r9 improves short-context speed, while the complete long-context run is roughly tied with r8.
What the model actually says
These examples come from the final r9 image.
Arithmetic, with the steps
Read the arithmetic transcript
USER: What is 37 × 48? Explain the steps briefly.
MODEL: <think>
We need to compute 37 × 48. The user asks to explain steps briefly. So I'll do multiplication: 37 × 48 = 37 × (50 - 2) = 37×50 - 37×2 = 1850 - 74 = 1776. Or directly: 37×40=1480, 37×8=296, sum=1776. I'll present a brief explanation.
</think>
To multiply 37 by 48, you can break it down:
- 37 × 48 = 37 × (50 – 2) = (37 × 50) – (37 × 2) = 1850 – 74 = 1776.
Alternatively, 37 × 40 = 1480 and 37 × 8 = 296, so 1480 + 296 = 1776.
The final answer is **1776**.
A little Python
System prompt: “You are a helpful assistant. Think briefly before you answer, then reply clearly and concisely.”
Read the Python transcript
USER: Write a Python function is_palindrome(s) that ignores case, spaces and punctuation.
MODEL: <think>
We need to write a Python function is_palindrome(s) that ignores case, spaces, and punctuation. So we need to filter out non-alphanumeric characters, convert to lowercase, then check if the resulting string is equal to its reverse. Provide the code.
</think>
```python
def is_palindrome(s: str) -> bool:
    """
    Returns True if the string s is a palindrome, ignoring case, spaces, and punctuation.
    """
    # Filter to keep only alphanumeric characters and convert to lowercase
    filtered = ''.join(ch.lower() for ch in s if ch.isalnum())
    # Compare with its reverse
    return filtered == filtered[::-1]
```
Where this puts us
This was a fun project. I’m not sure I’m ever going to use this specific chip architecture in this specific way, and I’m certainly not claiming that an 8,192-token-context model running on an FPGA is the way I’d recommend anyone use something like this in production. But it’s pretty epic that this just works. Capability like this completely shifts the overton window for what a committed team of engineers can work on. It should mean that entirely new types of ideas (scientific discoveries, products, startups) are possible if you can point the models in the right direction. I’m excited to see what everyone builds.

Read the agent’s retrospective: what it adopted and rejected
Its biggest design ideas were already in my plan before you pasted it. You pasted it on Oct 29 at 21:50 UTC: 4,741 characters describing what someone else did for a dense, batch-1, ternary FPGA decoder. Within 70 seconds I had sorted it into ten ideas to adopt and five to reject, and written that into every design agent’s brief. The shorter remark from your friend Edward, about using lookup tables as logic gates, came 24 minutes earlier and left nothing on the speed line.
Design ideas I adopted: mostly confirmation.
- Already planned: streaming weights straight into the compute lanes, running rows in parallel, and reading the attention cache in bursts were all in the design brief I’d launched 35–40 minutes earlier.
- Match what was later built:
      
  - interleaved lanes in the router (step 3);
  - a separate unit for head norms and RoPE (step 4);
  - a program-order sequencer (step 5);
  - a registered boundary on every crossing between dies (steps 8 and 12).
- I can’t show these wouldn’t have happened without the notes.
Ideas I rejected: all five numerics shortcuts. Lower-precision activations, a deferred norm, an 8-bit KV cache, online softmax and 16-bit probabilities each change the output bits. That is where their design got its efficiency, and your bit-exact rule ruled it out.
Process advice: this changed things right away.
- What you asked: not to do things that take hours.
- What I stopped: a simulation that had run for about 24 hours, and two 250 MHz builds.
- What I switched to: a short loop of C tests, synthesis and a logic-depth check, with one place-and-route per integration.
- Likely gain: it got hardware results sooner.
- Possible cost, which I can’t prove: two hangs that only RTL simulation could show reached the board the next day, and the big integration lost 38 hours to them.
One real find it triggered. While checking their memory ideas, I found AWS’s own reference memory design, which reaches 426 GB/s across all 32 memory channels. The memory subsystem was built from it.
In other words, for most of the actual design ideas it is unclear but there is good reason to believe the model would have figured them out, especially because it came up with them many cycles later after several compaction cycles. But the process advice was useful immediately for getting to hardware results sooner and increasing iteration speed. In other words, the humans are still useful at defining how to iterate fast and what correctness looks like, and the agents are great to manage the exploration.
The optimization log
Here are the changes the autoresearch loop found. Interestingly, it’s mostly a series of modest improvements that compound 1.5x ish every time, rather than one or two giant ones.
0Baseline6.43 tok/s
Baseline: the first full-model bit-exact FPGA image (R8), served with host-side prefix caching.
1.0× from baseline
1Host overhead8.07 tok/s
Host software only: per-command overhead fell from ~10 ms to 0.9 ms (persistent register mapping, streaming reads, no per-command SDK calls or fsyncs).
1.25× this step · 1.25× from baseline
2Burst attention14.02 tok/s
A 128-lane attention engine that reads the KV cache in bursts instead of a word at a time, keeping the reference's exact summation order.
1.74× this step · 2.18× from baseline
3Parallel router20.53 tok/s
A parallel MoE router with an exact GCC-sort tie-breaker plus a streaming output head with built-in argmax; moderate-effort routing let it run at 250 MHz.
1.46× this step · 3.19× from baseline
4Glue engine30.24 tok/s
A glue engine that parallelizes norms, RoPE, quantization and copies between engines in certified exact orders.
1.47× this step · 4.70× from baseline
5New pipeline49.21 tok/s
The full new pipeline (streaming ternary projection lanes + a new sequencer replacing R8's control path), after fixing two hangs that only appeared in hardware.
1.63× this step · 7.65× from baseline
6Prompt speculation51.25 tok/s
Exact speculative decoding: the host drafts tokens by prompt lookup and the FPGA verifies several in one pass.
1.04× this step · 7.97× from baseline
7Router relay58.43 tok/s
Fixed a relay that moved router weights between dies at 1/6 speed: router stall per layer 43,862 → 12,292 cycles.
1.14× this step · 9.08× from baseline
8Head shadow94.44 tok/s
Head shadow v2 (start the next token on a provisional guess; catch misses early) plus cross-die timing fixes, enabling 237.5 MHz kernel / 450 MHz memory.
1.62× this step · 14.68× from baseline
9Draft model105.63 tok/s
Exact speculative decoding with a real draft model: a cut-down Maple on the F2's host CPUs drafts ahead, and the FPGA verifies every token.
1.12× this step · 16.42× from baseline
10Memory ports108.81 tok/s
Doubled the memory ports from 4 to 8 (two projection lanes, output head striped across banks): 1.39× per clock, but congestion cut the clock to 181/375 MHz.
1.03× this step · 16.91× from baseline
11Attention overlap134.99 tok/s
Hid the attention engine's KV fetches behind its compute, and replaced the router's 2,048-step serial sum with an exact order-independent one (12,292 → 2,338 cycles per layer).
1.24× this step · 20.98× from baseline
12Weight packing171.3 tok/s
Lossless 5-trit weight packing (−20% weight bytes), shorter serial steps, and moving the output head to the far die, which relieved congestion and allowed 225/400 MHz.
1.27× this step · 26.63× from baseline
13Re-speculation184.19 tok/s
Re-speculation after head-shadow misses, speculative MoE expert prefill during the router, and a die rebalance (without the unused DDR controller) that ran 231.25/450 MHz on both boots.
1.08× this step · 28.63× from baseline
14Parallel head loader213.13 tok/s
A head loader that reads both of its memory banks at once (+17.3%, measured on and off on one image), a runtime expert-prefetch policy, and the router-job timing fix; run one clock step lower (225/425 MHz) after a soak test found random faults at 231.25.
1.16× this step · 33.13× from baseline
How it compares
It’s difficult to do a 1:1 comparison across these configurations. I didn’t run the same autoresearch loop on every alternative, and I assume there is more performance to extract if I did. The point is to show how slow the FPGA was in its first implementation, and how far the optimization loop took it.
The measurements below are from November 15 on AWS, replaying the same 8-chat × 3-turn benchmark. Prices are the on-demand rates recorded with that experiment. “Exact turns” counts outputs that matched the pinned CPU reference. The GPU implementations are included for context but did not satisfy the same bit-exact criterion but are pretty close in practice.
What happens as the context grows?
This is one generation starting with a 16-token prompt and continuing until the 8,192-token context is full: 8,176 generated tokens. The five middle columns show decode speed in tokens/s at the indicated context length for several generations of iteration. The last column is wall-clock time for the whole run/
* For r8 and r9, the first two columns come from separate, shorter runs generating 2,032 tokens. The other columns come from the full-context run.
r9 improves short-context speed, while the complete long-context run is roughly tied with r8.
What the model actually says
These examples come from the final r9 image.
Arithmetic, with the steps
Read the arithmetic transcript
USER: What is 37 × 48? Explain the steps briefly.
MODEL: <think>
We need to compute 37 × 48. The user asks to explain steps briefly. So I'll do multiplication: 37 × 48 = 37 × (50 - 2) = 37×50 - 37×2 = 1850 - 74 = 1776. Or directly: 37×40=1480, 37×8=296, sum=1776. I'll present a brief explanation.
</think>
To multiply 37 by 48, you can break it down:
- 37 × 48 = 37 × (50 – 2) = (37 × 50) – (37 × 2) = 1850 – 74 = 1776.
Alternatively, 37 × 40 = 1480 and 37 × 8 = 296, so 1480 + 296 = 1776.
The final answer is **1776**.
A little Python
System prompt: “You are a helpful assistant. Think briefly before you answer, then reply clearly and concisely.”
Read the Python transcript
USER: Write a Python function is_palindrome(s) that ignores case, spaces and punctuation.
MODEL: <think>
We need to write a Python function is_palindrome(s) that ignores case, spaces, and punctuation. So we need to filter out non-alphanumeric characters, convert to lowercase, then check if the resulting string is equal to its reverse. Provide the code.
</think>
```python
def is_palindrome(s: str) -> bool:
    """
    Returns True if the string s is a palindrome, ignoring case, spaces, and punctuation.
    """
    # Filter to keep only alphanumeric characters and convert to lowercase
    filtered = ''.join(ch.lower() for ch in s if ch.isalnum())
    # Compare with its reverse
    return filtered == filtered[::-1]
```
Where this puts us
This was a fun project. I’m not sure I’m ever going to use this specific chip architecture in this specific way, and I’m certainly not claiming that an 8,192-token-context model running on an FPGA is the way I’d recommend anyone use something like this in production. But it’s pretty epic that this just works. Capability like this completely shifts the overton window for what a committed team of engineers can work on. It should mean that entirely new types of ideas (scientific discoveries, products, startups) are possible if you can point the models in the right direction. I’m excited to see what everyone builds.

Google says it’s going to shut off Google Assistant on smart speakers next year
Unknown

18:06:41 UTC
03:06:41 KST

Google is announcing some significant changes to its smart home software. Starting next year, the company will shut down the Nest app in favor of the Google Home app and retire Google Assistant on its smart home devices.
Google says it’s going to shut off Google Assistant on smart speakers next year
Google is making some big changes to its smart home offerings, including killing the Nest app.
The Nest app shutdown takes place on January 25th, 2027, meaning you’ll have to manage your Nest products, including Nest thermostats, cameras, and Nest Protect alarms, from the Google Home app instead. If you get the Google Home app set up, Google will move over your Nest devices, including cameras and video history, to the Home app on that January 25th date. The company will also automatically transfer Nest Aware (1st Gen) subscriptions to Google Home Premium. Google says it will begin deleting Nest account data on March 25th, 2027.
The changes for Google Assistant will take place later: On August 2nd, 2027, the Gemini for Home voice assistant will replace Google Assistant on smart speakers and smart displays, the company says. Google argues that Gemini for Home allows users to ask more complex questions and speak naturally instead of needing to use dedicated commands or specific language.
Gemini for Home was announced last year, so it’s not exactly a shock that Google is going to move on from Google Assistant in the smart home (especially when the company also killed Google Assistant on phones earlier this year). The company says it plans to share more updates about the changes ahead of the August 2027 transition.

Davies' career also has included technology and cyber roles at Estee Lauder, Barclays-Africa Group, Hewlett Packard and Siemens. Multiplying your Switch 2’s storage costs less than usual right now

Anthropic shares tank after IPO as the Nasdaq erases 2026 gains
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
17:44:01 UTC
02:44:01 KST

The surging stock market of 2026 capsized Friday, wiping out gains for the year in the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite while the broad S&P 500 and the Dow entered correction territory, meaning they have declined 10% from recent peaks.
The dramatic sell-off is raising fresh fears about the viability of the AI investment wave that had powered stocks and economic growth for the first half of the year.
Friday’s declines were led by Anthropic, whose blockbuster IPO just 24 hours earlier now feels like a blip after the stock lost 9%, wiping out hundreds of billions in paper wealth and putting shares just a few dollars above the IPO price.
Nvidia also dragged markets lower, tumbling about 14% following a Reuters report that regulators are looking into accounting irregularities at the chip giant.
With Friday’s losses of 3.7%, the S&P 500’s seven-day losing streak is its worst since the Covid pandemic; the index is now up less than 1% on the year.
The Nasdaq finished the day down 5.3%, and the Dow dropped 1.9%.
The losses also sully one of the most visible bright points of President Donald Trump’s second term. Amid sticky inflation and frozen housing and jobs markets, U.S. equities were the one part of the economy that appeared resilient. The AI investment boom created seemingly insatiable demand for stocks among investors, minting multiple new trillion-dollar companies in the process.
While Friday’s declines were stark, they were not sudden, capping instead a weekslong slide that began in early October as the combination of elevated oil prices, rising interest rates, mounting corporate debt loads, stubborn inflation, supply chain issues and a flagging consumer put increasing pressure on stock prices.
The sell-off picked up steam earlier this month in the wake of Democrats’ midterm victories, which raised fears of a slowdown in the tech sector’s data center buildout. Losses accelerated again last week as the scandal erupted involving former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang and advanced chips potentially being diverted to China.
“The stock market is going through an AI repricing, reflecting an environment of slowing data center buildouts, reduced funding sources for AI-related projects and a slower trajectory of technology-related earnings growth,” wrote Richard Saperstein, chief investment officer at Treasury Partners, in a client note. “This has been exacerbated by the Nvidia crisis and the Democratic midterm win, which were a one-two punch for the AI story.”
Alongside the stock sell-off, investors have increased purchases of U.S. Treasury bonds — a sign that investors are seeking more reliable returns amid signs of weakening growth from higher interest rates and a stalling economy in China.
“The pressure is becoming increasingly widespread, and that’s been felt across other asset classes too,” Deutsche Bank managing director Jim Reid wrote in a note to clients Friday.
FOR SUBSCRIBERSMuddled messaging on Russia’s next steps in Ukraine
FOR SUBSCRIBERSAgriculture Department breached by hackers, sources say
FOR SUBSCRIBERSDOJ’s focus on voter fraud fades post-midterms
FOR SUBSCRIBERSFlorida Democrats lament near-miss election results

FOR SUBSCRIBERSAgriculture Department breached by hackers, sources say
FOR SUBSCRIBERSDOJ’s focus on voter fraud fades post-midterms
FOR SUBSCRIBERSFlorida Democrats lament near-miss election results
For U.S. households relying on 401(k)s and pension funds for retirement, the downturn is especially unwelcome at a time when inflation continues to prove nettlesome.
Kevin Gordon, head of macroeconomic strategy and research at Charles Schwab, told NBC News that markets are feeling particular pressure from an increasingly “hawkish” Federal Reserve that is signaling aggressive rate hikes in the face of unrelenting inflation.
“They’re signaling that they want to be aggressive in raising rates,” Gordon said. Combined with a weeks-long downturn among AI and tech stocks, “we have dual forces hitting the market.”
A prolonged downturn could begin to cut more deeply into economic growth, he said.
“If you have enough of a protracted downturn, which we really haven’t had since 2007-2009 in terms of length and duration, that’s when you’d really start to worry,” he said.

====================================================================================================

Chips can survive the space environment. Can their financing?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
17:40:01 UTC
02:40:01 KST

The push to build data centers in space faces plenty of obstacles, from tricky engineering to a dearth of rockets that could take chips to orbit. But what about paying for the chips?
The data center ecosystem currently depends on debt financing, with cloud providers borrowing to build out data centers and then paying back the loans with a share of the revenue.
Those deals typically use the chips as collateral — if the data centers default on their loans, the lenders can take possession of the chips and resell them. That backstop makes it cheaper for companies to finance their chips. Even older chips retain value — according to Compute Credit Index Research, some H100s are selling for about half of their original cost basis from 2023, while some H200 configurations are selling for the same price they did when introduced in 2024.
But you’re not going to repossess chips installed in a satellite orbiting 300 miles above Earth at 17,500 miles per hour. The Financial Times is reporting that lenders behind SpaceX’s $40 billion chip financing deal asked that none of the chips purchased be used for the company’s orbital data center plans. It’s not clear if they succeeded in getting SpaceX to agree with the provision.
That may not matter to the company in the near term — SpaceX’s ability to deploy compute at terrestrial ground stations has proven lucrative, and on the company’s most recent earnings call, CEO Elon Musk detailed how he was dispatching engineers from the company’s rocket team to accelerate that build-out by taking on challenges like casting turbine blades for generators at faster than existing suppliers.
However, open questions remain about how easy it will be to find land and power to support the data center build-out. Many potential projects face delays and local opposition, while markets wait to see the state of compute demand and the ability of its buyers to turn it into revenue.
Musk, meanwhile, began saying at the beginning of 2026 that he believes space will be the “cheapest” place to operate data centers in two to three years. That’s unlikely until SpaceX can fly its Starship rocket in a fully reusable configuration. In its most recent flight test, the company failed to recover the booster stage but did succeed in landing the orbital stage of the rocket back at its launch facility in Texas. It’s not clear, though, when the second stage will fly again or how expensive it will be to prepare it for a second trip to space.
Even with Starship, however, SpaceX — as well as startups like StarCloud, Cowboy Space Corporation, Sophia Space, Orbital, and others — will need to buy chips. If the already daunting economics of space data centers weren’t challenging enough, it looks like they’ll be spending more to buy them than their terrestrial counterparts.
That might be an advantage for Google, which is focused on deploying its own TPU chips in space through Project Suncatcher. SpaceX and Tesla are working together to build their own chip foundry, Terafab, in the U.S., but that project has yet to break ground and will require years and billions of dollars before it starts producing semiconductors at scale.

They’re Beats’ first IP-rated cans, with ear cushions that you can easily remove for cleaning (or style points). They also stay in place during most exercises, while still delivering solid bass. Read our review.
- If you want over-ears and are comfortable going without active noise cancellation, the Beats Solo 4 are down to $79.99 ($120 off) at Target in pink and white through November 22nd, marking a new low price. Amazon also has the pink headphones on sale for $79.99, while other colors are $20 more at both retailers. The headphones are a noticeable improvement over their predecessor, delivering more detailed sound and support for lossless audio. They also last up to 50 hours on a single charge, while their memory foam ear cushions make them comfortable enough for long listening sessions. Read our review.
- The Beats Studio Buds Plus are down to around $89.95 ($80 off) at Amazon, Best Buy, and Target, nearly matching their lowest price to date. The wireless earbuds feature a slick, eye-catching transparent option and solid noise cancellation, especially at this price. They’re also exceptionally comfortable, with four ear tip sizes to help you find a secure fit. Sure, you don’t get wireless charging, but they offer IPX4 water resistance and up to nine hours of battery life on a single charge. Read our review.
MacBook deals
MacBook Neo
Apple’s most affordable laptop uses the same A18 Pro chip found in the iPhone 16 Pro. It features a vibrant 13-inch display, surprisingly good speakers, and 8GB of RAM, making it a solid option for students and anyone who needs a laptop for everyday tasks. Read our review.
- The recently released 16.3-inch MacBook Pro with a PROLED XDR display, 24GB of RAM, and 1TB of storage is on sale starting at $3,249 ($150 off) — a new low price — at Amazon and Best Buy. The redesigned laptop is Apple’s first with an OLED touchscreen, which allows for rich colors and deep blacks. It’s also noticeably thinner and lighter than the non-touchscreen 16-inch MacBook Pro, and features a new Dynamic Island that replaces the notch and provides quick access to controls and notifications. Read our review.
Apple MacBook Air (M5)
Apple’s latest MacBook Air features an M5 chip and starts with 512GB of storage, with significantly faster performance than prior generations. It’s still an excellent machine despite the higher base price, especially given the battery life and speakers. Read our review.
- The 14-inch M6-powered MacBook Pro is on sale starting at $1,849 ($150 off) at Amazon and Best Buy, which is a new low price. The Pro builds on its predecessor with a faster chip and support for Wi-Fi 7. In our testing, it handled multitasking, photo editing, and occasional video editing with ease while still delivering exceptional battery life. It lacks the newer MacBook Pro’s gorgeous OLED display, but its Mini LED screen still looks great, especially given its smooth 120Hz refresh rate. It also offers excellent speakers and a good selection of ports, including three Thunderbolt 4 ports, HDMI, and an SD card slot. Read our review.
Apple Watch deals
Apple Watch Series 12
The Apple Watch Series 12 introduces AI-powered Audio Intelligence features, including sound recognition alerts, Live Rewind, and Siri Recap. It can also track your heart rate every five seconds, even when you’re not exercising, and offers faster charging than prior models. Read our review.
- The Apple Watch SE 3 is on sale in the 40mm / GPS configuration starting at $199 ($50 off) at Amazon, Best Buy, and Walmart, matching its best price to date. Apple’s newest entry-level smartwatch remains our top recommendation for anyone who wants a basic fitness tracker, as it offers all the essentials alongside an always-on display and Apple’s S10 chip, which enables on-device Siri requests.
Apple AirTag (second-gen)
Apple’s latest AirTags feature an upgraded ultra-wideband chip for more precise location tracking, a noticeably louder speaker, and an expanded Bluetooth range. Read our review.
Baseus AM52 power bank (with built-in cable)
The Baseus PicoGo AM52 is a 10,000mAh magnetic power bank that supports fast 25W Qi2.2 wireless charging and up to 45W wired charging via its built-in USB-C cable. It was one of the fastest wireless chargers we tested, bringing an iPhone 17 Pro Max to 65 percent in an hour. Read our guide to the best Qi2 batteries.
- You can buy the last-gen iPad Mini with 128GB of storage and Wi-Fi on Amazon in select colors for $484 ($115 off), which is the best price we’ve seen since Apple’s recent price hikes. It has a smaller screen and a slower chip than the newer model, and it lacks an OLED screen, the Acoustic Display, and water resistance. Still, it’s a great option for reading and everyday use that’s currently more affordable than its $699 successor, with support for Apple Intelligence and the Apple Pencil Pro. Read our review.
Sharge Icemag 3 Power Bank
The Icemag 3 can wirelessly deliver up to 25W of power to compatible smartphones and up to 35W wired charging via USB-C. The 10,000mAh Qi2 charger can also act as a foldable kickstand, comes with an integrated USB-C cable that serves as a lanyard, and sports a transparent design with colorful RGB lighting.
Apple Studio Display (2026)
Designed to pair with a Mac, the latest Apple Studio Display features a sharp 5K Retina display, a great 12-megapixel Center Stage camera that keeps you in frame during video calls, and a good six-speaker sound system with spatial audio. Sadly, it only offers two Thunderbolt 5 ports in addition to a pair of USB-C ports. Read our review.
- Apple’s latest Magic Mouse is matching its all-time low of $59.99 ($20 off) at Amazon. The wireless mouse features a touch-sensitive surface that lets you scroll through documents and swipe between apps with simple gestures; it also pairs automatically with compatible Macs and offers support for USB-C.

Unforced Navier-Stokes Singularity and the Generalized Riemann Hypothesis
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
17:21:36 UTC
02:21:36 KST

Obviously these results will be reported widely in other outlets, but I will note them here, in decreasing order of how “digested” the results currently are:
- Levent Alpöge, Tristan Buckmaster, and Matei Coiculescu, with heavy AI assistance, have written a human-readable proof of the finite time blowup for the unforced incompressible Euler equations in three dimensions, which is formalized in Lean here.
- Levent Alpöge and Tristan Buckmaster, again with heavy AI usage, have generated a proof of the finite time blowup for the unforced incompressible Navier Stokes equations in three dimensions, again with a Lean formalization. Here, the paper is technically human-readable and the general strategy of proof is broadly clear (following one of the expected paths to this result, namely to numerically build an approximate discretely self-similar solution and then use rigorous perturbation theory to convert this to an exact solution); but more work needs to be done to create a proper, professionally written proof.
- Levent Alpöge, using an internal AI model, has generated an (enormous) Lean-only proof of the Generalized Riemann Hypothesis. This Lean Zulip thread is probably the best place to follow the efforts to verify the proof and extract the strategy of proof (which, from very initial analysis, appears to use the full power of modern developments on the Langlands program and arithmetic geometry). (UPDATE: the moderators of the Lean thread are now focusing the discussion on the Lean-related aspects of the proof only.) I imagine the digestion process will take some time, though in this case at least we have a human author to communicate with to assist with this task.
See also Buckmaster’s Mastodon post regarding the first two results.

====================================================================================================
