====================================================================================================

Running two PSP emulators on one Switch 2 and sharing one via GameShare
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
17:14:03 UTC
02:14:03 KST

This branch experiments with a separate PPSSPP guest running on the host Switch 2 and streamed to a stock GameShare client. It is not a mirrored controller or a second PSP controller in the host emulator. Compilation and host tests do not establish console operation or acceptable two-game performance.
The working single-instance checkpoint is switch2-single-instance-working
(commit 1c3b38a). Its exact tested NRO, package and matching debug ELF remain
under dist-switch2/checkpoints/switch2-single-instance-working/.
sh Tools/build-switch2-gameshare.sh
python3 Tools/deploy-switch2.py 192.168.1.182 --experimental-gameshare
Open FTPD for the second command. A successful transfer is accepted without a
validation download. The experiment installs as PPSSPP GameShare, at
sdmc:/switch/ppsspp-switch2-gameshare/ppsspp-switch2-gameshare.nro. It leaves
the working ppsspp-switch2 NRO and its settings/saves in place. The normal
Tools/build-switch2.sh selects GameShare OFF. Both builds reuse the build
cache, but packaging rejects mismatched native/experimental modes.
Launch through the same GameShare-capable native title override used for the
Moonlight SDK example. A generic title override is not known to grant the
required GameShare context. If another title locks profile switching, the SDK
uses the Nintendo profile selected for that launch when available. The host
log distinguishes a missing launch profile from a later StreamPlay or
matchmaking error. Choose GameShare (experimental) in the host's
main or pause menu, then join from the client's system GameShare screen.
The client should see its own PPSSPP menu, with a guest-only startup message,
and browse sdmc:/switch/ppsspp-switch2/games. Both browsers can access SD-card
games; no game files are copied into the package.
Choose the same GameShare action to stop sharing. The guest pauses between sessions, retaining its independent emulator state. Exiting the guest's PPSSPP ends that guest instance; restart the application before creating another. Exit the host normally when done. If a real cleanup failure reports retained resources, close the entire hosting title from HOME, rather than returning to hbmenu. The SDK explicitly requires this for uncertain native ownership.
The build links a second PPSSPP frontend/core into a relocatable object and renames every defined core-owned global symbol and ELF COMDAT group signature. Only eight plain-data C entry points are exported. The audit requires distinct Config, memory-base and core-state symbols; the host also compares their runtime addresses before starting. SDK/OS/C++ runtime entry points remain shared. The two emulators have independent core, UI, filesystem state, JIT registries, Vulkan contexts and settings/memory-stick directories. They share one OS process, so a fatal error in either copy can still affect the entire app.
The host keeps its 1920x1080 output and 120 Hz request. The guest uses PPSSPP's existing offscreen Vulkan path at 1280x720 with a 60 fps presentation target. A readback callback transfers completed RGBA frames to a latest-frame mailbox; a dedicated presenter uploads them to a separate native VI2 guest layer. This first route adds CPU readback/swizzle cost; zero-copy is not claimed.
The GameShare SDK is vendored from Moonlight-Switch's research worktree.
Switch/GameShare/sdk-provenance.json records the source revision and file
hashes. Its MIT license and per-file ISC notices are retained. The SDK's pinned
CPU-console uploader is disabled. The guest renderer borrows the statically
linked OunceVK driver's GSV main and worker sessions and uses its registered VM.
It owns the guest image backing, registrations and client. The adapter tracks
the driver's occupied slots at the libounce call boundary and reserves a free
slot for guest range retirement. Each GameShare session reuses the reserved
slot's completion event, which closes with the Vulkan driver's GPU. It does not access
OunceVK's internal structs. Retire the guest images before Vulkan teardown.
Coexistence and reuse on hardware still require qualification.
The SDK owns matchmaking, native hosting, controller reads and the separate VI2 display/layer. Renderer retirement runs before native Finalize; layer closure runs afterwards. Uncertain presentation or release/retirement failure retains backing and blocks ordinary unload. Guest controls never enter the host emulator. Stale/disconnected snapshots become neutral.
Host settings/saves use ppsspp-switch2-gameshare/config/ppsspp; guest
settings/saves use ppsspp-switch2-gameshare/guest/config/ppsspp. The existing
working app's memory stick is not reused. Guest native text-entry applets are
disabled because their routing to the remote screen is not implemented.
PPSSPP sends the guest emulator's PCM to the native per-guest queue and keeps the host emulator on normal AudOut. See the recovered audio contract. The console test confirmed audible client playback in the direct-audio build.
Start GameShare and join from the client. Open the same game on both screens, then enter its local wireless or Ad Hoc mode. Create a lobby on either console and look for it on the other. If the host had already initialized multiplayer before pairing, restart that game once to select the local route.
While GameShare is hosting, PPSSPP runs its built-in discovery service and aemu_postoffice packet relay on IPv4 loopback ports 27314 and 27315. Both cores use the private route automatically with their existing, independently configured PSP MAC addresses. The guest still runs on the host Switch 2, so game packets stay on that console. The route does not use a public relay, Wi-Fi IP address, or UPnP mapping, and it does not change either core's saved network settings. Stopping GameShare closes the listeners. A game already in a lobby may need to leave and re-enter multiplayer after GameShare is restarted.
The user reported a successful Tekken session in the first loopback build.
The subsequent build keeps each core's original MAC address and passed the ten
paired desktop tests. That save-preserving version is tagged
switch2-gameshare-multiplayer-baseline (commit 0699365). Its NRO, ZIP and
debug ELF are in dist-switch2-gameshare/checkpoints/multiplayer-baseline/.
The earlier console run used the temporary-MAC build.
Host boot.log, ppsspp.log, native-exit.bin and native-fault.bin live in
the experimental app directory. The guest writes guest/ppsspp.log. Native
fault/exit records with valid headers are retained as .previous on the next
launch. Preserve diagnostics before relaunching after a failure.
python3 Switch/tests/run.py
python3 Switch/tests/run_gameshare.py
python3 Switch/GameShare/sdk/tests/native/run.py
python3 Switch/GameShare/sdk/tests/guest_input/run.py
python3 Switch/GameShare/sdk/tests/guest_display/run.py
git diff --check
Actual-source sanitizer tests cover native guest-image sector locations, partial creation, successful retirement, retained uncertain presents and release/range timeouts. SDK tests cover native lifecycle/cancellation, controller parsing/freshness and display ownership. They do not simulate the GPU, native title permissions, GameShare radio behavior or console performance. CPU, GPU, threading and shutdown regression risks require console testing; serialized PSP state layouts are unchanged.
The dual-core AArch64 NRO and ZIP built and validated. The symbol audit found 27,173 renamed core-owned symbols and eight guest ABI exports. All 28 SDK native lifecycle scenarios, the input parser/freshness suite and display lifetime suite passed under ASan/UBSan. Six actual-source image-owner scenarios passed (clean, partial create, uncertain present, release timeout, range retirement timeout and unregister failure). Existing JIT publication and dedicated-worker tests also passed.
Host regression suites: 102 C++ unit tests passed; 527 executed single-instance
PSP cases and ten paired relay scenarios passed. Eleven paired P2P cases remain
unrun for the existing missing 127.0.0.2 host loopback alias, causing the
aggregate runner's failure status. No executed regression test failed.
Console dual-instance rendering, pairing, guest controls/audio and sustained
performance still require physical qualification.
The initial experimental build produced a system error before the PPSSPP menu. Its boot log reached display configuration; native fault/exit records remained empty. The separate working single-instance installation is unchanged. Pairing and the second emulator have not yet been reached on the console.
The SDK bridge is compiled as C++20 separately from the C++17 frontend. Startup
checkpoints around Vulkan initialization write startup-stage.bin directly
with native flushes. Its four little-endian 64-bit words are magic
0x3254475350505350, stage number, recorder address and system tick. Stages
1–4 cover diagnostics, display configuration, graphics-object construction and
error-string construction; stages 10 and 11 bracket InitAPI's initial string
assignment. Stages 20 and 21 bracket NativeInit, and 22 precedes window setup.
The diagnostic boot marker is comdat-isolation. The native exit recorder
also wraps svcBreak (kind 6, reason in the high 32 bits and a readable four-byte
detail in the low 32 bits).
The final ELF showed that the linker turned a required host string-constructor call into a no-op. GCC used a local C5 signature for the constructor group. Renaming only global symbols let the guest group retain that signature, causing the linker to discard the host copy. The build now renames and checks all 7,351 guest COMDAT groups in addition to the global symbol audit. Disassembly of the corrected ELF confirms that host startup calls its own string constructor and the guest constructor has a distinct address.
The console opened the corrected build and started a host game. On the first
GameShare attempt, VI2 reached the renderer hook, where a second GSV GPU open
failed with 0x200F3. The guest emulator independently reached offscreen
Vulkan initialization. The revised uploader uses the existing driver session.
The host also joins the SDK thread after terminal states before asking if
native resources were retained, so a completed thread alone no longer forces a
title restart. Confirm pairing, guest frames and stop/restart on hardware.
The shared-session build paired with the client, and the SDK completed cleanup. The client remained black because PPSSPP rejected readback from the guest's offscreen Vulkan presentation before any frame could reach the uploader. Offscreen presentation now declares its transfer-source capability. The copy waits for the color attachment writes and skips frames without an acquired image. A local MoltenVK run cleared three consecutive offscreen frames to different colors and verified every returned RGBA byte. Client presentation and independent input still require a console check.
The console confirmed that the host and client can see and control their own
PPSSPP screens. Both emulator audio streams were audible from the host. The
console-confirmed NRO, matching ELF and installation receipt are saved under
dist-switch2-gameshare/checkpoints/dual-screen-controls/.
The client was silent in separate-display sessions with both AudOut and
Audren. The Audren test boot log confirms the application mix used Audren,
the guest received frames and SDK cleanup completed. The launcher NRO was
restored to the console-confirmed dual-screen build using the saved SD-card
copy. The renderer trial is recorded in commit c5a8716.
At the dual-screen checkpoint, both games played through the host. The SDK exposed an application-wide mirroring flag and device-volume configuration.
The shipped SDK exports a separate PCM writer and per-guest queue counters. The reusable Moonlight SDK and its PPSSPP copy now expose a PCM callback. Tests of the native worker cover the recovered command layout, the unwritten tail after a partial write, and a failed audio command that leaves video and input running.
On November 19, 2026, the console test confirmed sound on the GameShare
client. The working build is tagged switch2-gameshare-guest-audio-working
(commit 50cd977). The exact installed NRO, SD-card package, matching ELF,
SDK archive and build records are saved under
dist-switch2-gameshare/checkpoints/guest-audio-working/. For a console
rollback, reinstall that NRO as
/switch/ppsspp-switch2-gameshare/ppsspp-switch2-gameshare.nro.

The console confirmed that the host and client can see and control their own
PPSSPP screens. Both emulator audio streams were audible from the host. The
console-confirmed NRO, matching ELF and installation receipt are saved under
dist-switch2-gameshare/checkpoints/dual-screen-controls/.
The client was silent in separate-display sessions with both AudOut and
Audren. The Audren test boot log confirms the application mix used Audren,
the guest received frames and SDK cleanup completed. The launcher NRO was
restored to the console-confirmed dual-screen build using the saved SD-card
copy. The renderer trial is recorded in commit c5a8716.
At the dual-screen checkpoint, both games played through the host. The SDK exposed an application-wide mirroring flag and device-volume configuration.
The shipped SDK exports a separate PCM writer and per-guest queue counters. The reusable Moonlight SDK and its PPSSPP copy now expose a PCM callback. Tests of the native worker cover the recovered command layout, the unwritten tail after a partial write, and a failed audio command that leaves video and input running.
On November 19, 2026, the console test confirmed sound on the GameShare
client. The working build is tagged switch2-gameshare-guest-audio-working
(commit 50cd977). The exact installed NRO, SD-card package, matching ELF,
SDK archive and build records are saved under
dist-switch2-gameshare/checkpoints/guest-audio-working/. For a console
rollback, reinstall that NRO as
/switch/ppsspp-switch2-gameshare/ppsspp-switch2-gameshare.nro.

PostgreSQL 19: A hands-on tour with runnable browser examples
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
17:08:56 UTC
02:08:56 KST

PostgreSQL 19: less friction, more control
Run the new SQL, inspect the planner, and replicate between real PostgreSQL 19 servers.
A release you can try at the prompt
PostgreSQL 19 brings useful new SQL alongside maintenance and replication improvements. Try IGNORE NULLS, get-or-create inserts, JSON export, JSONPath transformations, and range subtraction, then explore planner advice and real replication.
PostgreSQL 19.0 · released 19 November 2026. The stable release announcement introduces the version installed in this sandbox. Each feature links to its official documentation. The release notes remain the complete change log.
About this terminal. Experiments run on a real PostgreSQL 19 server as student, in a disposable guest. Maintenance tables live in blog_lab. Replication experiments start additional real servers on demand: a logical subscriber on 5433, and a physical standby on 5434, both fed by the primary on 5432. To fit the guest’s memory budget, only one of these extra instances runs at a time: each replication card stops the other and starts its own. Re-run that card before checking its current state again. Their first runs take longer because they initialize a cluster or take a base backup. Reset discards all three clusters and their data.
How to compare with 18. Sidenotes offer older SQL/workflows that you can run on 19, or a setting that disables a particular new optimization. These show the difference in expression or behavior. They do not turn PostgreSQL 19 into PostgreSQL 18. For timing claims, run the same workload on actual 18 and 19 servers with equal data, settings, cache conditions, and hardware.
Run the experiments in any order. Commands beginning with \! run a shell command from psql. The logical replication walkthrough shows its server setup and SQL directly; as-postgres runs server utilities as the Linux postgres account. Query and comparison examples state when to use another port or the administrative SQL role.
Start with the server you actually have
Before reading a release article, ask the server which release you are actually running. SELECT 1 is also a pleasantly boring connectivity test: a result beats a spinning status indicator.
The version returned below belongs to the sandbox, not to the release covered by the article. The SQL role is student; ownership of the prepared demo database gives you room to experiment without administrative credentials.
Look for: one row, a real server version, and the expected role. Keep that baseline when you compare an upgrade later.
From the shell, open the demo database (PGDATABASE=blog_lab):
psql
Then run this at the SQL prompt:
SELECT 1 AS terminal_is_alive,
       current_setting('server_version') AS actual_version,
       current_user AS sql_role;
Expected: One row with terminal_is_alive = 1, the installed server version, and student.
REPACK — rewrite and reclaim space
Deleting rows leaves reusable space behind. Regular vacuum cleans that space; PostgreSQL 19’s native REPACK rewrites the table and returns unused bytes to the OS. The card deletes middle rows, preserving occupied tail pages so ordinary vacuum cannot simply truncate them. Compare the file size after vacuum with its size after a real rewrite. The tiny table demonstrates the operation, not a storage benchmark. REPACK documentation.
The concurrent variant uses logical decoding to capture intervening writes and takes an access-exclusive lock for the final swap. After the card, run this example at the same psql prompt. REPACK requires the table's MAINTAIN privilege; student owns events, so no role change is needed.
REPACK (CONCURRENTLY, ANALYZE) events;
Compare with 18: VACUUM (FULL, ANALYZE) events; performs the older blocking rewrite. For physical index ordering, compare CLUSTER events USING events_pkey; with REPACK events USING INDEX events_pkey;. Restore the same bloated table before each run. Use a second connection to try SELECT/INSERT while a larger rewrite runs, and inspect pg_locks: the concurrent form still locks briefly at the swap. PostgreSQL 18 has no native concurrent equivalent; the separately installed pg_repack extension is another workflow.
DELETE FROM events WHERE id BETWEEN 301 AND 700;VACUUM (ANALYZE) events;SELECT pg_relation_size('events') AS bytes_before_repack;REPACK (ANALYZE) events;SELECT count(*) AS surviving_events,
       pg_relation_size('events') AS bytes_after_repack
FROM events
WHERE id <= 1000;
Expected: DELETE 400 on the first run, VACUUM, REPACK, then 600 original surviving events. Compare bytes before/after; sizes vary.
Autovacuum — inspect the real priority scores
Autovacuum now prioritizes tables using real scores and can parallelize index work. The card reads pg_stat_autovacuum_scores and the configured score weights. Use \x on to show the wide scores view as readable records, then \x off to return to a table for the two-column settings query. Scores change with workload and statistics, so zero after maintenance is a useful result too. Autovacuum scheduling, statistics views.
autovacuum_max_parallel_workers controls the server-wide allowance; autovacuum_parallel_workers is a per-table setting. The sandbox’s current parallel allowance is zero, so the card inspects priorities rather than launching parallel vacuum work. PostgreSQL 19 can also mark eligible pages all-visible during ordinary scans, improving future index-only scans.
Compare with 18: On an administrative connection, set autovacuum_max_parallel_workers = 0 with ALTER SYSTEM, then reload using SELECT pg_reload_conf(); to disable this new parallel index work. Restore the previous value afterward. Manual VACUUM (PARALLEL 0) versus VACUUM (PARALLEL 2) is also runnable on 18, but measures manual vacuum. There is no faithful switch back to PostgreSQL 18’s autovacuum scheduler; compare the scheduling on separate installations.
\x onSELECT *
FROM pg_stat_autovacuum_scores
WHERE relid IN ('events'::regclass, 'orders'::regclass);\x offSELECT name, setting
FROM pg_settings
WHERE name LIKE 'autovacuum%score_weight'
   OR name = 'autovacuum_max_parallel_workers'
ORDER BY name;
Expected: Expanded records for per-table scores, then a two-column table of configured weights; recently vacuumed tables may score zero.
Plan advice — constrain one query, then release it
pg_plan_advice generates a description of planner choices and lets you constrain selected choices. pg_stash_advice stores advice by query identifier for automatic reuse. In this database setup has enabled the advice module for the student’s sessions; on another installation an administrator can use LOAD 'pg_plan_advice' or configure preloading. Plan advice, stored advice.
The first plan emits generated advice; the second requests SEQ_SCAN(orders). Look for matched feedback. Advice restricts the normal planner and can become stale when data changes.
Compare with 18: Run the settings, query, and resets in the same connection:
SET enable_indexscan = off;
SET enable_bitmapscan = off;
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT sum(amount)
FROM orders
WHERE customer_id = 1;
RESET enable_indexscan;
RESET enable_bitmapscan;
These broad settings discourage whole classes of paths. PostgreSQL 19’s advice can instead target a named table in a query. Our small table is likely to choose a sequential scan already; the interesting evidence is the advice and its feedback.
Inspect the generated plan advice.
EXPLAIN (PLAN_ADVICE, COSTS OFF)
SELECT sum(amount)
FROM orders
WHERE customer_id = 1;
Apply session advice, inspect the plan, then reset the setting.
SET pg_plan_advice.advice = 'SEQ_SCAN(orders)';
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS, COSTS OFF)
SELECT sum(amount)
FROM orders
WHERE customer_id = 1;
RESET pg_plan_advice.advice;
Expected: Generated Plan Advice, then Supplied Plan Advice with matched feedback and an Aggregate plan.
Logical replication — see what moves between two servers
Follow one row and one counter from source to destination. The publisher on 5432 owns the source data. The subscriber on 5433 is a separate PostgreSQL process with its own data directory. Both databases are named pg19_repl; the port identifies which one you are changing.
The commands below show the entire setup. Run them in order, or use Run all steps to enter the same visible commands. \! runs a shell command from psql. as-postgres runs the following command as the Linux postgres account; initdb initializes the cluster and pg_ctl starts it. \connect database role host port chooses the SQL connection. Administrative SQL here explicitly uses the SQL role postgres. Local trust authentication is for this disposable guest.
The standby from experiment 06 is stopped first to fit the guest's memory budget. The subscriber uses small buffers and synchronous I/O for this runtime. Its log is /tmp/pg19-subscriber.log. Conditional setup preserves existing objects on repeat runs; each repeat advances the publisher counter again.
What is new in 19? FOR ALL TABLES EXCEPT (TABLE scratch) excludes a table, and ALL SEQUENCES includes sequence state. Initial subscription creation copies sequence values; later publisher calls to nextval can leave the subscriber behind. REFRESH SEQUENCES requests another copy. We wait for the initial copy before advancing the publisher so it cannot mask the before/after comparison. The visible polling loops have a deadline because synchronization is asynchronous. Definitions must match on both servers. Sequence synchronization, publication syntax.
The subscription receives data over loopback TCP; psql uses local Unix sockets for administration. Compare wal_level = replica with effective_wal_level = logical on the publisher after creating the publication: PostgreSQL 19 enables logical WAL when required without restarting this primary. WAL configuration.
Compare with 18: Publish the included table explicitly with FOR TABLE messages. The publisher needs wal_level = logical and a restart. Sequence state is not replicated: at cutover, stop publisher writes, read last_value and is_called, and apply setval('ticket_numbers', captured_value, captured_is_called) on the subscriber. In 19, repeat the steps below and compare the counter before and after REFRESH SEQUENCES.
1 · Make room for the subscriber. Stop the physical standby only if experiment 06 has started it.
\! if as-postgres pg_ctl status -D /tmp/pg19-standby >/dev/null 2>&1; then as-postgres pg_ctl stop -D /tmp/pg19-standby -m fast -w; fi
2 · Initialize a separate cluster. initdb creates its data directory; the condition preserves it on repeat runs.
\! if ! as-postgres test -f /tmp/pg19-subscriber/PG_VERSION; then as-postgres initdb -D /tmp/pg19-subscriber -U postgres --auth=trust --locale=C -E UTF8; fi
3 · Start that cluster on port 5433. pg_ctl waits for startup; the -o options choose the port, sockets, and small memory settings for this guest.
\! as-postgres pg_ctl status -D /tmp/pg19-subscriber || as-postgres pg_ctl start -D /tmp/pg19-subscriber -l /tmp/pg19-subscriber.log -w -o "-p 5433 -h 127.0.0.1 -k /tmp -c dynamic_shared_memory_type=mmap -c shared_buffers=16MB -c max_connections=10 -c io_method=sync"
4 · Publisher, port 5432. Create the source database if absent. psql’s \gexec executes the CREATE DATABASE text only when the SELECT returns it.
\connect postgres postgres /tmp 5432
SELECT 'CREATE DATABASE pg19_repl OWNER student'
WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT FROM pg_database WHERE datname = 'pg19_repl') \gexec
5 · Publisher schema and publication. messages is included, scratch is excluded, and ticket_numbers is explicitly published through ALL SEQUENCES. The existence checks make setup repeatable.
\connect pg19_repl postgres /tmp 5432
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS messages (id integer PRIMARY KEY, body text);
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS scratch (id integer);
CREATE SEQUENCE IF NOT EXISTS ticket_numbers START 1;
SELECT 'CREATE PUBLICATION pg19_pub FOR ALL TABLES EXCEPT (TABLE scratch), ALL SEQUENCES'
WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT FROM pg_publication WHERE pubname = 'pg19_pub') \gexec
SELECT pubname, puballtables, puballsequences FROM pg_publication;
SHOW wal_level;
SHOW effective_wal_level;
6 · Subscriber, port 5433. Create the destination database and matching objects. Logical replication does not create their definitions.
\connect postgres postgres /tmp 5433
SELECT 'CREATE DATABASE pg19_repl'
WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT FROM pg_database WHERE datname = 'pg19_repl') \gexec
\connect pg19_repl postgres /tmp 5433
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS messages (id integer PRIMARY KEY, body text);
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS scratch (id integer);
CREATE SEQUENCE IF NOT EXISTS ticket_numbers START 1;
7 · Subscriber. Subscribe to the publisher on 5432. The connection string is the source; the current psql connection is the destination.
SELECT $sql$CREATE SUBSCRIPTION pg19_sub
  CONNECTION 'host=127.0.0.1 port=5432 user=postgres dbname=pg19_repl'
  PUBLICATION pg19_pub$sql$
WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT FROM pg_subscription WHERE subname = 'pg19_sub') \gexec
8 · Wait for the initial sequence copy before changing the publisher. This visible shell loop checks synchronization metadata, with up to 300 checks, sleeping 0.1 seconds between checks.
\! ready=0; for attempt in $(seq 1 300); do if [ "$(psql -w -X -h /tmp -p 5433 -U postgres -d pg19_repl -At -c "SELECT count(*) FROM pg_subscription_rel WHERE srrelid = 'ticket_numbers'::regclass AND srsublsn IS NOT NULL")" = 1 ]; then ready=1; break; fi; sleep 0.1; done; test "$ready" = 1 && echo 'Initial sequence copy ready' || echo 'Initial sequence copy timed out' >&2
9 · Publisher. Write one included row and one excluded row. Advance the sequence 50 times so the change crosses a sequence WAL batch; record publisher_ticket.
\connect pg19_repl postgres /tmp 5432
INSERT INTO messages VALUES (1, 'hello from the publisher')
ON CONFLICT (id) DO UPDATE SET body = EXCLUDED.body;
INSERT INTO scratch SELECT 1 WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT FROM scratch WHERE id = 1);
SELECT max(n) AS publisher_ticket
FROM (SELECT nextval('ticket_numbers') AS n FROM generate_series(1, 50)) q;
SELECT last_value AS publisher_ticket, is_called FROM ticket_numbers;
10 · Subscriber, before refresh. Its counter can lag even while table changes replicate. Compare subscriber_ticket with the publisher value above.
\connect pg19_repl postgres /tmp 5433
SELECT last_value AS subscriber_ticket_before_refresh, is_called FROM ticket_numbers;
11 · Subscriber. Request sequence synchronization explicitly. ALTER SUBSCRIPTION schedules asynchronous work; it does not wait for the new value.
ALTER SUBSCRIPTION pg19_sub REFRESH SEQUENCES;
12 · Wait for equal sequence values and the included row. The SQL and deadline are visible here; this loop only observes the servers.
\! target=$(psql -w -X -h /tmp -p 5432 -U postgres -d pg19_repl -At -c 'SELECT last_value FROM ticket_numbers'); synced=0; for attempt in $(seq 1 300); do if [ "$(psql -w -X -h /tmp -p 5433 -U postgres -d pg19_repl -At -c "SELECT (last_value = $target AND is_called AND EXISTS (SELECT FROM messages WHERE id = 1 AND body = 'hello from the publisher'))::int FROM ticket_numbers")" = 1 ]; then synced=1; break; fi; sleep 0.1; done; test "$synced" = 1 && echo 'Sequence synchronization confirmed' || echo 'Sequence synchronization timed out' >&2
13 · Subscriber evidence. messages contains the published row, scratch stays empty, and subscriber_ticket matches publisher_ticket. Inspect the synchronization metadata, then return to blog_lab as student.
SELECT * FROM messages;
SELECT count(*) AS excluded_scratch_rows FROM scratch;
SELECT last_value AS subscriber_ticket_after_refresh, is_called FROM ticket_numbers;
SELECT srrelid::regclass, srsubstate, srsublsn
FROM pg_subscription_rel ORDER BY 1;
\connect blog_lab student /tmp 5432
Expected: messages contains 1 | hello from the publisher; excluded_scratch_rows is 0; subscriber_ticket_after_refresh matches publisher_ticket. Configured WAL is replica; effective WAL is logical. A timeout means synchronization has not been confirmed. Values advance on repeat runs.
WAIT — trace a committed write to a physical standby
A write can commit on the primary before a standby can read it. This walkthrough follows an event with ID 2000 from the primary on 5432 to a physical standby on 5434. Unlike the logical subscriber in section 05, a physical standby starts from a backup of the entire cluster and replays WAL to keep that copy current.
Run the steps in order, or use Run all steps. Every server command and SQL statement is shown below. \! executes a shell command from psql; as-postgres runs server utilities as the Linux postgres account. SQL writes and reads use student, and each \connect identifies the destination port. The logical subscriber is stopped first to fit the guest’s memory budget. The backup and its replication slot are reused on repeat runs. Physical standby setup.
The read follows three stages: commit the write → capture its WAL position → wait for standby replay. Capture the LSN after the INSERT has committed. Waiting for WAL to arrive or be flushed does not establish that the rows have been applied. MODE 'standby_replay' waits for that application step; the following SELECT obtains a fresh snapshot. Keep WAIT outside an explicit transaction for this example. WAIT documentation.
Look for: success from WAIT, then is_standby = true, replay_reached_target = true, and the expected row. If WAIT times out, inspect /tmp/pg19-standby.log and retry the wait after resolving the delay. Do not treat a timeout as permission to perform the dependent read. If a failover changes the timeline, establish whether the captured LSN still refers to the expected history.
Compare with 18: Poll SELECT pg_last_wal_replay_lsn() >= :'target_lsn'::pg_lsn; on the standby with a short sleep and an overall deadline. Read only after it returns true. PostgreSQL 19’s WAIT performs that wait inside the server. Optional NO_THROW returns timeout or not in recovery as a status instead of an error; your application must handle that status before reading.
1 · Make room for the physical standby. Connect to the primary before stopping the logical subscriber from experiment 05; only one extra server fits in this guest.
\connect blog_lab student /tmp 5432
\! if as-postgres pg_ctl status -D /tmp/pg19-subscriber >/dev/null 2>&1; then as-postgres pg_ctl stop -D /tmp/pg19-subscriber -m fast -w; fi
2 · Copy the primary cluster. pg_basebackup takes the initial physical backup from port 5432. -R configures standby mode and its upstream connection; -X stream includes WAL generated during the backup. -C -S creates a named replication slot that retains WAL for this standby. The condition reuses the backup on repeat runs.
\! if ! as-postgres test -f /tmp/pg19-standby/PG_VERSION; then as-postgres pg_basebackup -h /tmp -p 5432 -U postgres -D /tmp/pg19-standby -R -X stream -c fast -C -S pg19_standby; fi
3 · Give the copied cluster its own port. It inherits the primary’s configuration, so override port 5432 with 5434. Empty preloaded libraries and disabled log collection keep this extra instance within the guest’s memory budget.
\! as-postgres sh -c "printf '%s\n' 'port = 5434' \"shared_preload_libraries = ''\" 'logging_collector = off' >> /tmp/pg19-standby/postgresql.auto.conf"
4 · Start the standby. pg_ctl starts PostgreSQL from the copied data directory and waits until it accepts connections. A running standby is left running; startup messages go to /tmp/pg19-standby.log.
\! as-postgres pg_ctl status -D /tmp/pg19-standby || as-postgres pg_ctl start -D /tmp/pg19-standby -l /tmp/pg19-standby.log -w
5 · Verify the destination. Connect to blog_lab on 5434 as student. is_standby must be true and port must be 5434. A physical backup includes the database and its tables; no CREATE TABLE is needed here.
\connect blog_lab student /tmp 5434
SELECT pg_is_in_recovery() AS is_standby, current_setting('port') AS port;
6 · Commit a write on the primary. Switch to 5432, enable psql autocommit, and insert an event with ID 2000. The next command runs after this INSERT commits. ON CONFLICT makes the demonstration repeatable.
\connect blog_lab student /tmp 5432
\set AUTOCOMMIT on
INSERT INTO events VALUES (2000, 'replica-visible write')
ON CONFLICT (id) DO UPDATE SET payload = EXCLUDED.payload;
7 · Capture the WAL position after commit. An LSN is a position in the write-ahead log. This position is at or beyond our transaction’s commit record. psql’s \gset saves it as target_lsn; \echo shows the value. The client variable survives switching servers.
SELECT pg_current_wal_insert_lsn() AS target_lsn \gset
\echo Target WAL position: :target_lsn
8 · Wait on the standby for replay. Switch to 5434 and substitute the saved LSN with :'target_lsn'. standby_replay waits until WAL has been applied to the database. TIMEOUT limits the wait to 30 seconds; an error stops Run all steps before the read. A caught-up standby returns success immediately.
\connect blog_lab student /tmp 5434
WAIT FOR LSN :'target_lsn'
WITH (MODE 'standby_replay', TIMEOUT '30s');
9 · Read after WAIT succeeds. This separate SELECT takes a fresh snapshot on the standby. Expect is_standby and replay_reached_target to be true, plus the event with ID 2000 and payload replica-visible write. Finally return to the primary for the next experiment.
SELECT pg_is_in_recovery() AS is_standby,
       pg_last_wal_replay_lsn() >= :'target_lsn'::pg_lsn AS replay_reached_target,
       id, payload
FROM events WHERE id = 2000;
\connect blog_lab student /tmp 5432
Expected: On port 5434: is_standby = true; WAIT returns success; the separate read shows replay_reached_target = true and the event with ID 2000 and payload replica-visible write. Target LSN values vary. A timeout means the standby has not replayed the target yet.
EXPLAIN IO — see the work behind the wait
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, IO) adds asynchronous I/O evidence alongside buffers. Worker I/O can now grow and shrink its worker pool using io_min_workers, io_max_workers, io_worker_idle_timeout, and io_worker_launch_interval. EXPLAIN options, I/O settings.
The sandbox is configured with sync I/O. The card demonstrates the new EXPLAIN syntax; it cannot measure an asynchronous worker pool here. Tiny warm tables also make poor storage benchmarks.
Compare with 18: Run the query once with EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS) and once with the new IO option to inspect the extra reporting. On a native server configured for worker I/O, setting the 19 minimum and maximum worker counts to the same number approximates 18’s fixed worker-count policy; it does not restore the old implementation. Keep a separate actual 18 baseline for throughput comparisons.
SHOW io_method;EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, IO, BUFFERS, COSTS OFF)
SELECT count(*)
FROM events
WHERE length(payload) > 100;
Expected: The guest uses sync I/O. EXPLAIN accepts IO and reports buffers; asynchronous activity may be absent or zero.
Foreign keys — faster inserts, same contract
Most application schemas have relationships. Here, a customer can have zero or many orders, and each order can reference zero or one customer because customer_id is nullable. Every non-NULL customer reference must exist, so foreign-key checking is on the hot path for an ordinary order insert.
The Beta 1 announcement reported up to roughly twice the insert performance for workloads involving foreign-key checks. That is a workload-specific result, not a promise that every INSERT becomes twice as fast.
Insert a demo order, then join it to the customer. The ON CONFLICT clause makes the experiment repeatable. An insert referencing a nonexistent customer would still be rejected: faster checking preserves the constraint's job.
Look for: order 1001 attached to Ada. To evaluate the upgrade, replay realistic batches with the same relationships, transaction sizes, and contention. Do not turn off constraints to make the comparison look better.
INSERT INTO orders VALUES (1001, 1, 42)
ON CONFLICT (id) DO NOTHING;SELECT o.id, c.name, o.amount
FROM orders o
JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id
WHERE o.id = 1001;
Expected: INSERT 0 1 on the first run, then 1001 | Ada | 42.
IGNORE NULLS — carry the last known value forward
lag, lead, first_value, last_value, and nth_value now support IGNORE NULLS. Here, last_value carries the most recent known reading through gaps. The explicit frame ends at the current row so no future observation leaks backward. Window functions.
Compare with 18: A running maximum is wrong when readings decrease. Run this older two-window workaround on the same data and compare its result with the card:
-- PostgreSQL 18 approach: group readings, then fill each group.
WITH islands AS (
  SELECT tick, reading,
         count(reading) OVER (ORDER BY tick ROWS UNBOUNDED PRECEDING) AS grp
  FROM readings
)
SELECT tick, reading, max(reading) OVER (PARTITION BY grp) AS carried
FROM islands ORDER BY tick;
Each non-null value starts a new group. The results match; the 19 expression needs one window rather than the grouping workaround.
-- PostgreSQL 19 approach: carry the latest non-null reading forward.
SELECT tick, reading,
       last_value(reading) IGNORE NULLS OVER (
         ORDER BY tick
         ROWS BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW
       ) AS carried
FROM readings ORDER BY tick;
Expected: Carried values 10, 10, 7, 7, while the original missing readings remain NULL.
Get or create — return the conflict without updating it
ON CONFLICT DO SELECT ... RETURNING implements get-or-create without a dummy update. Run it twice: the second call returns the existing ID and tuple xmin. Optional FOR UPDATE / FOR SHARE can lock the conflicting row. INSERT documentation.
Compare with 18: Replace DO SELECT with DO UPDATE SET handle = EXCLUDED.handle and repeat. It returns the ID, but also creates a new row version and can fire UPDATE triggers. Inspect xmin before/after; under autocommit it changes. DO NOTHING RETURNING returns no row on conflict. The common single-statement CTE fallback can miss a concurrently inserted row under READ COMMITTED; an insert-then-select retry loop is another 18 approach. Sequence gaps can occur even on the new conflict path because defaults are evaluated before resolving the conflict.
INSERT INTO handles(handle) VALUES ('ada')
ON CONFLICT (handle) DO SELECT
RETURNING id, handle, xmin;
Expected: Both calls return the same id and xmin for ada; the second call selects the existing row.
COPY TO JSON — export objects directly
COPY TO now emits JSON directly, either one object per row or a single array with FORCE_ARRAY. SQL NULL becomes JSON null. COPY formats.
Compare with 18: Use SQL functions to produce objects or an array:
-- PostgreSQL 18: one JSON object per row.
SELECT row_to_json(c)
FROM (SELECT id, name FROM customers ORDER BY id) c;
-- PostgreSQL 18: one JSON array.
SELECT json_agg(c ORDER BY id)
FROM (SELECT id, name FROM customers) c;
For a clean output stream in the shell:
psql -X -At -d blog_lab \
  -c 'SELECT row_to_json(c) FROM customers c ORDER BY id'
Compare parsed JSON rather than whitespace. The aggregate constructs an array in memory; COPY provides a direct export path.
COPY (SELECT id, name FROM customers ORDER BY id)
TO STDOUT (FORMAT JSON);COPY (SELECT id, name FROM customers ORDER BY id)
TO STDOUT (FORMAT JSON, FORCE_ARRAY);
Expected: Two JSON objects, then a JSON array containing Ada and Grace.
COPY FROM — keep a row with one malformed field
The prepared CSV contains two preamble/header lines and 2,oops. HEADER 2 skips both lines; ON_ERROR set_null keeps row 2 with its invalid integer replaced by NULL. The card uses client-side \copy so student does not need server-file privileges. COPY error handling.
Compare with 18: Remove the first preamble line in a CSV preprocessing step, use HEADER, and choose ON_ERROR ignore: the bad row is discarded rather than preserved. To keep it on 18, import text into a staging table and convert with:
CASE
  WHEN pg_input_is_valid(reading, 'integer')
  THEN reading::integer
END
Inspect the three rows afterward. set_null handles input conversion failures; CHECK, NOT NULL, foreign-key, or malformed-CSV errors can still abort the import.
\! cat /home/student/pg19-import.csvTRUNCATE imported_readings;\copy imported_readings FROM /home/student/pg19-import.csv WITH (FORMAT CSV, HEADER 2, ON_ERROR set_null)SELECT id, reading,
       reading IS NULL AS repaired
FROM imported_readings
ORDER BY id;
Expected: COPY 3. Row 2 survives with a NULL reading; rows 1 and 3 retain 42 and 7.
COPY a partitioned parent — include every partition
COPY partitioned_parent TO ... now exports its child rows directly. Both partitions in the card contribute a row. COPY relation behavior.
Compare with 18: Use the older query wrapper:
COPY (SELECT * FROM export_events ORDER BY id)
TO STDOUT (FORMAT CSV, HEADER);
It also gives a deterministic order; plain table COPY makes no ordering promise.
COPY export_events TO STDOUT (FORMAT CSV, HEADER);
Expected: The id,label header and both 1,low and 11,high (row order is unspecified).
JSONPath — transform strings inside the path
JSONPath adds lower, upper, initcap, replace, split_part, ltrim, rtrim, and btrim. The card transforms every email into a lowercase handle directly in the path. JSONPath methods.
Compare with 18: Extract to SQL strings and aggregate the result back to JSON:
SELECT jsonb_agg(split_part(lower(email), '@', 1) ORDER BY ord)
FROM jsonb_array_elements_text(
  '["ADA@EXAMPLE.COM","GRACE@EXAMPLE.COM"]'::jsonb
) WITH ORDINALITY AS e(email, ord);
This preserves the input order and matches the string-array example. Mixed-type arrays require explicit handling; JSONPath lax/strict behavior can differ from extracting every element as text.
SELECT jsonb_path_query_array(
  '["ADA@EXAMPLE.COM","GRACE@EXAMPLE.COM"]'::jsonb,
  '$[*].lower().split_part("@", 1)'
) AS handles_from_json;
Expected: The JSON array ["ada", "grace"].
Range subtraction — return both remaining pieces
range_minus_multi returns each non-empty remaining range as a row. multirange_minus_multi returns a non-empty multirange result, or no rows if nothing remains. Useful for availability minus booked time. Range functions.
Compare with 18: Subtract multiranges, then expand the result into rows:
SELECT unnest(
  int4multirange('[0,10)'::int4range)
  - int4multirange('[3,7)'::int4range)
);
This gives the same two pieces. Direct range subtraction with - cannot represent a result split into two disjoint ranges.
SELECT remaining
FROM range_minus_multi(
  '[0,10)'::int4range,
  '[3,7)'::int4range
) AS remaining
ORDER BY remaining;
Expected: Two rows: [0,3) and [7,10).
Random dates and timestamps — generate realistic fixtures
random(min, max) now accepts dates, timestamps, and timestamptz bounds. The card generates five fixtures and checks the date bounds. Random functions.
Compare with 18: Generate a date with date '2026-01-01' + floor(random() * 365)::int, or a timestamp with timestamp '2026-01-01' + random() * interval '1 day'. Keep the same bounds; output samples will differ. These are fixture generators, not cryptographic randomness.
WITH samples AS (
  SELECT random(date '2026-01-01', date '2026-12-31') AS d,
         random(
           timestamp '2026-01-01',
           timestamp '2026-01-02'
         ) AS ts,
         random(
           timestamptz '2026-01-01 00:00+00',
           timestamptz '2026-01-02 00:00+00'
         ) AS tz
  FROM generate_series(1,5)
)
SELECT d, ts, tz,
       d BETWEEN date '2026-01-01' AND date '2026-12-31' AS within_bounds
FROM samples;
Expected: Five varying samples; all within_bounds values are true.
Binary values — UUID casts and URL-friendly encodings
This section demonstrates two PostgreSQL 19 additions:
- UUID conversion: a UUID can be cast directly to bytea, PostgreSQL’s raw byte type, and back. The first query checks its size and verifies that converting it back gives the original UUID.
- Text encoding: base64url and base32hex turn raw bytes into printable strings. The second query starts with the text Hi, converts it to UTF-8 bytes, and encodes those bytes in both formats: SGk in Base64URL and 91KG==== in Base32hex. The trailing = characters are padding, not extra data. Encoding changes how bytes are represented; decoding restores the bytes.
base64url uses - and _ in place of the + and / characters in standard Base64. base32hex uses digits 0–9 followed by letters A–V; with bytewise comparison, this alphabet supports preserving byte ordering. The small example shows the encodings, rather than testing their ordering. Binary encodings, UUID type.
Compare with 18: For UUID bytes use decode(replace(u::text, '-', ''), 'hex'). For a URL token use rtrim(translate(encode(bytes, 'base64'), '+/', '-_'), '='), removing embedded line breaks for longer byte strings. Compare with the native 19 encoder on identical bytes.
1 · Convert a UUID to bytes and back. The sample UUID is named u. u::bytea converts it to raw bytes; octet_length counts them. Casting those bytes back to uuid and comparing with u checks that the original value survives. Expect uuid_bytes = 16 and round_trip = true.
WITH value AS (
  SELECT '123e4567-e89b-12d3-a456-426614174000'::uuid AS u
)
SELECT octet_length(u::bytea) AS uuid_bytes,
       (u::bytea)::uuid = u AS round_trip
FROM value;
2 · Turn bytes into text tokens. convert_to('Hi', 'UTF8') converts the familiar text Hi to its two UTF-8 bytes. Both encode calls represent those same bytes as text: base64url uses an alphabet suitable for URL tokens, while base32hex uses digits 0–9 and letters A–V. Compare the two representations; this example does not use the UUID above.
SELECT encode(convert_to('Hi', 'UTF8'), 'base64url') AS url_token,
       encode(convert_to('Hi', 'UTF8'), 'base32hex') AS ordered_encoding;
Expected: First result: uuid_bytes = 16 and round_trip = true. Second result: Hi becomes url_token = SGk and ordered_encoding = 91KG====. Both encode the same two UTF-8 bytes; the equals signs are Base32hex padding.
Catalog identities — database names and physical tuple addresses
regdatabase resolves a database name to its OID and displays the name again. oid8 is an unsigned 64-bit type; tid_block and tid_offset split a physical tuple identifier without parsing its text. Identifier types, system functions.
Compare with 18: Look up the OID using SELECT oid FROM pg_database WHERE datname = current_database();. Use numeric(20,0) with a bounds CHECK if you need to model unsigned 64-bit values. A ctid can change after updates or rewrites: use it to inspect storage, not as a permanent business identifier.
SELECT current_database()::regdatabase::oid AS database_oid,
       '18446744073709551615'::oid8 AS unsigned_max;SELECT id, ctid,
       tid_block(ctid) AS block_number,
       tid_offset(ctid) AS tuple_offset
FROM customers
ORDER BY id;
Expected: The database OID, the full unsigned 64-bit maximum, and block/offset parts of each ctid.
CHECK constraints — switch enforcement explicitly
Version 19 lets ALTER TABLE ... ALTER CONSTRAINT toggle CHECK enforcement; this previously applied to foreign keys. The card temporarily disables the rule, inserts a negative price, cleans it up, and restores enforcement. Constraint alteration.
Compare with 18: Drop and recreate the CHECK. ADD CONSTRAINT ... NOT VALID is a different operation: it skips validating old rows while still enforcing the rule for new writes. Version 19 also allows changing a virtual generated column’s expression when it has a CHECK constraint; extended statistics can now include virtual generated columns.
TRUNCATE prices;ALTER TABLE prices ALTER CONSTRAINT positive_price NOT ENFORCED;INSERT INTO prices VALUES (-10);SELECT amount FROM prices;DELETE FROM prices WHERE amount <= 0;ALTER TABLE prices ALTER CONSTRAINT positive_price ENFORCED;SELECT conname, conenforced
FROM pg_constraint
WHERE conrelid = 'prices'::regclass;
Expected: A negative amount is accepted while enforcement is off; after cleaning it up, positive_price is enforced again.
NOT IN — find candidates that are not blocked
Find candidates that are not on a blocked list. The prepared candidate_ids table contains IDs 1–1000; blocked_ids contains 1–500. The condition NOT IN excludes those blocked IDs, leaving 501–1000: 500 rows.
PostgreSQL 19 can turn this exclusion into an anti join: keep a candidate only when there is no matching blocked row. Here, both ID columns are primary keys, so neither can contain NULL. That lets the planner use this optimization; its choice of join method still depends on costs. Planner changes.
The first command shows how the query executes. The second shows how many rows it returns.
1 · Inspect how PostgreSQL finds the unblocked IDs. EXPLAIN ANALYZE executes the SELECT and shows its plan. Look for an Anti Join: it keeps candidate rows that have no matching blocked ID. The exact join method depends on planner costs.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, COSTS OFF)
SELECT id
FROM candidate_ids
WHERE id NOT IN (SELECT id FROM blocked_ids);
2 · Count the result. This runs the same exclusion without EXPLAIN and returns the number of unblocked IDs. Expect unblocked = 500, corresponding to IDs 501–1000.
SELECT count(*) AS unblocked
FROM candidate_ids
WHERE id NOT IN (SELECT id FROM blocked_ids);
Expected: First: an execution plan, potentially using an Anti Join. Second: unblocked = 500. Both statements exclude the blocked IDs 1–500.
Compare with PostgreSQL 18 (optional)
On PostgreSQL 18, a common way to express the same exclusion is NOT EXISTS. Read it as “keep this candidate if no blocked row has the same ID”:
-- PostgreSQL 18 approach: exclude candidates with a matching blocked ID.
SELECT c.id
FROM candidate_ids c
WHERE NOT EXISTS (
  SELECT 1
  FROM blocked_ids b
  WHERE b.id = c.id
);
For these primary-key IDs, this returns the same IDs 501–1000. To compare plans, prefix it with EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, COSTS OFF).
If the blocked list can contain NULL, NOT IN and NOT EXISTS can produce different results. The example avoids that difference through its primary-key constraints. Running the older query on 19 compares SQL forms; use an actual PostgreSQL 18 server to compare planner versions.
Eager aggregation — aggregate before the join
Version 19 can aggregate eligible rows before joining, reducing intermediate row volume. The card runs the same aggregate with enable_eager_aggregate on by default, then off in one session. Look for a Partial HashAggregate below the join: hundreds of orders become two customer totals before the join. With eager aggregation off, the join processes the orders before aggregating. Plan choices still depend on statistics and data. Optimizer changes.
Compare with 18: The second command disables this particular new optimization on 19. Run SET, EXPLAIN, and RESET in one connection, as shown below. This is a useful controlled comparison, but every other 19 planner improvement still applies.
Inspect the plan with eager aggregation enabled.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, COSTS OFF)
SELECT c.name, sum(o.amount)
FROM customers c
JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id=c.id
GROUP BY c.name;
Disable eager aggregation for the comparison, then reset it.
SET enable_eager_aggregate = off;
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, COSTS OFF)
SELECT c.name, sum(o.amount)
FROM customers c
JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id=c.id
GROUP BY c.name;
RESET enable_eager_aggregate;
Expected: Two plans for the same result. A small dataset may produce the same shape; the second disables eager aggregation.
Defaults and diagnostics — inspect what changed
Release 19 disables JIT by default, enables lock-wait logging, changes the default TOAST compressor to LZ4 where supported, and lets wal_compression = on select the best available compression method. max_locks_per_transaction changes from 64 to 128 along with a lock allocation change. Ordinary strings always use standard-conforming behavior; the old off mode is removed. The card distinguishes each configured setting from its boot default. This sandbox was built without LZ4 support, so its TOAST default remains pglz. Migration notes.
The new pg_stat_lock and pg_stat_recovery views add operational evidence. A recovery view on the primary may have no interesting activity; after starting the standby, query it there as well. Statistics views.
Compare with 18: For a representative analytical query, compare SET jit = on with SET jit = off in the same native installation; use EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS) and reset afterward. This build may lack LLVM JIT. For newly written TOAST values, compare SET default_toast_compression = 'pglz' with lz4 if supported. Changing the setting does not recompress existing rows. String literals needing escapes should use explicit E'...' syntax.
SELECT name, setting, boot_val, source
FROM pg_settings
WHERE name IN (
  'jit',
  'log_lock_waits',
  'default_toast_compression',
  'wal_compression',
  'max_locks_per_transaction',
  'standard_conforming_strings',
  'password_expiration_warning_threshold',
  'md5_password_warnings'
)
ORDER BY name;SELECT * FROM pg_stat_lock LIMIT 5;SELECT * FROM pg_stat_recovery;
Expected: Configured and built-in defaults plus the new lock/recovery views; recovery counters may be idle on the primary.
What did not make the cut
Earlier beta headlines are not a reliable feature list. SQL/PGQ property graphs were reverted in the Beta 4 announcement. Online checksum toggling is also absent: pg_checksums still requires a cleanly stopped server.
PostgreSQL 19 rejects shorthand GROUP BY ALL without grouping elements. The current SELECT and ALTER TABLE syntax pages also omit the previously advertised inferred GROUP BY ALL, partition SPLIT/MERGE, and FOR PORTION OF DML. Those are excluded from this tour. PostgreSQL 18’s temporal constraints remain available; they are not new 19 DML.
The card reads the current checksum setting and returns editorial removal labels. These labels are not server capability detection. Use the final release documentation when choosing application dependencies.
SHOW data_checksums;SELECT feature, status
FROM (VALUES
  ('online checksum toggling', 'not available in PostgreSQL 19'),
  ('SQL/PGQ property graphs', 'not available in PostgreSQL 19')
) AS release_watch(feature, status);
Expected: Current checksum setting, followed by two explicitly labelled editorial rows.
More changes worth knowing
The runnable cards cover the major SQL and operational additions. The rest of the release matters in specific workloads; use the linked documentation to choose your next experiment.
- More planner and executor work: additional LEFT JOIN to anti-join transformations, Memoize on eligible anti joins, incremental sorts under Append/MergeAppend, and simplification of null/boolean expressions. Faster foreign-key checks, row deformation, sorting, UTF-8 case folding, selective NOTIFY wakeups, SIMD COPY parsing, streaming index cleanup, and parallel TID range scans round out the performance work. Full optimizer/performance list.
- Replication controls: publication exclusions, sequence refresh, subscriber connection parameters via a foreign server, per-subscription receiver timeouts, and retained conflict-detection information (retain_dead_tuples, bounded by max_retention_duration). Review retention overhead and new conflict/error counters. CREATE SUBSCRIPTION, ALTER SUBSCRIPTION.
- Observability: statistics-reset timestamps on more relation/function/conflict views; vacuum/analyze initiators; autovacuum scores; sequence synchronization errors; slot-sync skip reasons; WAL full-page-image bytes; extension installation locations; and dynamic shared-memory allocations. EXPLAIN also reports Memoize estimates. Statistics, EXPLAIN.
- Logging and authentication: separate autoanalyze duration logging, per-process log levels, more COPY/WAL wait events, earlier wraparound warnings, expiring-password warnings, and warnings after MD5 authentication. RADIUS authentication is removed. Server SNI uses pg_hosts.conf; OAuth has custom validator options and improved client hooks/CA configuration. Logging, authentication, libpq.
- Smaller SQL conveniences: error_on_null(value) raises on NULL rather than silently passing it through; IS JSON accepts domains over supported base types; Polish/Esperanto stemmers; PL/Python event triggers; forward foreign-key references inside CREATE SCHEMA; CHECKPOINT options; aggregate support functions; and GRANTED BY for privilege changes. Function additions, CHECKPOINT, GRANT.
- psql 19: show the search path with %S, standby status with %i, customize true/false display, inspect comments on publications/subscriptions/statistics, and use an explicit service file. The client version matters separately from the server version. Try \pset display_true yes and \pset display_false no, then SELECT true, false;. psql reference.
- Administration and extensions: dump/restore extended planner statistics; vacuumdb --dry-run; partitioned-table analysis improvements; tar-WAL support in backup verification/WAL inspection; upgrade improvements for large objects/tablespaces; pgbench --continue-on-error; generic/custom plan counters in pg_stat_statements; foreign statistics import in postgres_fdw; and buffer mapping/dirty-marking tools in pg_buffercache. pg_dump, vacuumdb, postgres_fdw, pg_stat_statements, pg_buffercache.
For extension authors there are new planner hooks, index-handler/API changes, C11 requirements, additional platform support, and shared-memory registration improvements. See the source-code changes.

Teenage Engineering’s first CD player puts the spinning disc on display
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
17:05:52 UTC
02:05:52 KST

Teenage Engineering announced what it describes as “a modern take on a classic CD player” with its new EP/BC–1 featuring a slab-like design that leaves the spinning disc exposed instead of hiding it under a lid or inside a slide-out tray. The company says the EP/BC–1 is the first entry in its new broadcast series of products and was designed to bring “physical audio formats into a modern context, evolved from a shared obligation within the company to preserve the formats they grew up with.”
Teenage Engineering’s first CD player puts the spinning disc on display
The EP/BC–1 can wirelessly stream audio to Bluetooth headphones or multiple devices using Auracast.
The EP/BC-1 is available now through Teenage Engineering’s online store for $299. The CD player joins the company’s existing EP–series of audio devices and has the same dimensions as its KO II synthesizer that launched in 2023 so it can be paired with, and connected to, other Teenage Engineering gear including the recently-announced EP-35 battery-powered amp.
Although it’s designed to play a nearly 45 year old medium, the EP/BC-1 features modern conveniences like variable speed playback, cue markers, a jog wheel for forward and reverse search, and built-in beat detection. There’s a very retro-looking series of digital displays showing track, index, time information, and audio levels, and around the edges you’ll find both digital and analog audio connections including a headphone jack and a USB 2.0 port.
If you want to go completely wireless the CD player has a rechargeable battery and supports both Bluetooth connections to headphones and Auracast streaming to multiple devices that support it. The EP/BC-1 is compatible with standard CDs as well as discs full of MP3 files, and when using the former it can rip tracks to 44.1 Hz/16-bit WAV files. If you want MP3s or other formats, you’ll need to convert them yourself.

Google Workers Tell Their Employer Not to Punish Schools That Don't Want AI
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
16:59:27 UTC
01:59:27 KST

Hundreds of workers at Google are pushing senior leadership to retract a policy that would block schools who turn off its Gemini AI tools from access to the company’s search engine starting next year.
Google made the surprise announcement two weeks ago, on Election Day, with executives saying they wanted to address teachers and parents’ concerns around AI in the classroom. Since then, communities across the country have protested Google’s tight hold on tech in many schools.
As I wrote last week after attending a rally in New York City:
Attendees of all ages held signs reading, “NO AI IN MY SCHOOL!”, “A.I. Gets an F,” “Stop stealing our imaginations,” and “SCHOOL Librarians = THE BEST Search Engines.” Some students handed out fliers to passersby while younger children ran around and drew pictures off to the side.
Many cars driving by honked their horns in support, and a few construction workers on the other side of the one-way street cheered the protesters on.
That day, protests took place in six other cities—Los Angeles; Chicago; Boston; Portland, Oregon; Boulder, Colorado; and Durham, North Carolina—all organized by a nationwide alliance of parent and community advocacy organizations.
School districts across the country use the company’s software and Chromebooks, which were more affordable than Windows and Apple computers when many were quickly assembling remote learning programs during the early years of the Covid pandemic. The Chromebooks have built-in AI classroom tools, and Google Search embeds AI Overview and AI Mode powered by Gemini.
Responding to the backlash, three of the largest school districts in the US—New York City, Los Angeles, and Chicago—are now discussing alternative search engines.
“Any opportunity to drive positive change with AI in education will rely on educators having the freedom to choose how to implement it in their curriculum,” employees wrote in a petition brought by the Alphabet Workers Union, which represents more than 1,000 workers at Google’s parent company. “Cutting off access to Search for schools that don’t wish to use AI will not help persuade our users, and will more likely result in them shutting out Google products altogether.”
“It is our job as Googlers to respect the user, and listen to them when they have concerns about our products,” they continued, addressing Alphabet and Google CEO Sundar Pichai, Google vice president and general manager of education Chris Phillips, and Elizabeth Reid, its vice president and head of search.
A software engineer in Google’s education department told me on Thursday that most workers did not know about the Google Search decision before it was publicly announced. Corporate leadership has now acknowledged the letter but has not responded to any of its specific demands.
AI classroom tools should be “built responsibly and prioritize the best interests of the students, not the company’s bottom line,” the Google engineer said.
Another engineer and union member in New York, who also signed the petition, said to Mother Jones that the executive team “seems convinced that resistance to AI adoption is because people don’t see the vision.”
That goes beyond teachers, parents, and students: the Google staffer said their colleagues are also concerned about how the company is implementing their work on AI tools.
“If the tech is really as useful and desirable as they think it is, why don’t they trust us to choose to use it on our own terms?” the engineer asked.
Google has been contacted for comment.

Anthropic mathematician claims to have solved prestigious Millennium Prize problem with Claude
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
16:34:13 UTC
01:34:13 KST

Anthropic mathematician Levent Alpöge claims he has proved the Riemann hypothesis, one of mathematics’s most notoriously difficult problems, with the help of Claude, cracking a puzzle that has eluded some of the world’s best mathematicians for decades. If verified, it would mark yet another seismic achievement for AI in mathematics and add to the upheaval already unleashed by OpenAI’s previous, often inelegant forays into the field.
Anthropic mathematician claims to have solved prestigious Millennium Prize problem with Claude
An Anthropic mathmetician’s latest breakthrough is another sign of how quickly AI is transforming mathematics.
Alpöge’s solution has yet to be verified independently. Anthropic has not commented or involved itself in Alpöge’s announcement. In a document on his website, Alpöge says he texted Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei about the solution “late Sunday night” and suggests he held off publication until after the company’s IPO on Thursday.
The Riemann hypothesis is one of seven Millennium Prize problems, which are widely considered some of the most formidable open challenges in mathematics. First proposed in 1859, the Riemann hypothesis concerns the distribution of prime numbers and how zeroes of a function are involved. It’s regarded as one of the most important unsolved problems in mathematics.
A statement on Alpöge’s website says Claude was integral to developing the proof:
I believe I have, working with Claude, discovered a proof1 of the Riemann Hypothesis. In fact, the method easily extends much further, resolving the so-called Generalized Riemann Hypothesis, and I expect, with more time, the Extended Riemann Hypothesis too. Claude and I have also formalized the arguments without assumptions. It is natural to feel that this is an end, in my case a loss of a principal guiding light, but what I saw during the course of progress so amazed me that I believe it is one dawn across science of something far greater.
The Verge reached out to Anthropic but didn’t immediately hear back. Anthropic said it was working on the Riemann Hypothesis with Claude in August, but noted at the time: “We don’t expect that the techniques Claude used will lead to proving the Riemann hypothesis.”
Alpöge’s solution, presented in Lean, has only been published on his website and has not yet been publicly accepted by the Clay Mathematics Institute, which administers the Millennium Prize and offers a $1 million bounty for each problem solved. Under the institute’s rules, a proposed solution must be published and withstand at least two years of scrutiny before it can be considered for the prize, during which time it must have “received general acceptance in the global mathematics community.”
In the quarter-century since the prize was established, only one problem has been officially solved: The Poincaré conjecture, a topological problem concerning three-dimensional spheres. In September, OpenAI claimed to have cracked another, the Navier-Stokes problem, which concerns the flow of fluids.
OpenAI said it pursued Navier-Stokes after hearing rumors that rival researchers were closing in on a solution. Alpöge had been working on the problem with New York University mathematics professor Tristan Buckmaster. Buckmaster claimed OpenAI produced a solution for Navier-Stokes using the route he and Alpöge were working on with OpenAI’s Codex and Claude.
OpenAI spent millions of dollars and deployed 10,000 agents in the attempt. The result is widely considered credible, though it has yet to receive formal recognition. But the achievement has been marred by allegations of questionable research practices, including claims it used unpublished work from mathematicians who had shared their research with its AI tools. OpenAI has strongly denied wrongdoing, but this has done little to quell the uproar.
Though most of the spotlight has been on OpenAI’s movement in math, Alpöge said in July that he solved the Jacobean conjecture using Claude. The deluge of findings has left mathematicians scrambling to digest the material and figure out where they belong in what is rapidly becoming a profoundly different mathematical landscape.

Language Models Can Rewrite Their Own Weights
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
16:29:06 UTC
01:29:06 KST

Computer Science > Artificial Intelligence
  [Submitted on 16 Nov 2026 (v1), last revised 18 Nov 2026 (this version, v2)]
Title:Language Models Can Rewrite Their Own Weights
View PDF HTML (experimental)
            Abstract:A language model is trained once and frozen. Making it better takes another training run, or a prompt, a harness or extra computation at inference that is gone once the task ends. We show a third way: to our knowledge for the first time, a language model rewrites its own weights by its own reasoning and becomes better, with no gradients and no training. We call this self-rewriting: the model is its own optimizer. In earlier work, a frontier model edited a much smaller one; here an open 27B model, given its weights, its graded answers and the ability to run itself, diagnoses its own failure and rewrites the weights behind it. It surpasses itself: its own edit makes it 6.6 points more accurate on problems it never saw. It keeps getting better: it tests its edits on practice problems and submits only what works, each edit improving on the last, and over eight consecutive sessions its accuracy only rises. It repairs a damaged copy of itself, like a broken robot that fixes itself without being rebuilt: the copy solves almost nothing, yet with no retraining and without ever reading the original weights, the model brings it back to over 80% of the original accuracy on image tasks it never saw. And the ability emerges with scale: in one model family, the smaller models make no edit or break themselves, and only the largest improves and repairs itself. Unlike test-time scaling, self-rewriting writes its gain into the weights, at no cost per answer; unlike training, it runs only forward passes, as serving does. The wall between training and inference is a limit of our tools, not a property of intelligence. We believe self-rewriting is a necessary step toward recursive self-improvement: a model that, like a human, does not separate training from inference could keep upgrading itself for life, with no larger model, no harness or prompt engineering, and no pre- or post-training.

Title:Language Models Can Rewrite Their Own Weights
View PDF HTML (experimental)
            Abstract:A language model is trained once and frozen. Making it better takes another training run, or a prompt, a harness or extra computation at inference that is gone once the task ends. We show a third way: to our knowledge for the first time, a language model rewrites its own weights by its own reasoning and becomes better, with no gradients and no training. We call this self-rewriting: the model is its own optimizer. In earlier work, a frontier model edited a much smaller one; here an open 27B model, given its weights, its graded answers and the ability to run itself, diagnoses its own failure and rewrites the weights behind it. It surpasses itself: its own edit makes it 6.6 points more accurate on problems it never saw. And the ability emerges with scale: in one model family, the smaller models make no edit or break themselves, and only the largest improves and repairs itself. Unlike test-time scaling, self-rewriting writes its gain into the weights, at no cost per answer; unlike training, it runs only forward passes, as serving does. The wall between training and inference is a limit of our tools, not a property of intelligence. We believe self-rewriting is a necessary step toward recursive self-improvement: a model that, like a human, does not separate training from inference could keep upgrading itself for life, with no larger model, no harness or prompt engineering, and no pre- or post-training.

Boys Noize turns to the club (and an obscure Italian synth) for inspiration on 7LLVSION
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
16:22:15 UTC
01:22:15 KST

If you’re wondering how Alex Ridha’s (aka Boys Noize) collaboration with Nine Inch Nails influenced his new record 7LLVSION, the answer is simple: It didn’t. That’s not to say Ridha didn’t learn anything from the virtuosic production team of Trent Reznor and Atticus Ross, but most of the songs on 7LLVSION had been knocking around for some time. And as Ridha is quick to point out, these are not rock songs built for live performance; they’re “battle tested” for clubs and DJ sets. “I was kind of back on the usual kind of thing,” he tells The Verge.
Boys Noize turns to the club (and an obscure Italian synth) for inspiration on 7LLVSION
‘I always look for the newest machines.’
“I do the same I kind of always do. I go make coffee, go in my room, put on the machines, and I’ll start messing around with sounds and build one sound to another and there comes a record,” he says. Like previous Boys Noize releases, a lot of 7LLVSION starts with sound design. “It’s kind of tech-driven, in a way,” he says, “because I always look for the newest machines.”
On 7LLVSION, Ridha frequently turned to an obscure Italian semimodular synth, the Grp A4. “It’s just so powerful, and it sounds so big,” he says. The routing options, dual filters (which he uses in parallel), and built-in sequencer kept leading him to new and inspiring places. Because of that, the A4 ended up on a number of tracks, including “Shut It Down,” “1+1+1,” and “Ycore.”.
“The exciting part is always to, you know, not repeat myself, even with the drum sounds, and try something new that I haven’t tried before,” Ridha says. For a few tracks, that meant getting away from the rigid, quantized grooves of techno and opting for a more dynamic, live feeling on tracks like “Shut It Down” and “1+1+1” especially. “Live drums are the new 808,” he says.
He looked to German bands of the ’70s and ’80s for inspiration, like Can, Amon Düül, and Deutsch Amerikanische Freundschaft. He also called out Conny Plank, who produced landmark records by Neu!, Kraftwerk, and Eurythmics. But in general he says, “My goal is to not sound like anything you can point to. You know, I don’t want to be a direct copy.” Instead, he was chasing the sound of “electronic punk.” That means aggressive basslines, driving drums, and stripped-down arrangements that avoid overstuffing the mix with too many instruments.
But it all comes back to the club at the end of the day. “Environment has always been the main thing in my music. I need to have a record that I want to play at the party,” he says, and that meant testing the songs off 7LLVSION extensively in his DJ sets and letting the audience tell him what works and what doesn’t. “The club is the most open and objective situation you can have,” he says. “They don’t know what I’m playing, so they hear something for the first time, and you can just look at someone’s face, or someone’s there in the room to get a cool reaction, and that usually makes me feel good about something or not.”
Boys Noize is currently touring the world on the festival circuit, and 7LLVSION is available now on most major streaming services.

Bending Spoons buys Mailchimp for $3.68 billion
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
16:16:06 UTC
01:16:06 KST

Bending Spoons has bought up another distressed internet company. This time, it’s the email marketing firm Mailchimp, bought for $3.68 billion from the tax-preparation giant Intuit.
Intuit acquired the company in 2021 for roughly $12 billion, so it’s taking quite a loss — and, perhaps, Bending Spoons is getting quite a bargain. At the time of the sale to Intuit, that acquisition caused headlines because Mailchimp employees reportedly received no equity, so no share of the sale proceeds, because the founders had allegedly promised them they didn’t need it as they would never sell.
Flash forward to now, with Intuit hit hard by the Death Star to SaaS trade — aka AI — with the company’s shares worth less than half what they were a year ago. The company told Reuters that the sale will let it sharpen its focus on AI tools for small businesses, and the new cash will be used to repurchase shares.
Bending Spoons describes the transaction as an all-cash deal, although that’s not quite true: As part of the same package, Intuit is buying $368 million in newly minted shares of Bending Spoons, so the net effect is a split of 90% cash to 10% stock.
Still, the transaction is a major pickup for Bending Spoons, which has become a kind of clearinghouse for late-stage web properties, with its game plan to hold them long term. In just the last year, the company has snapped up AOL, Eventbrite, Airtable, and Miro. Previous acquisitions include older stalwarts like Evernote, Meetup, and Vimeo. In each case, Bending Spoons integrated the products into centralized engineering and revenue systems, resulting in significant cost savings while often improving the available support and product resources.
“We want to place ourselves as an operator that takes beloved brands and makes them much better,” Bending Spoons co-founder and chief product officer, Matteo Danieli, told TechCrunch in July.
