UK Government Forced To Disclose Secret Apple Backdoor Order
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
16:00:00 UTC
01:00:00 KST

UK Government Forced To Disclose Secret Apple Backdoor Order (techcrunch.com) 29
			
		 	
				An anonymous reader quotes a report from TechCrunch: The U.K. government has been ordered to confirm that it issued a secret order demanding Apple to provide authorities with encrypted customer information. The Thursday ruling from the U.K.'s Investigatory Powers Tribunal, an independent court that allows citizens to challenge government surveillance orders, is a rare rebuke for the government, which sought to keep the encryption backdoor order a state secret. The tribunal's ruling rejected the Home Office's claim that disclosing the order would be damaging to national security. [...] A final hearing on what specific order details can be released is anticipated next year. The order follows a year-long dispute that began when British authorities reportedly sought a backdoor to users' data worldwide, drawing criticism from U.S. lawmakers over privacy concerns. Rather than comply, Apple disabled its Advanced Data Protection feature for U.K. users and challenged the order in court.
The government later narrowed its demand to British nationals' data, prompting another legal challenge from Apple.
Apple said it welcomes the ruling to make public the fact that the U.K. Home Office issued a technical capability notice, reiterating that it has "never built a backdoor or master key" to its products or services and "never will."

A transcription firm used by police, prosecutors and tech giants exposed customer data online
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
15:46:07 UTC
00:46:07 KST

A transcription company that caters to law enforcement, government departments, and Silicon Valley tech giants has resolved a security lapse after leaving a large amount of internal data, including customer transcriptions and their source materials, exposed to the web.
The data security lapse at San Francisco-based Tigerfish exposed transcriptions of police interviews, body-cam footage, and customers’ raw audio and video footage. The exposed data also included invoices of the company’s customers, which include police departments and prosecutors’ offices.
According to Anurag Sen, a good faith security researcher who found the data, the files were located on an Amazon-hosted (S3) cloud server that was openly listed and accessible to anyone via an easy-to-guess web address — no passwords needed.
The security lapse underscores how simple flaws or misconfigurations can result in sensitive information getting exposed online, especially as AI makes it easier to uncover weaknesses that can put companies and their data at risk.
Sen discovered the exposed data online in October and alerted the company to the issue, but did not hear back. Sen later provided details to TechCrunch in an effort to flag the incident with the company for resolution.
Sen, 30, is no stranger to TechCrunch. For the past decade, he has searched for sensitive datasets exposed online with the goal of notifying the companies and organizations that their information is open to the internet. Like many security researchers, much of his efforts are driven by a duty to secure the internet, and he describes his work as an “unpaid air-traffic controller.”
From his island home in Indonesia, Sen has uncovered sensitive U.S. military emails spilling from a compartmentalized Department of Defense cloud server; databases exposing reams of customer records; the inner workings of a country’s national license plate surveillance system; and public systems that inadvertently sent out streams of SMS codes used by people around the world to log into their online accounts.
Given Sen’s propensity for finding often highly confidential information online, we verified the source of the leak and the data, and contacted Tigerfish.
Soon after making contact, Tigerfish secured its exposed S3 cloud bucket.
“We’ve blocked all public access to the bucket you identified, including directory listings and individual files,” Tigerfish president, Adam Goldberg, said in an email. “We’ve also verified that all of our other S3 buckets are blocked to public access.”
“We’re continuing to review and strengthen our storage security more broadly,” added Goldberg.
The company said that Sen’s email disclosing the data leak was caught in its spam filter.
It’s unclear if the company has notified its customers. Tigerfish did not respond to a follow-up email.
“Basic human error”
Discoveries of exposed datasets have consumed a large part of Sen’s early cybersecurity career so far. He scours the web daily using automated scanners and specialist search engines to identify databases, cloud storage, or other sensitive information that likely shouldn’t be left unprotected online.
The modern web runs on absolute trust, he said, and operates under the notion that the world’s data is kept secure. But he described how his findings reveal the reality of how “fragile” the internet is behind the scenes.
Despite the recent focus on how AI is accelerating people’s ability to discover vulnerabilities, Sen said that many of the data leaks and exposures that he finds come down to basic human errors.
“An engineer spinning up a test cloud database on a Friday, disabling authentication to test an API, and forgetting to shut the door before heading into the weekend,” was just one example, he recounted.
Sen’s findings over the years highlight the ongoing problems caused by such security mistakes, just as the new wave of vibe-coding and AI-generated development is creating new security problems due to insecure and unchecked code deployed to the web.
Similar to Amazon’s recent efforts to make it difficult to make S3 buckets public without going through clear multistep warnings, newer vibe-coding platforms like Lovable and Supabase have also made changes to their platforms to prevent user mistakes.
“Everyone often fears shadowy hackers,” Sen said. But he argues that the real crisis is leaving sensitive systems and databases “wide open to the public internet without a password.”

Andrey Zvyagintsev believes filmmakers know what the audience wants — not executives
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
15:45:27 UTC
00:45:27 KST

The year is 2022. Vladimir Putin has announced the military draft. In Andrey Zvyagintsev’s Minotaur, set in the fictional town of Krasoborsk, a quietly unhappy couple, called Gleb and Galina, carry on with their lives, or try to, geopolitics notwithstanding. For Galina, carrying on means an affair, which we deduce from a scene of her texting with a faint smile, her face lit in the dark by the light of her phone. (Minotaur is a remake of Claude Chabrol’s 1969 film The Unfaithful Wife, whose title gives the plot away more readily than Zvyagintsev’s.)
Andrey Zvyagintsev believes filmmakers know what the audience wants — not executives
The director of Minotaur says there is no politics in Russia.
Meanwhile, Gleb runs a kind of private contracting concern and is soon asked to scare up military recruits to meet a quota. At this point, the domestic noir and the workplace drama merge to dramatic, devastating, and darkly funny effects.
In Minotaur, as in his five other features, Zvyagintsev has the camera moving seemingly objectively, almost omnisciently, putting the characters in their context. Occasionally they appear rather small, especially when set against the epic scale of their mythic and biblical fates. That the environment shapes the person seems to be a recurring revelation across all six of his staggering films. Minotaur, too, opens with a natural landscape of the arboreal kind and ends with another, different landscape, of clouds. In the middle is a movie about people trying to make sense of the landscapes they find themselves in, of the social, moral, relational variety.
Zvyagintsev has some experience with finding oneself in incomprehensible landscapes. After receiving Russia’s Sputnik V covid-19 vaccine during the pandemic, he fell ill, and later learned about the invasion of Ukraine while recovering from a medical coma in Germany. He subsequently chose not to return home, moving to France.
At the Cannes Film Festival in May, Minotaur won the second-highest honor. In his acceptance speech, Zvyagintsev spoke about the war, saying: “The only person who can stop this butchery is the President of the Russian Federation. Put an end to this carnage. The whole world is waiting for it.” While he resists the identity of exile, in September the Russian government designated him a “foreign agent.”
Zvyagintsev returned this year to the New York Film Festival, where he last visited with his 2014 film Leviathan, which won a Golden Globe and was nominated for an Oscar. His debut in 2003, The Return, won a top prize at Venice International Film Festival, after which he was invited to meet Putin, an account of which appears in his forthcoming book. Out of his six films, four have won prestigious prizes at Cannes, including Elena and Loveless.
We discussed the three years he spent cleaning the streets of Moscow in the early 1990s, his status as a Russian immigrant in France now, and how he captures that great Chekhovian instinct: that life is wretched, but what can we do, we must go on living. Zvyagintsev spoke to The Verge through a translator from Los Angeles.
I understand you went from cleaning streets to making commercials. What happened in between?
It kind of happened almost at the same time, because I was cleaning the streets to be able to live in Moscow. From 1990 to 1993, I was cleaning the streets. And then I was going to the Museum of Cinema and watching a movie every single day. Those were my formative years, when I learned a lot about cinema.
And then I was let go.
I had been living in a huge communal flat, free accommodation in a beautiful historic mansion in central Moscow. It was a really big apartment, and there were three street cleaners, including me, that lived there. In 1993, I was forced to leave the apartment after someone from the local housing administration decided to take it over. The two others had families, so they decided that if someone was supposed to move, it was going to be me, since I was single.
I was offered alternative accommodation, but realized that the only reason I had continued working as a street sweeper was to keep that particular apartment.
So the first night when I had to leave the apartment, I left it fast without an actual plan. I had to spend the night inside a truck that was abandoned and that was parked in the street.
I got a new job as a guard, as a night guard at a kindergarten where I also ate leftover food from the children’s meals.
After the first day, I called my friend who was working in commercials and doing music videos. I asked him to help with a job, because I was sure I was able to shoot commercials. After the first commercial, I met some producers who liked my work, and then I started shooting commercials on the regular and working as a freelance for some time. One of those commercials was noticed by a producer from a TV channel called REN TV, who was shooting a bunch of TV series at the time. His name was Dmitry Lesnevsky and he asked me whether I wanted to shoot movies, and I said it was my dream. And then Dmitry presented me with 15 scripts of choice, and I shot three episodes of a television series called The Black Room for this channel, each 25 minutes long.
How did you know this friend?
We were army friends. He was a close friend from the army. He was a cinema mechanic. He was starting movies for the soldiers when they were in the army. They had a theater, and I was in the music ensemble in the army. He was also an actor.
You had an adverse reaction to a covid vaccine. You were ill for a long time. You nearly died. You moved, and eventually settled in France. You’ve said you can’t know how that experience changed you as a filmmaker, but I’m curious if you know how that experience changed you as a person.
I got sick in Russia. I was in a coma for 40 days in Germany. I had a major problem with my lungs, a bacteria called klebsiella found its way there. They thought that they would need a lung transplant. Fortunately, the Germans were able to operate, and the transplant was not needed. I moved to France a year later after the coma.
What was the biggest change for me was the rhythm of life. I understood that you cannot lose time, so I’m trying to do things in a much more dynamic manner, and not leave things for later because we don’t know if this later exists. So that that’s the biggest change, really, just seeing life as something that passes by quickly.
You are a vocal opponent of the current Russian government. Do you consider yourself to be in political exile?
I am not in political exile. I did not have that status, and I never applied for it because I don’t see any necessity for that. I consider myself an immigrant. I’ve been living away from my native country for almost five years, but I did not leave on purpose. I was already in Europe when the war happened. There is a term in Russian, called nevozvrashchenets, which means a person who does not come back, someone who has left their home country and has not returned, particularly for political reasons. I was in the clinic in Germany and then in France when the war started, and I realized that I could not go back to the country that is at war.
You’ve said you are not political, but your movies seem overtly political, to me. You’ve also said that the job of an artist is to reflect reality. You don’t think you’re political, but your job is to reflect reality, and we are living at a time when politics is dominant. How do you reconcile all this?
It’s very simple to reconcile. It’s just that the politics barges into our lives, and we have to reflect what’s going on in the world. You become a witness of what’s going on beyond your window, what’s going on to the environment, to the world around you, to the world close to you. It just so happens that the politics dominate our lives. We are witnessing historical events that we cannot not reflect in the movies.
“There is no politics in Russia, in the sense of politics being a struggle for power, dialogue between different parties or worldviews… This does not exist in Russia. There’s only one power”
When I say I’m not a political animal, I mean that I have no way of influencing the political process in my country, especially while the war is going on. There is no politics in Russia, in the sense of politics being a struggle for power, dialogue between different parties or worldviews, you know, left, right, Democrats, Republicans. This does not exist in Russia. There’s only one power.
It feels more like domination in the way Foucault meant it.
Yes. It’s not even that there is only one party that dominates. It’s that no one even questions it, and no one even tries to struggle. It’s kind of a totalitarian regime at this time.
Let’s talk Minotaur. I believe you said you wanted to adapt Ingrid Bergman first, but when that didn’t go, you asked your producers for other available properties and they sent you a list. You also said you asked for marital dramas from the ’60s and ’70s specifically. Why?
I wanted to do a remake of a movie that would have three or four main characters, not a lot of different locations, that would be a little more compact, because there was a certain budget consideration. The films that I had previously considered, the scripts I considered, were more expensive, and so I was not able to get any of them going.
And that’s when I asked for the list from my friend who is French, and he gave me a list of French films. If I had an Italian friend, those would have been Italian movies.
I asked him for the best films from the ’60s and ’70s because it’s far away enough for us, those were probably the films that were a little bit more forgotten by the public, or a new generation of the public maybe hasn’t seen them. And that’s how I discovered Claude Chabrol’s The Unfaithful Wife.
You also said that something about the Chabrol did not work for you, and that’s what gave you the inspiration to want to remake it.
I wouldn’t formulate it as something not working, but I would rather say that he made a kind of a formal choice when it comes to the office life of the main character. Chabrol concentrates more on the crime plot and the love triangle [than the office life], which is totally his right.
And I thought it was something that I would like to fill in. In 2022, the war was going on, and then in September they started the partial mobilization in Russia, and so this plot kind of just barged into our movie, and filled out that space that was empty previously.
Your movies are full of scenes Hollywood executives would ask to cut. I am thinking of the ending of Minotaur, with the clouds, or The Banishment, which ends with women collecting hay and they are singing. What is the effect of these moments in your movies?
It would be impossible to cut out those moments.
What are they doing in the movie?
When you say that Hollywood execs would probably like to cut that stuff out, the viewers, including viewers in Hollywood, really like the clouds [that Minotaur ends on], for example, and they even tell me that the sequence could have been longer. So I want to tell you that the execs do not know what the viewers want because when the viewer goes to the cinema, he kind of goes to sleep and then he watches a dream. And as long as this dream is aesthetically pleasing and harmonious and beautiful, that’s what the viewers should be watching, and no one should cut that out.
This is the key conflict between the producer and the creator. The producer does not trust the creator to know what the audience wants, but the creator knows. And so, if the producer is so sure of himself, he should write a script himself.
You published a book about the making of your third film, Elena. I understand you’re working on a second book. What can you tell us about this project? Will it include advice to young filmmakers?
The whole book, in a way, will be an advice to younger filmmakers, or people who love movies, because it will be a very detailed account of the six films that I have released so far, six conversations with one of the best cinema critics writing in Russian, Anton Dolin.
Would you ever work in another language apart from Russian or with Netflix?
Yes, I am open. In fact, I have a script in English. Two even.

====================================================================================================

Elegance Flows Through Sheena Liam’s Embroideries in Green
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
15:40:00 UTC
00:40:00 KST

Elegance Flows Through Sheena Liam’s Embroideries in Green
Model and ballet dancer Sheena Liam brings her understanding of poise, elegance, and graceful bodily movements to a more fixed medium. For the past several years, the artist has been stitching tender portraits of women with long, three-dimensional braids that cascade off the fabric. Occasionally, a single loose thread appears to have slipped out of its plait as it drifts across a protagonist’s cheek.
Liam’s latest works are on view this month in her solo exhibition, The Unbearable Lightness. Several pieces feature figures sporting the same striped bodysuit with an open back, and the methodical pattern on the garment evokes both a sense of precision and rhythm depicted in the scenes. Dancers grasp hands in a circle, clutch each other at the waist, and quietly stretch in solitude, their movements steady and self-determined.
Created across the last eight years, this body of work extends Liam’s interest in women’s labor and how the slow, careful process of stitching can also be an act of defiance in an increasingly digital, fast-paced world. Each piece begins with an underlying sketch that the artist then covers in thread and typically finishes with sleek braids and quick snips of the remaining loose ends.
The Unbearable Lightness opens today at Item Gallery in Paris and runs through December 19. Follow Liam’s latest on Instagram.

Finite-time blow-up for the Navier–Stokes equations
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
15:34:31 UTC
00:34:31 KST

This repository holds the paper Finite-time blow-up for the Navier–Stokes equations by L. Alpöge and T. Buckmaster and the Lean 4 formalization that accompanies it.
- paper/finite-time-blow-up-navier-stokes.pdf: the paper (79 pages, version of 20 November 2026).
- lean/: the Lean package (Lean v4.33.1 with Mathlib, at the revision pinned in lean/lakefile.toml).
  - lean/README.md explains how the formal statements relate to Theorem 1.1, Theorem 7.1, Corollary 7.2
and Appendix B of the paper, and where the formal proofs differ from the proofs of the paper.
  - lean/NOTICE says in words what each statement file contains and what is proved.
The statement files at the top of lean/ give the definitions and state the theorems with sorry in
place of their proofs; Solution.lean proves them from the library lean/Breakdown/. Each
comparator*.json pairs a statement file with Solution.lean, names the theorems whose statements must agree,
and permits only the axioms propext, Quot.sound and Classical.choice.
To build, from lean/:
lake exe cache getlake build
The library is large (about 2,270 files and 600,000 lines), and a full build needs a great deal of memory and time.
The formalization contains no data. The certificate whose hash the paper gives is not in this repository; the paper says that it will be released. That the certificate satisfies the conditions of Appendix B is verified by a rigorous computation, involving interval arithmetic (128 bits of precision, with the Arb library through FLINT), which is not part of the formalization and is not in this repository either.
The source of the paper is not in this repository.
The package is the one of the paper's working repository, with one patch of 20 November 2026 applied to the
cell-layer statements and to the library proofs that depend on them: the weight of one bound becomes
$(|m|+\tfrac12)^2$ in place of $m^2+\tfrac14$, one constant of a cell may be zero, and the set of the harmonics
of the model in a cell is restricted to those that do not vanish there. Appendix B of the paper states the
conditions in this patched form. The patched package was not rebuilt when this repository was assembled.

====================================================================================================

UN court cites military Facebook posts while finding Myanmar guilty of genocide
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
15:04:34 UTC
00:04:34 KST

In its ruling that Myanmar committed state-sponsored genocide against the Rohingya people in 2016 and 2017, the United Nations’ top court says that Facebook played a role in helping Myanmar’s military spread hate speech against its Muslim minority. The ruling from the International Court of Justice determined that Facebook posts made by the country’s military “dehumanized” the Muslim minority “and at times encouraged and legitimized violence against them,” as reported earlier by Reuters.
UN court cites military Facebook posts while finding Myanmar guilty of genocide
Posts across Facebook ‘dehumanized’ the Rohingya Muslim people, the UN’s top court has found.
The decision that Myanmar committed genocide comes after The Gambia sued the country in 2019 over the military’s violent “clearance operations” targeting the Rohingya Muslims. The military relied heavily on Facebook, which was the most popular platform in Myanmar at the time of the attacks, with a 2022 report from Amnesty International finding that the platform “proactively amplified and promoted content on the Facebook platform which incited violence, hatred, and discrimination against the Rohingya.” Despite numerous warnings from human rights activists, Amnesty International said Facebook failed to stop the spread of hateful content in Myanmar.
The UN is now backing up these findings. It cites a report from the Independent Investigative Mechanism for Myanmar (IIMM), which says that a post made by the military ahead of its August 25th, 2017 massacres on the Rohingya people “unleashed a torrent of public vitriol,” with over 200 comments calling for Rohingya people “to be shot, killed, exterminated or permanently removed from Myanmar.”
Facebook later removed military-linked pages containing thousands of posts with anti-Rohingya hate speech published between July and December 2017, according to the IIMM. The company admitted in 2018 that it didn’t do enough to prevent the incitement of violence in Myanmar, and later introduced a “corporate human rights policy” that includes an annual report about its impact on human rights. The Verge reached out to Meta with a request for comment but didn’t immediately hear back.

Why there are still no non-WebKit browsers on iOS
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
15:03:31 UTC
00:03:31 KST

Why there are still no non-WebKit browsers on iOS
Firefox on iPhone is growing fast across the world. This is especially true in the EU since 2024, when the EU Digital Markets Act (DMA) started requiring Apple to let people choose their default browser; millions have picked Firefox, and daily use on iOS has doubled. People want to choose the browser on their phone.
But there remains an important limitation to what is available on iPhones: Apple forces all browsers on iOS to be built on Apple’s own browser engine.
This has meant that companies like Mozilla cannot easily bring the full range of Firefox features and functionality to iPhone users because many of these are built within our browser engine, Gecko. iPhones and iPads are unique in having this restriction. On all other major operating systems, including Apple’s macOS, people can access the Gecko-powered version of Firefox.
As a result of the DMA, Apple began allowing alternative browser engines on iOS in the EU. It published a set of conditions that companies like Mozilla must comply with to bring their own browser engine. At the time we said Apple’s rules made it “as painful as possible” for alternatives like Gecko. In the meantime, Japan also introduced new rules requiring alternative browser engines on iOS. Apple applied very similar conditions in Japan to those in the EU.
Nearly three years since Apple introduced these conditions in the EU and almost a year since Japan, not one developer has been able to launch a non-WebKit browser on iOS. The reason is simple: Apple’s conditions remain unworkable. The real test of any rule is whether it delivers the change it was meant to achieve in practice. Compliance on paper alone falls short of that test.
Since the DMA came into effect we’ve been working behind the scenes to test and improve Apple’s conditions so that we can bring Gecko to iOS users. Those efforts have so far largely failed.
One of the conditions requires us not to disclose non-public information related to the project, but here’s an example of what we are up against: to use Gecko, we can’t simply update the Firefox app people already use and love. We’d have to build a whole separate app, a second Firefox. Then we’d have to ask every Firefox user to download it, move their bookmarks, passwords and history across, and manually switch over, step by confusing step.
That’s a lot to ask of people who already made their choice. A significant proportion of people would understandably give up, including many who picked Firefox from the EU’s choice screen.
The conditions Apple imposes, including a level of user friction Apple would never accept for its own apps, mean that the open door feels more like a trap door.
Firefox users on iPhone chose an independent browser. They should get everything that comes with it, including Gecko, the last major browser engine not developed by a big tech company. We’ll keep pushing for a real level playing field, not an opening that drops independent browsers through the floor.

====================================================================================================

Cornbread Salad
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
14:30:00 UTC
23:30:00 KST

I love big community gatherings. Anytime there’s a melting pot of people, there’s bound to be really good food. I was born and raised in the South, and I love bringing this Southern-style Cornbread Salad to family reunions, church potlucks, holiday gatherings, and cookouts. It’s a hearty layered side with tender cubes of cornbread, pinto beans, crisp veggies, and sharp cheddar under a generous pour of homemade ranch (though you can add what you like!) It’s easy to make, packed with flavor, and at just $0.92 a serving, it might be the talk of the town at your next gathering!
All recipes are rigorously tested in our Nashville Test Kitchen to ensure they are easy, affordable, and delicious.
A Southern Potluck Favorite
This classic has been around for decades, and like so many recipes, it came out of necessity. Cornbread was often made in large batches, and sometimes the leftovers went into this colorful layered salad. And it still stretches your dollar today! A can of pinto beans, some budget-friendly frozen corn, and a few fresh veggies make this a hearty side that easily feeds ten. I rarely have leftover cornbread (because, well, cornbread), so I usually bake it fresh. For this salad, I adapted our everyday cornbread into a smaller loaf and kept the whole egg.
While the cornbread cools, I chop the veggies and stir together the ranch. I use plain yogurt and lemon juice in the dressing because their tang balances the lightly sweet cornbread. Once the bread is cool, all that’s left is to pile on the toppings and pour the ranch over the cheddar. You can even assemble the whole thing the day before, so the flavors have time to come together, and you have one less dish to make before everyone arrives!
Recipe Success Tips
- Mix the batter just until combined. When making the cornbread from scratch, I mix the dry ingredients together first so the baking powder is evenly distributed, then whisk the wet ingredients in a separate bowl. Once I bring the two together, I stop whisking as soon as the dry streaks disappear. Extra mixing can make the cornbread tough.
- Cool the cornbread completely before cubing. Warm cornbread is more likely to crumble under the knife. Once the loaf has cooled all the way through, the crumb firms up enough to cut into 1-inch cubes. Crumbled cornbread is still delicious here, but those larger pieces hold together better when you scoop through the layers.
- Rinse the beans and drain the corn well. Let the beans drain well in a colander and also pour off any extra liquid from the thawed corn. The less liquid that goes into the bowl, the less there is to soak into the cornbread and make the bottom layer soggy.
- Layering is pretty much up to you! I do recommend always starting with the cubed cornbread on the bottom so the other flavors can meld together. This salad is also typically topped with either the cheese or the dressing, so have fun!
how to make Cornbread Salad step-by-step photos
Gather all your ingredients: Preheat the oven to 425°F and lightly coat a glass loaf pan with nonstick cooking spray.
Mix the dry ingredients: In a medium bowl, stir together ½ cup cornmeal, ½ cup all-purpose flour, ⅛ cup sugar, ½ Tbsp baking powder, and ¼ tsp salt. Stir well so the baking powder is evenly distributed before you add the wet ingredients.
Whisk the wet ingredients: In a separate medium bowl, whisk together ½ cup milk, 1 egg, and ⅛ cup vegetable oil until you no longer see streaks of egg white.
Make the cornbread batter: Pour the wet ingredients into the dry ingredients and whisk just until the dry streaks disappear. A little graininess from the cornmeal is fine. I stop whisking here to keep the cornbread tender.
Pour the batter into the loaf pan: Scrape the batter into the prepared pan and gently spread it into the corners so it bakes in an even layer.
Bake and cool the cornbread: Bake for 20 minutes, or until the edges are golden brown and a toothpick inserted into the center comes out clean. Set the loaf aside to cool completely before you assemble the salad.
Make the homemade ranch: While the cornbread cools, stir together 1 cup mayonnaise, 1 cup plain yogurt, 1 Tbsp lemon juice, ¾ tsp garlic powder, ½ tsp onion powder, 4 Tbsp chopped fresh parsley, 4 chopped green onions, ½ tsp salt, and ½ tsp pepper in a medium bowl. Stir until creamy and evenly flecked with herbs. Set aside the dressing and save the remaining 2 chopped green onions for the topping.
Chop the vegetables: Dice 1 green bell pepper, 1 red onion, and 3 Roma tomatoes into similar bite-sized pieces so they’re easy to scoop together. Keep them in separate piles on the cutting board so you can add each one as its own layer.
Cut the cornbread into cubes: Once the loaf is completely cool, cut it into 1-inch cubes. I like this size because the pieces are big enough to hold together when you scoop, but still easy to eat with a fork!
Start with the cornbread and bell pepper: Spread the cornbread cubes across the bottom of a trifle bowl, then scatter the diced green bell pepper evenly over the top. Bring the pepper right out to the sides of the bowl so that green layer shows through the glass.
Add the pinto beans and red onion: Rinse and thoroughly drain one 15.5 oz. can of pinto beans. Let the beans sit in the colander until they stop dripping so the extra liquid doesn’t soak into the bottom layer. Then spread them over the bell pepper and top with the diced red onion.
Layer on the corn and tomatoes: Drain off any extra liquid from 1½ cups thawed frozen corn and spread it over the red onion. Add the diced Roma tomatoes in an even layer.
Add the cheddar and ranch: Sprinkle 2 cups grated cheddar evenly over the tomatoes, then pour the homemade ranch over the cheese.
Finish with green onions: Sprinkle the remaining 2 chopped green onions over the ranch (I like to garnish with green onions, but fresh parsley, dill, basil, or chives would also be great). Serve and enjoy!
How to Serve Cornbread Salad
I always serve my cornbread salad in a clear trifle bowl so those colorful layers can be the centerpiece of the potluck table. A big serving bowl works just fine, too, or you can layer it in a glass baking dish with a lid if you’re taking it somewhere. For individual servings, try mason jars or clear cups. Set it out with a big spoon so everyone can scoop all the way down to the cornbread, or serve it alongside whatever you’re making for dinner.
If you’re making something that takes a little longer, like slow cooker pulled pork, you can assemble the salad while the pork cooks and pop it in the fridge until it’s time to eat. For a gathering or party, this salad is also right at home next to all the classic potluck favorites. The cool ranch and crisp veggies go so well with a hot scoop of cheesy potato casserole and a spoonful of sweet and tangy baked beans. Add a plate of deviled eggs, and you’ve got a spread worth a second trip to the table!
Storing Leftovers
Cover the bowl with plastic wrap or a lid and refrigerate any leftovers for up to 2-3 days. The cornbread will soften as it soaks up some of the dressing and juices from the vegetables. I actually like the way the flavors come together overnight, but if you prefer firmer cubes, you can store the cornbread separately in an airtight container. You can also prep all the veggies and the ranch ahead, and store them separately until assembly, too. Drain the canned beans and any other canned add-ins well, and pour off any extra liquid from the thawed corn so it doesn’t pool at the bottom of the bowl before layering.
Freezing (just the cornbread!)
The assembled salad is best kept in the fridge since the vegetables can turn watery and the ranch can separate after thawing. To freeze the plain cornbread, let it cool completely, then wrap it tightly in portions and tuck them into a freezer bag or airtight container. Freeze for up to 3 months and thaw at room temperature before cutting it into cubes for the salad.
Try These Bean Salads Next:
- Like to get your potluck prep done early? Our Classic Three Bean Salad gets even better as the kidney beans, cannellini beans, and green beans soak up the sweet and tangy Dijon dressing.
- I’d bring this Cucumber and Black Bean Salad to a party with a bag of tortilla chips for scooping. The crisp cucumbers and tangy vinaigrette make it a refreshing side, too.
- A couple of cans of cannellini beans are the start of our easy White Bean Salad. Toss them with red onion, fresh parsley, and a lemon-Dijon vinaigrette for a side you can make in 15 minutes.

Llms.txt and MCP didn't move the needle
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
14:00:23 UTC
23:00:23 KST

Two years ago, an admin panel for a simple website took me a day, maybe two - and I was proud of how fast I was. Now it takes me about...
Two years ago, an admin panel for a simple website took me a day, maybe two - and I was proud of how fast I was. Now it takes me about an hour or two.
The old day went like this. Install the package, generate the CRUDs, configure every field and column, wire up the relationships, click through everything to check it works. Thirty to sixty minutes per CRUD, easily. Now I describe what I want, the agent runs our generators, writes the configuration, writes the tests, and I review. A day of work fits in an hour. A week-long feature fits in a day.
I'm not the only one seeing this. In February 2026, a customer from New Zealand gave us a 10 out of 10 in our feedback form. The whole review was one line:
"Straightforward to use (particularly with Claude looking over my shoulder!)"
That's the new normal. The developer isn't alone with the docs anymore. There's an agent next to them that already knows the package.
I maintain Backpack for Laravel, an admin panel package that's been around for 10 years. We did a bunch of things to make the AI agents better at writing admin panels with Backpack - it's a quick-moving niche. But when I looked at why AI agents are so good at it, the answer surprised me. Most of it isn't the AI-specific stuff we built. Two of the things everyone tells you to add, llms.txt and an MCP server, barely helped us at all.
Here's what did help, so you can do the same for your package.
Let's get these out of the way first, because most "make your package AI-ready" advice starts with them.
llms.txt. We shipped one. I don't think it does much. Agents rarely go looking for it. When they do, they find a table of contents for docs they could already read. Thinking of removing it, tbh.
An MCP server. We built one so agents could query our docs. The results were mixed at best. The agent has to decide to call it, and it usually doesn't. Then the queries some of the AIs make are... laughable. Of course our MCP doesn't return anything useful, your AI just typed two words - absurd! So no, I don't think our MCP is helpul any more - thinking of removing it too, tbh. An MCP server is a great fit for live data the agent can't get any other way - or tool use. Static documentation isn't that.
The lesson: anything the agent has to remember to go fetch is weak. What works is what's already in front of it, or already in its head.
Our biggest wins came from decisions we made years before anyone used AI to write code.
Backpack's core API has barely changed in the last four years. Even v8, which we're working on right now, started with maintenance: bumping and removing dependencies, fixing inconsistencies. The way you define a field or a column stays the same. We did that to make upgrades painless for old projects. The side effect: years of Backpack code ended up in model training data, and all of it is still correct.
When an agent writes CRUD::column('name'), it isn't searching or guessing. It already knows. Every rename you ship splits what the model learned into "old way" and "new way", and it will keep suggesting the old one for years.
There is a downside. Models sometimes write patterns from much older versions. We fix that with in-project instructions (more on that below), not by hoping the model catches up.
For your package: deprecate instead of rename. Keep old signatures working. Treat every breaking change as costing you twice: once for your users, once for every agent that learned the old API.
Our core, backpack/crud, is open source. That means ten years of GitHub issues, pull requests, tutorials, Stack Overflow answers and blog posts. All of it is training data. All of it is searchable when an agent does go looking.
You can't buy that. But if you're deciding between open source and closed, this is a new argument for open.
This is the one I think matters most for commercial packages. Composer installs everything into vendor/, including our paid add-ons. No obfuscation, no remote API. An agent can open the actual source of any feature, free or paid, and see exactly how it works.
We've always kept that code simple, commented and predictably organized, for humans. It turns out that's exactly what agents need too. In practice, reading vendor/ is how agents answer most of their own questions about Backpack. They do it without being told.
For your package: if you sell code, ship it readable. Obfuscated or SaaS-only features are invisible to the agent writing your customer's code.
Backpack has always been loose on purpose. You can override almost anything with a closure or a small tweak, without creating classes, registering providers or extending five things.
That turns out to be ideal for agents. Need a computed column? One line:
CRUD::column('full_name')->value(fn ($entry) => $entry->first_name.' '.$entry->last_name);
No custom column class, no view, no binding. Small changes stay in one file. That means less context for the agent to load, and fewer places for it to get things wrong. A strict, heavily abstracted architecture forces the agent to understand a lot before it can change a little.
For your package: for every extension point, ask how many files it takes to customize. If the answer is more than one, add a shortcut.
We added a Laravel Boost plugin for Backpack. Run php artisan boost:install, tick backpack/crud, and your agent gets our guidelines and skills straight in its context: which features exist, how to use them properly, which version-specific patterns to follow. The skill is split by topic (fields, columns, filters, operations, testing and so on), so the agent loads only the part it needs. We wrote about it when v7.1 shipped.
This worked where MCP didn't, for one reason: the agent doesn't have to decide to fetch it. It's already in context when the work starts. It also fixes the training-data downside above, because the instructions match the version actually installed.
For your package: if you're in the Laravel ecosystem, ship Boost guidelines. If you're not, ship the equivalent: a short, version-matched instructions file your users can drop into their agent's context.
php artisan backpack:crud Product creates the model, request, controller, route and menu item in one go. Then php artisan backpack:tests (from our backpack/test-generators add-on) writes a feature test for every CrudController, in PHPUnit or Pest, and backpack:tests:status shows which controllers still have no coverage. There are smaller ones too: backpack:field, backpack:column, backpack:filter, backpack:button, backpack:crud-operation.
This matters more with agents than without. A command gives the same correct result every time. Boilerplate the agent writes by hand is a fresh chance to make a small mistake in every file. The agent's time is better spent on what's actually specific to the project.
For your package: if users repeatedly write the same scaffolding, turn it into a command. Agents use CLI tools happily and reliably.
When names follow a pattern, agents guess right. A text field has a text column. A select2 field has a select2_multiple sibling, so select2_from_ajax has a select2_from_ajax_multiple one too. A date_picker field pairs with a date_picker column. The agent has seen one, so it correctly guesses the rest, without opening a single file.
Every inconsistency is a spot where the agent guesses wrong and you pay for it in review.
For your package: make things guessable. Same name for the same concept everywhere, same argument order, same patterns.
Our docs sit behind Cloudflare, and we turned on the feature that serves Markdown instead of HTML when an agent asks for it. Agents read our docs faster and with less noise. It's a small win, and a free one if you're already on Cloudflare.
In rough order of impact:
None of our biggest wins were clever. Stability, open code, simple architecture and good generators were all things we did for human developers. They just happen to be what AI agents need most too. The AI-specific additions only helped when they put the right information in front of the agent, without it having to ask.
One more thing, on the user side: use the best model you can get. The gap between today's frontier models and the ones on top six months ago is huge. It shows most on exactly this kind of work.
If you use Backpack with AI: how much faster are you building admin panels now? One hour instead of ten? A day instead of a week? Tell me in the comments. I'd love real numbers to compare against mine.
Subscribe to our "Article Digest". We'll send you a list of the new articles, every week, month or quarter - your choice.
What do you think about this?

Global stock markets slump as Anthropic's record IPO fails to stem AI sell-off
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
13:29:59 UTC
22:29:59 KST

As Anthropic’s stock dropped during its second day of trading, the wider stock market dipped with it. On Friday, the Nasdaq Composite erased all its gains for 2026 and closed down 5% from its value on 1 January. The Dow Jones Industrial Average and S&P 500 closed in correction territory.
Anthropic’s record-breaking IPO failed to stem a global sell-off in AI-linked companies, and the tech-heavy Nasdaq erased some $4tn in market value this week, according to the Wall Street Journal.
Shares in the frontier AI company behind the Claude chatbot, which floated in the US on Thursday, fell by 9% on Friday, ceding most of the gains from the biggest initial public offering in history. They dropped to $88.30, towards the $83 level at which Anthropic priced its stock market debut.
Anthropic’s first two days as a listed company have been volatile. Its shares jumped by 17% on Thursday, after briefly surging by almost 40% when trading in the firm began.
US technology stocks followed Anthropic down on Friday, with the memory chipmaker SanDisk falling 14% and rival Micron down 12%. Nvidia fell 14%, eliminating hundreds of billions of dollars in market cap.
Neil Wilson, a UK investor strategist at Saxo, said there was “just relentless selling in tech”. He warned: “Anthropic shares tumbled to take an $85 handle as the post-IPO euphoria gives way to the reality of a steep sell-off across the tech and AI space that shows no sign of easing just yet.”
Global markets slumped on Friday too; Asian stock markets recorded their worst week since 2020, as shares in big chip companies fell. In Taiwan, stocks dropped 6.7% to hit their lowest point since mid-April, with the chip maker TSMC down about 6%.
The scale of the decline in Taiwan prompted the chair of its stock exchange to pledge to ensure the stable operation of capital markets, and reassure investors they should remain confident in the fundamentals of Taiwanese companies.
In Japan, the Nikkei index fell 5%, while South Korea’s tech-heavy Kospi index lost 5.5%. Shares in the tech investor SoftBank had their worst day in more than two years, down 14.5% in Tokyo, amid worries around its aggressive bets on AI.
European stock markets posted their biggest weekly loss since March 2025, with the pan-European Stoxx 600 dropping 1.9% on Friday. London’s blue-chip stock index hit a five-month low.
The sell-off followed losses on Wall Street on Thursday, where the main indices all fell by more than 2% on a day dominated by Anthropic’s IPO.
Chris Beauchamp, the chief market analyst at the investing and trading platform IG, said Anthropic could be “draining market liquidity”, as investors sell shares in other companies to raise funds to pay for stock in the newly floated AI firm.
“The slide from yesterday’s highs sends a message that there might be more at work here than investors realise, and while brokers are scrambling to buy more, that liquidity has to come from somewhere, and the other tech names may be the first ones to feel the pressure,” Beauchamp said.
Anthropic’s flotation has failed to ease worries around the AI trade, as investors increasingly question whether companies can deliver the returns necessary to justify the billions of dollars being poured into the AI buildout.
Daniela Hathorn, a senior market analyst at Capital.com, said: “The IPO demonstrated that demand for AI exposure remains strong, but it has not been enough to overcome concerns about elevated valuations, profitability and the sustainability of investment spending.
“The broader market mood has shifted from buying the dip towards questioning whether the correction has further to run. The weakness in equal-weighted indices suggests that selling pressure is no longer confined to a handful of technology giants, while Europe’s recent outperformance has also faded.”
Nvidia shares fell byafter California served the chip company with a subpoena as part of an investigation into whether its advanced semiconductors ended up in China.
Britain’s FTSE 100 share index dropped by 5.1% this week, its biggest weekly fall since the start of the Iran war.
Fears that US central bankers may raise interest rates to fight inflation have lifted the dollar to a 19-month high, and pushed down the gold price – with gold on track for its worst week since March.
Bitcoin hit its lowest level in four months, dropping below the $60,000 point for the first time since early July.

Behind Jensen Huang’s exit, questions about a management style reportedly built on fear
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
13:25:00 UTC
22:25:00 KST

Earlier this week at the DealBook conference in New York, Elon Musk, beamed in from a trip to China with President Donald Trump, was asked about the ouster of his longtime friend, former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang. Musk compared it to kicking Michael Jordan off the Chicago Bulls’ roster during his historic run. Of Nvidia’s employees, he said, “They love [Huang]. They love him.”
Well, maybe not all of them. Thousands of employees have signed a letter asking for Huang’s return, but others tell The New York Times he ruled through praise and fear, as well as by humiliating people.
According to the Times, Huang told subordinates that Nvidia’s wealth meant it wasn’t possible to motivate employees with money, so humiliation was the only lever left. He reportedly told one executive his mother should have aborted him and told another employee he would rip his arms off and beat him to death with them.
Naturally, over a 33-year career, regrettable things may have been said, but the Times found a long paper trail that suggests the incidents weren’t isolated. In 2005, for example, Michael Winston, a former HR chief, threatened to sue and called Huang’s conduct the most abusive he’d seen. Nvidia paid him more than $1 million. Huang’s spokesman calls the allegations fabrications.

💧 Robots Are Ruining My Inbox
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
13:21:46 UTC
22:21:46 KST

Believe it or not, there was a time when I found email to be a delight. Messages were infrequent, and there was an extremely high signal-to-noise ratio, with notes from friends, family, and businesses I cared about. Though spam grew to be a problem in the 2000s, paid spam filtering rapidly got quite good. The weekly chore of checking spam quarantines felt like an acceptable tradeoff for all the positives of email. Until very recently, nearly everything that reached my inbox was valid.
A.I., however, has shattered that peace. Every day, I’m now assailed with dozens of nonsense emails. These messages pitch me on reviewing new A.I-created software, taking out a line of credit, or hiring a company for a service I do not need, such as catering or dumpsters. It’s all very stupid, and I’m certain that A.I. has supercharged the problem.
It used to be that a relatively long, well-written email was probably worth a look, because it required effort to create. Now, however, A.I. can crap out a valid-looking message in seconds, and spend another few seconds scouring the web to gather targeted leads. The end result is that my spam filtering systems are getting overwhelmed.
Here’s a recent favorite, which I’ve lightly censored:
This is, of course, an utterly absurd request to make of a complete stranger. Even setting that aside, however, this is just so poorly done. I try to be generous, but Alexander No-Last-Name-Given didn’t give even the weakest of reasons why I might give him a gift. He also didn’t provide a location for this purported wedding, nor even mention his supposed spouse. Actually, the way the second sentence reads, it’s possible Alexander is intended to marry me. Won’t everyone be surprised when I fail to show?
I was amused when I first received this, and I’ve laughed several times as I shared it with others. Until I was writing this post, however, I was undecided as to the exact nature of the ploy. Is this a real-but-ridiculous request from a real dumb person, or is the entire thing a lie? I recently settled on the latter explanation, after checking next year’s calendar:
Yes, July 27, 2027 is a Tuesday. A Tuesday wedding! You’ve got to do better than that.

California AG subpoenas Nvidia over worries about Huang’s ties with China
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
13:21:11 UTC
22:21:11 KST

California’s Attorney General Rob Bonta on Thursday evening said the state has served an investigative subpoena to Nvidia following news of former CEO Jensen Huang’s affair with a woman who the U.S. government believes is a Chinese spy.
The state is investigating the company to find out if its advanced GPUs or confidential information may have been shared with China, and if the company had somehow misled its investors and the public.
“The conduct of Nvidia and its former CEO raises bright red flags, including about the ways in which the company may have misled investors,” Bonta said in a statement.
“My office is investigating whether Nvidia or Jensen Huang’s actions resulted in China obtaining advanced chips or confidential information — and if Nvidia misled investors and the public about this risk, in violation of the law,” he added.
The investigation comes in the wake of a messy couple of weeks for Nvidia, which has overnight come under intense scrutiny around the world due to the implications of Huang’s alleged relationship with the Chinese national, Alice Huang (who is not related to Jensen).
The Nvidia founder resigned last week after further revelations of extramarital affairs with the company’s employees. And while many tech voices have been petitioning Nvidia to bring back Huang as CEO, saying they see him as indispensable to the further development of the AI industry, the company’s new chair, Harvey Jones, said earlier this week that Nvidia had no intention of reinstating Huang to his position.
The company’s board has hired a law firm as well as an independent slate of directors to investigate the extent of the damage, and the U.S. government is trying to find out the extent of Alice Huang’s ties with the Chinese government.
The U.S. Congress is also gearing up to subpoena Huang, seeking questions about the affair, and the SEC is said to be launching a civil probe into the company, trying to find out if Nvidia’s public disclosures could have misled investors.
Democratic representatives have also opened an inquiry into the Trump administration’s management of Nvidia’s chip sales to China. In a scathing letter to Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, Democrats last week asked the Commerce Department to preserve records about an arrangement that they called questionable and illegal.
“Nvidia is in a messy, transnational situation but the underlying principle of our corporate securities law is clear: Companies cannot mislead their investors,” Bonta wrote.
Huang has agreed to testify before Congress after several senior lawmakers threatened to subpoena him.

====================================================================================================

If you stop writing code, you also get worse at reviewing it
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
12:41:38 UTC
21:41:38 KST

Whatever skill you’re learning, it won’t take long before someone with experience in that particular field tells you to get your hands dirty. It’s fine to read “Born to Run”, but you won’t be running an ultra marathon any time soon if you don’t start running. Whether it’s muscle memory, technique, physical fitness or problem solving you need to work on to reach your goals, you need to practice. This isn’t a controversial statement in any field at all, and has never really been.
Except in software engineering, somehow.
When it comes to software engineering, we somehow lost our way, thinking that our skills won’t stop improving if we stop practicing. Making such a claim in any other field would be sneered at at best. Imagine John Scofield1 saying he stopped practicing his guitar because “he was at a good enough level, musically, to maintain (and even improve!) his expertise” simply by 1) instructing others on how to play (either in vibe general terms, or with a strict spec musical notation), and then 2) listening to what they played and iterating until it sounded good enough for his next record. “My music is better”, he would claim, and moreover “actually playing the guitar was never the point anyway, there’s more to making music.”
Mind you, my point in this post is not to discuss the short term quality of code output (or recorded guitar playing, to continue the proverbial thread), but rather the skill of the craftsman practicing his craft vs the one who doesn’t practice at all.
To make another comparison that’s arguably closer to software engineering, or at least the “writing and reasoning about code” part of it, let’s look at authors in other fields. I have no doubt that reading great prose has the potential to nudge a writer towards better writing of his/her own; in fact it’s super important to have creative references to draw upon - no doubt at all. But, imagine an author who 1) stopped writing, and only pursued reading and giving feedback to other writers, and worse yet 2) mostly read AI generated prose!? To be perfectly honest, I would expect a quite steady (if not exponential) decline of this author’s writing skills.
“There’s more to software engineering than writing code” Link to heading
Sure. But if we can at least agree that the code that runs our software is a non-trivial part of it (though, granted, not all of it by far: The other parts of developing and maintaining software speak even louder against using generative AI as a significant tool service!), we should aspire to be proficient in understanding how code works, what good code looks like, what to watch out for and how software architecture plays a vital part in making code Easy To Change.
Simply “reviewing all the code Claude produced” won’t quite cut it. And who are you trying to fool, after all; at this point you’re not really reading half of it, are you?
So I propose something that used to be a no-brainer: Keep practicing!
Which brings me to my next point:
All engineers(!) need to be good at solving problems Link to heading
if || while you can argue that the need for being hands-on on your software’s code varies, I propose you can’t argue that we’re still solving problems. And the moment you stop practicing solving problems is the moment you stop getting better at it, just like everything else. Simple as that. This is hardly new, btw: you could always tell who beat Doom using cheat codes and who finished the game the real way! If the end goal is to “beat the game”, then by all means cheat; if the end goal is getting good at it, then I’d advice you not to. Some lessons are only learned the hard way (trial and error), and very few are learned without effort at all. Repeated LLM usage for problem solving pounds the following premise into our brain’s wiring: If something is hard, tricky or requires effort, you shouldn’t even try. And right there goes our learning, craft and joy, right out the window.
In a world where people are using AI to summarise books instead of reading them and turning to AI for therapy, I don’t want to keep using it. It feels like such a slippery slope. As literacy and numeracy rates decline, I can’t help but question whether encouraging us to rely on AI so heavily is a good idea.
It makes me wonder: are we still thinking for ourselves at all?
— Em’s blog2, emphasis mine.
Please, John Scofield, don’t stop playing your guitar!
- ↩︎
- I’m not sure who Em is, but their blog is great! ↩︎

Signal is blocking Gemini and Siri AI from reading your screen
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
12:30:47 UTC
21:30:47 KST

Signal is restricting the ability to take screenshots on Android and iOS devices in an effort to prevent AI agents from reading users’ messages and other data. The agentic AI capabilities built into operating systems that analyze on-screen information to learn more about the user are “undermining Signal’s ability to independently guarantee privacy,” Signal president Meredith Whittaker said in a blog post, while urging OS providers to make it easier for app developers to control what data agents can access.
Signal is blocking Gemini and Siri AI from reading your screen
Signal will now restrict screenshots on mobile devices, and says AI agents are ‘hostile to privacy.’
“We want to be very clear: it is a serious problem that Signal – and application developers beyond – lack robust tools to prevent AI agents from undermining application-level privacy,” Whittaker said. “Even where we are able to detect and block AI data access, Google, Apple, Microsoft, and others do not provide ways for developers to easily restrict such AI data access without making drastic compromises to the core user experience. This is also the reason we are currently unable to implement these protections on macOS.”
Signal is addressing the privacy concerns by blocking the ability to take screenshots in the app on iOS 27 and Android, which should prevent Siri, Gemini, and other Android AI assistants from reading on-screen content. Users can restore screenshot capabilities in Signal’s app settings, but Whittaker warns that doing so will “potentially expose your (and your contacts’) Signal content to these AI agents, and break our privacy guarantees.” Whittaker admits that Signal’s screenshot-blocking remedy is “incomplete,” as it doesn’t address alternative ways that AI agents can access app data, such as retrieving it via the device file system.
Signal took a similar approach last year to prevent Microsoft’s controversial Recall feature from capturing secured chats. The encrypted messaging service says it’s struggling to develop a better solution due to the lack of available information regarding how AI agents are actually accessing application data, and is now calling on Apple, Google, and Microsoft to provide “simple technical tools and accurate documentation” to app developers. Signal also says more tools are needed to prevent third-party agents, like Meta’s Muse, from using deep accessibility settings in apps like a “skeleton key” to access application data.
“We all need to recognize that the current integration of AI agents into the operating system, without these common-sense remedies, represents an existential threat to privacy at the application layer, and the ability of independent developers like Signal to protect your sensitive data,” said Whittaker. “Signal is the canary in the coal mine, but this affects the entire ecosystem and should galvanize anyone who cares about these fundamental rights.”

====================================================================================================

Epic Games Turns to AI Coding for the Unreal Engine
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
12:16:36 UTC
21:16:36 KST

When Travis McGeathy’s studio, Seeker Entertainment, ran out of funding in September, its game KinForge went down with it. KinForge was an online game built in Unreal Engine 5.7 and designed to run on servers hosted by Amazon Web Services (AWS). With no one left to pay the server bill, the game became unplayable even for those few developers, including studio co-founder McGeathy, who had the code.
Then, on a whim, McGeathy pointed Anthropic’s Claude Code at the project and asked it to make KinForge playable without AWS. After four hours of mostly hands-off work he had a build that let players host their own games. Claude added support for peer-to-peer play over both the Internet and a local area network. To McGeathy’s surprise, it also added persistent save states, a feature that lets players resume a game if they’re disconnected.
Although KinForge is still built in Unreal Engine, developments like this could give companies that create game engines cause for concern. The conversion was accomplished with an agentic AI workflow that partially bypasses the elaborate, user-friendly game engine UI that many companies, including Unreal Engine creator Epic Games, have spent decades perfecting. Anthropic’s Fable 5.2, released November 9, further upped the ante. The model is capable of generating a 3D game with a custom game engine in the style of the hit Call of Duty series.
This raises a question: can game engines evolve quickly enough to keep pace with model improvements?
“We’re definitely in a world where I can just dream up software really fast. So, if Unreal Engine was static, what’s the value at the end of next year? This is why we are pivoting hard towards velocity,” says Daniel Vogel, chief operating officer at Epic Games. “I think with any fixed set of features or functionality, it can eventually be replicated. If we’re doing our job right, we’re pushing the engine and pushing the envelope forward.”
From Code to Nodes, and Back Again
Major game engines often offer developers new visual tools and workflows that help them build games while interacting with less code. Unreal Engine’s Blueprints, a graphically based system introduced in Unreal Engine 4 that lets designers script gameplay simply by wiring boxes together, is an example. Clair Obscur: Expedition 33, an award-winning game from French studio Sandfall Interactive, leaned heavily on Blueprints for scripting gameplay encounters.
That should have been proof of the feature’s success. Instead, Epic will remove Blueprints in Unreal Engine 6 (UE6). Why? Blame LLMs. “AI doesn’t handle Blueprints very well,” says McGeathy. “It would much rather work with the code.”
Currently, LLMs interact with Unreal Engine primarily through a Model Context Protocol server. MCP is an open-source standard created by Anthropic that lets AI send commands to software. “It’s not actually moving the mouse,” says Ryan Barker, a gameplay and systems engineer who has worked on projects for Amazon Games and Sony Online Entertainment. “It’s sending signals to the editor that the editor understands.”
UE6 will lean into that reality by moving all code to Verse, a text-based programming language that will replace both Blueprints and Unreal Engine 5’s C++ gameplay framework. UE6 will also replace Unreal’s actor framework, which is based on object-oriented programming, with Scene Graph, which resembles an entity component system. “Typically, an ECS is a lot harder to code around,” says Barker, but ECS can provide superior game performance and result in less duplicate code. An LLM can help manage the complexity.
Verse debuted in Unreal Editor for Fortnite in 2023, while Scene Graph arrived in 2024. Verse was designed to let millions of creators run code side by side in Fortnite, so it’s strict. The compiler catches mistakes before code runs and limits what each function can do. And because Scene Graph is built on Verse, an AI can work with a game world through code. That’s different from Blueprints, which are stored as binary files that LLMs can’t read directly.
In addition to these changes, UE6 will make the engine more modular and expose its systems through an application programming interface “so that AI tooling can get at them without needing to fall back to slow computer use,” says Marcus Wassmer, the executive vice president of development at Epic Games. An internal effort that Epic calls “Python All The Things” aims to make everything in the editor scriptable. UE6 will also store more game assets as text so an LLM can edit them directly. For binary assets (non-text files) that remain, Epic is building a command-line tool that allows agents to interact with the files without loading the full UE6 interface.
The traditional Unreal Engine editor isn’t going away, but Vogel expects it will change as LLM use becomes more common. “You’re not going to move stuff visually in the editor. Those times are gone,” he says. Instead, he expects game developers will more frequently use the Unreal Engine editor to visualize “the context needed to make decisions, or make the tweaks.”
Unreal Engine 5.9 offers a glimpse at what that transition will mean in practice. Released as a developer preview on 9 November, it includes a new feature called Unreal Canvas. Vogel says it’s a collaboration feature that takes inspiration from visual workspace software like Miro. “It’s a way to make sense of the project,” says Vogel.
Why Buy an Engine When You Can Build One?
Epic Games’ goal is to keep Unreal Engine relevant in a world where AI coding tools are becoming ever-more capable. The company has the benefit of approaching the problem from a position of strength: Unreal Engine is the second most commonly used engine, after Unity Technologies’ Unity engine, and ranks first if measured by the number of games sold.
Will that be enough?
McGeathy says there can be benefits to creating an engine from scratch. “If you own the engine, you’re not reliant on a third party who could yank it out from underneath you.” However, he remains skeptical that many developers will go that route, even if AI makes it less painful. “Your goal as a game developer is to make a game. It’s not to make a game engine.”
What LLMs do change, he says, is to lower the cost of switching between game engines. McGeathy, an Unreal veteran, used Claude to start building in Godot, an open-source engine he had never touched himself. “AI made transitioning from using Unreal into Godot trivial,” he says. Barker agrees. He used an LLM to build physics test scenes in both Unreal and Godot, which let him evaluate which engine was better suited to handle a game concept.
Wassmer recognizes that AI makes it “easier [for developers using Unreal Engine] to pick up a new standard, or to convert stuff,” and says that Epic won’t attempt to block this shift. On the contrary, UE6 will remove barriers where possible. “Our explicit goal is to be able to interoperate with other ecosystems and other games,” he says.
He feels confident that, even with the aid of LLMs, building an engine from scratch carries a heavy price that most developers won’t want to pay. “You still have to burn a lot of tokens to get something of scale that is optimized and that runs across all platforms,” Wassmer says. “Would you rather spend that time and energy building something from scratch? Or would you rather use something modular, like UE6, and trim out the bits you don’t need?”
- Will the Unreal Engine 5 Realize the Metaverse’s Potential? ›
- AI Coding Assistants Are Getting Worse ›
- Why AI Isn’t Ready to Be a Real Coder ›
Matthew S. Smith is a freelance consumer technology journalist with 17 years of experience and the former Lead Reviews Editor at Digital Trends. An IEEE Spectrum Contributing Editor, he covers consumer tech with a focus on display innovations, artificial intelligence, and augmented reality. A vintage computing enthusiast, Matthew covers retro computers and computer games on his YouTube channel, Computer Gaming Yesterday.
